Backend & API Design Mastery

The decisions that outlive your framework. How to model a resource, choose a status code that means something, page through a moving collection, make a retry safe, expire a cache, evolve a contract without breaking a client, and know — from the outside — what your service is doing at three in the morning.

How to use this course

  • This course is about design, not about a framework. Every example runs on FastAPI because it is small enough to disappear behind the idea being taught, but almost nothing here is FastAPI-specific. The same decisions face you in Express, Spring, Rails, Go's net/http and ASP.NET, and they are decided the same way.
  • It assumes you can write Python and read HTTP. If async def, type hints and pydantic are unfamiliar, work through the Python course first — its ecosystem section builds a framework-free ASGI app by hand, which is exactly the layer this course sits on top of.
  • It assumes you can write SQL. Transactions, indexes, constraints and the N+1 problem are taught in the SQL course; here they appear only where they change an API decision.
  • The authority for HTTP is the specification, and it is readable. RFC 9110 (HTTP Semantics) replaced the old 723x series in June 2022 and is the document that actually defines what PUT means. Where this course makes a claim about HTTP, the section that supports it is named so you can check.
  • Each section is Concept → Mechanism → Practice → Exercises with solutions. The mechanism matters more here than in most subjects, because API design is mostly the discipline of not inventing something HTTP already has.
  • Set yourself up before section 1:
python3 --version                      # 3.13 or newer for the whole course
mkdir ~/api-mastery && cd ~/api-mastery
python3 -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate
pip install "fastapi[standard]" uvicorn httpx pytest pytest-asyncio

Versions every transcript in this course was produced against: FastAPI 0.141.1 · Starlette 1.6.0 · pydantic 2.13.4 · httpx 0.28.1 · uvicorn 0.52.4 · pytest 9.1.1, on CPython 3.14.4.

What you are actually learning

There are three different skills hiding inside "backend development", and confusing them is why so many services are hard to work with.

Protocol fluency. Knowing what HTTP already defines — that PUT is idempotent and POST is not, that 409 and 422 are different claims, that ETag plus If-Match is a complete optimistic-concurrency mechanism. Most "clever" API designs are re-implementations of something in §9, §13 or §15 of RFC 9110.
Interface design. Choosing the resources, the names, the shapes and the boundaries. This is the part that cannot be generated, cannot be checked by a linter, and is the part clients live with for years.
Operational design. Building a service that behaves under retry storms, partial failure, slow dependencies and duplicate deliveries — and that can be diagnosed from the outside. Nothing in this category shows up in a demo, and everything in it shows up in an incident.

The course is ordered that way on purpose: protocol first, because it constrains everything else.

SECTION 1

The Shape of a Backend

A backend is a program that turns requests into effects and answers. That sentence is short enough to be useless, so this section unpacks it into the four things you actually decide when you build one: what the units of your system are, what style of interface exposes them, what the server is allowed to remember between calls, and how the code inside is arranged so that the first three can change without a rewrite. Everything in the remaining fifteen sections is a refinement of a choice made here.

1.1 The Path a Request Takes

Before designing anything it is worth being precise about what happens between a client's fetch and a row in a database, because most API design mistakes are a decision made at the wrong stop along that path.

1. DNS, TCP, TLS. The client resolves a name, opens a connection, negotiates a certificate. Nothing here is yours to design; everything here contributes to your latency budget.
2. Edge. A CDN, load balancer or API gateway terminates TLS and may answer from cache, reject an unauthenticated call, enforce a rate limit, or add a request ID. This tier can serve a response your application never sees — which is the whole point of §10.
3. Routing. Your framework matches method + path to a handler, and rejects what does not match with 404 or 405. The shape of this table is your public interface (§3).
4. Parsing and validation. Bytes become a typed object, or the request dies here with a 400/422. This is the trust boundary (§6): everything downstream is allowed to assume the data is well-formed because this step refused it otherwise.
5. Authentication, then authorization. Who is calling (§8), then whether they may do this to this object (§9). Two questions, two answers, two different failure codes.
6. Application logic. The part you think of as "the code". In a well-designed service it is the smallest tier and the only one that knows the domain.
7. Effects. A database transaction (§12), a call to another service, a message onto a queue. This is where "did it happen?" becomes a question with a genuinely difficult answer.
8. Serialisation. Domain objects become a representation, with a status code, a content type, cache directives and validators attached. Steps 3–7 get all the attention; this step is what the client actually experiences.

Two properties of that path shape almost every later decision.

The path is not atomic. Any step can be the last one. A client that never receives step 8 cannot tell whether step 7 happened — the effect may be committed and the response lost in the network. That single fact is why §11 exists, and why "just retry it" is a design decision rather than an implementation detail.

Steps 2 and 8 are the ones you communicate with. A cache at the edge cannot read your handler; it reads your Cache-Control, ETag and Vary headers. A client's retry logic cannot read your intent; it reads your status code and your Retry-After. Almost everything this course calls "design" is the practice of putting information into steps 3 and 8 that would otherwise be locked inside step 6.

1.2 Resources and Representations

RFC 9110 §3.1 defines a resource as "the target of an HTTP request" — anything that can be named by a URI. §3.2 defines a representation as "information that is intended to reflect a past, current, or desired state of a given resource, in a format that can be readily communicated". Two definitions, and the gap between them is the single most useful idea in HTTP.

A resource is a concept with an identity: customer 4183, the invoices of that customer, the current exchange rate for EUR→GBP. A representation is one particular rendering of that concept at one particular moment, in one particular format. The resource is not the JSON. The JSON is one representation of it, and the same resource can have several — JSON for your SDK, CSV for the finance team's download, an image for a thumbnail endpoint.

GET /customers/4183 HTTP/1.1
Host: api.example.com
Accept: application/json

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/json
Content-Length: 118
ETag: "9f4a1e"
Cache-Control: private, max-age=60

{"id":"4183","name":"Acme Ltd","status":"active","createdAt":"2026-02-11T09:14:00Z"}

Everything above the blank line is about the representation, not about the customer. Content-Type says what format this rendering is in. Content-Length measures this rendering. ETag identifies this rendering, so a later request can ask "still this one?" (§10.4). Cache-Control says how long this rendering may be reused. The customer has none of those properties; the bytes do.

Holding the distinction gives you four things immediately:

  • Format is negotiable, identity is not. /customers/4183 is the customer whether you ask for JSON or CSV. This is why /customers/4183.json and /api/json/customers/4183 are design mistakes: they mint a second identity for a difference that belongs in a header (§2.5).
  • A resource need not be a database row. /reports/quarterly/2026-Q1 is a perfectly good resource computed on demand. Modelling your API as a mirror of your schema is a choice, and usually a bad one, because your schema is the thing you most want the freedom to change.
  • Two resources can share a representation. /customers/4183 and /me may return identical bytes. They are still different resources with different cache keys and different authorization rules.
  • Conditional requests, caching and concurrency all key on the representation. That is why §10 works at all: ETag is a hash of the bytes you sent, not of the entity in your database.

1.3 REST, RPC, GraphQL and gRPC

There are four common interface styles, and the useful question is not "which is best" but "which unit does the client name".

Style The client names Transport Typical shape
REST a resource, plus a standard method HTTP, many URLs GET /orders/91
RPC a procedure, plus arguments HTTP, usually one URL and POST POST /api {"method":"getOrder","id":91}
GraphQL a query over a type graph HTTP, exactly one URL and POST POST /graphql {query: "{ order(id:91){ total } }"}
gRPC a service method, in a .proto HTTP/2, binary protobuf OrderService.GetOrder(GetOrderRequest)

REST is resource-centric. Its leverage is that the verbs are already defined — by HTTP, with agreed semantics about safety, idempotency and cacheability that every intermediary in the world already implements. You get conditional requests, caching, content negotiation and range requests without designing any of them. Its weakness is that not everything is a noun, and clients often need several round trips to assemble a screen.

RPC is procedure-centric. It is the most direct expression of "call this function over there", it handles operations that are genuinely not CRUD without contortion, and it is very easy to generate from an interface definition. Its weakness is that it opts out of HTTP's semantics: with everything behind one POST URL, no cache can help you, no proxy can retry safely, and every cross-cutting concern must be re-implemented in your own envelope. JSON-RPC and most /api?action=... designs are here.

GraphQL moves the shape of the response into the client's hands, which genuinely solves over-fetching and the "one screen, six endpoints" problem. The costs are real and often underestimated: caching becomes your problem rather than HTTP's (a POST to a single URL is uncacheable), authorization must be enforced per field rather than per endpoint, and a sufficiently nested query is a denial-of-service vector unless you implement depth and complexity limits. It is a strong choice for a rich, client-diverse read surface and a weak one for a small machine-to-machine write API.

gRPC is RPC with a strict schema, code generation in every language, binary framing and first-class streaming, over HTTP/2. It is excellent between your own services — low latency, small payloads, a compiler that catches contract drift. It is awkward at the public edge: browsers cannot speak it without a proxy (grpc-web), and debugging it requires tooling rather than curl.

The honest summary: REST at the edge, gRPC between services, GraphQL where a diverse read surface justifies owning the machinery yourself. Plenty of good systems are a mixture, and a REST API with three or four carefully chosen non-CRUD operations (§3.6) is not a failure of purity — it is the normal case.

1.4 The Richardson Maturity Model and HATEOAS

Leonard Richardson's maturity model is a four-level ladder, useful less as a scorecard than as a description of what each rung buys.

Level 0 — one URI, one verb. HTTP is a tunnel. POST /api with the operation in the body. Nothing in the network can help you.
Level 1 — resources. Many URIs, still mostly POST. POST /orders/91/cancel. Now at least things have identity, and logs, metrics and authorization can be per-resource.
Level 2 — HTTP verbs and status codes. GET is safe and cacheable, PUT and DELETE are idempotent, codes carry meaning. Caches, proxies and retrying clients now work correctly without knowing anything about your domain. This is where the overwhelming majority of the value is, and where most good APIs stop.
Level 3 — hypermedia controls. Representations carry links describing what may be done next, so the client discovers transitions rather than hard-coding URLs.

Level 3 is HATEOAS — Hypermedia As The Engine Of Application State. In principle a client starts at one entry URL and follows links, exactly as a person browsing a website does; the server can then relocate resources and change available transitions without breaking anyone.

{
  "id": "91",
  "status": "pending",
  "total": {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"},
  "_links": {
    "self":   {"href": "/orders/91"},
    "cancel": {"href": "/orders/91/cancellation", "method": "PUT"},
    "items":  {"href": "/orders/91/items"}
  }
}

Be clear-eyed about what that buys. The strong claim — clients that need no out-of-band knowledge — has essentially never materialised in practice, because real clients are written against documentation and typed SDKs, not discovered at runtime. The weak claims, though, are genuinely worth having and cost almost nothing:

  • Links replace URL construction. A client that stores _links.items.href cannot be broken by a change to your path structure. A client that concatenates "/orders/" + id + "/items" can.
  • The link set encodes state. Omitting cancel from a shipped order tells the client what is possible now, in one place, rather than duplicating your state machine in every UI.
  • Pagination is hypermedia already. A next link is the one HATEOAS control that essentially every API ships, and it works precisely because the client does not compute it (§7.2).

Two conventions are common: JSON:API (links, strictly specified) and HAL (_links, minimal). Pick one and be consistent; inventing a third is a cost with no benefit. Aim for solid Level 2 with links where they carry state, and treat full Level 3 as a goal for hypermedia-shaped domains rather than a badge to chase.

1.5 Statelessness, and What It Buys

HTTP is a stateless protocol in a specific sense (RFC 9110 §3.3): the server is not required to retain any state between requests, and each request must carry everything needed to understand it. That is a constraint on where state lives, not a claim that your system has none — obviously it has a database.

The rule that matters in practice is: no request may depend on having been routed to the same process as an earlier one. Session data in a module-level dictionary, a multi-step wizard held in process memory, an upload assembled across calls in a local temp file — each of these works perfectly on one machine and fails the moment there are two, intermittently, in a way that looks like a heisenbug.

# ---- stateless: everything the handler needs arrives with the request ----
SESSIONS = {"tok-abc": {"user": "ada", "tenant": "acme"}}   # a shared store, not memory

def handle(headers: dict[str, str]) -> str:
    token = headers.get("authorization", "").removeprefix("Bearer ")
    session = SESSIONS.get(token)
    if session is None:
        return "401 Unauthorized"
    return f"200 OK — {session['user']}@{session['tenant']}"

print(handle({"authorization": "Bearer tok-abc"}))
print(handle({}))
print(handle({"authorization": "Bearer tok-nope"}))

200 OK — ada@acme
401 Unauthorized
401 Unauthorized

The dictionary stands in for Redis or a database. The point is not that state disappeared; it is that the process holds none of it, so any of ten processes can answer, and killing one loses nothing.

Statelessness is what makes four other things possible, and they are the reason it is worth the discipline:

  • Horizontal scaling. Add a process, add capacity. No sticky sessions, no session replication.
  • Trivial failure handling. A crashed process loses in-flight requests only. Clients retry (§11) and land somewhere else.
  • Deploys that are not events. Rolling replacement works because no instance is special.
  • Caching and gateways. An intermediary can answer or reject on its own, because the request is self-describing.

The costs are real and worth naming: every request re-authenticates (which is why token verification must be cheap, §8.6), and shared state moves to a store that is now a dependency with its own latency and failure modes. Statelessness does not remove the hard problem; it moves it somewhere you can manage it.

1.6 Layers, DTOs and Dependency Injection

A service that grows without layering ends up with SQL in its HTTP handlers and HTTP status codes in its business rules, at which point neither can be tested or changed independently. Layered architecture is the standard remedy: four tiers, with the rule that gives layered architecture its value being that dependencies point inwards only.

Transport (HTTP) — routing, parsing, status codes, headers, serialisation. Knows HTTP. Knows nothing about business rules.
Application (use cases) — orchestrates one operation: load, decide, persist, emit. Knows the domain and the ports. Knows nothing about HTTP.
Domain — entities and rules. Pure. Depends on nothing.
Infrastructure — database, queue, HTTP clients, clock. Implements interfaces the application layer declares.

Two mechanisms make the arrangement hold: DTOs at the edges, and dependency injection across them.

A DTO (data transfer object) is a type that exists purely to carry data across a boundary. It is not your domain entity. Keeping them separate is what stops your public JSON from being whatever your ORM happened to expose — a change to a column then does not change your API, and, more importantly, adding a column does not silently publish it (§6.3).

Dependency injection simply means a component receives its collaborators rather than constructing them. In Python this needs no framework: a parameter with a default is dependency injection.

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Protocol

# ---- domain: pure, no imports from anywhere else -------------------------
@dataclass
class Account:
    id: str
    owner: str
    balance_cents: int
    frozen: bool = False

    def withdraw(self, cents: int) -> None:
        if self.frozen:
            raise PermissionError("account is frozen")
        if cents > self.balance_cents:
            raise ValueError("insufficient funds")
        self.balance_cents -= cents

# ---- a port: declared by the application layer, implemented by infra ------
class Accounts(Protocol):
    def get(self, account_id: str) -> Account: ...
    def save(self, account: Account) -> None: ...

# ---- application layer: knows the domain and the port, not HTTP ----------
def withdraw_use_case(accounts: Accounts, account_id: str, cents: int) -> Account:
    account = accounts.get(account_id)
    account.withdraw(cents)
    accounts.save(account)
    return account

# ---- infrastructure: an in-memory implementation, good enough for a test --
class InMemoryAccounts:
    def __init__(self, *rows: Account) -> None:
        self._rows = {a.id: a for a in rows}
    def get(self, account_id: str) -> Account:
        return self._rows[account_id]
    def save(self, account: Account) -> None:
        self._rows[account.id] = account

# ---- transport: the only place that knows about status codes -------------
def http_withdraw(accounts: Accounts, account_id: str, body: dict) -> tuple[int, dict]:
    try:
        account = withdraw_use_case(accounts, account_id, int(body["cents"]))
    except KeyError:
        return 404, {"title": "Account not found"}
    except PermissionError as exc:
        return 403, {"title": str(exc)}
    except ValueError as exc:
        return 409, {"title": str(exc)}
    return 200, {"id": account.id, "balance": account.balance_cents}   # the DTO

repo = InMemoryAccounts(Account("a1", "ada", 5_000),
                        Account("a2", "grace", 900, frozen=True))
print(http_withdraw(repo, "a1", {"cents": 1_200}))
print(http_withdraw(repo, "a1", {"cents": 99_000}))
print(http_withdraw(repo, "a2", {"cents": 10}))
print(http_withdraw(repo, "nope", {"cents": 10}))

(200, {'id': 'a1', 'balance': 3800})
(409, {'title': 'insufficient funds'})
(403, {'title': 'account is frozen'})
(404, {'title': 'Account not found'})

Notice what became possible. withdraw_use_case can be tested with InMemoryAccounts and no database. Account.withdraw can be tested with no I/O at all. The status codes live in exactly one function, so changing 409 to 422 is a one-line change with no risk of missing a call site. And Accounts is a Protocol, so the real Postgres implementation needs no inheritance — it merely needs the two methods.

FastAPI's Depends is this same idea with request-scoped lifecycle and an override hook for tests; the Python course covers its mechanics in the FastAPI part of its ecosystem section. The design principle is independent of it: name what you need as a parameter, and let the caller decide what to pass.

1.7 Backend for Frontend

One API serving a web app, an iOS app and three partner integrations will make all of them slightly unhappy. The web app wants denormalised data for a dashboard; the mobile app wants small payloads and few round trips; the partners want a stable contract that never changes. Optimise for any one and you have degraded the others.

The backend for frontend pattern (BFF) resolves this by giving each client class its own thin API, owned by that client's team, sitting in front of shared downstream services. The BFF's job is aggregation and shaping, not business logic.

Web BFF → one /dashboard call fanning out to orders, billing and notifications, returning exactly the fields the page renders.
Mobile BFF → the same data, trimmed and paginated harder, with image URLs pre-sized for the device.
Public API → resource-shaped, versioned, deprecation-managed, changing far more slowly than either of the above (§14).

The pattern earns its keep when client teams are separate from service teams, because it lets the client team ship a shaping change without negotiating with anyone. It is overhead when one team owns everything, and it becomes actively harmful when business rules leak into it — you then have the same rule in three BFFs, drifting.

An API gateway is the neighbouring idea and is often confused with it. An API gateway is a single shared entry point handling cross-cutting concerns: TLS termination, routing, authentication, rate limiting, request IDs, and sometimes response caching. One gateway serves all clients; a BFF serves one client class. They compose — gateway at the edge, BFFs behind it — and the distinction is worth keeping, because putting client-specific shaping into a shared API gateway is how gateways turn into unmaintainable monoliths.

The practical test for whether something belongs in the gateway: does every client need it, and can it be decided without domain knowledge? Authentication, rate limiting and request IDs pass. "Return the fields the dashboard renders" does not — that is a BFF concern, and putting it in the gateway couples a shared component to one screen's layout.

Exercises

Exercise 1.1 — Resource or representation? For each item, say whether it is a property of the resource or of the representation, and why it matters: (a) the customer's name, (b) Content-Type: text/csv, (c) ETag: "9f4a1e", (d) the URI /customers/4183, (e) Content-Language: fr.

Solution
  • (a) Resource. The name is part of the customer's state. Every representation renders it, in whatever format.
  • (b) Representation. The same customer served as CSV and as JSON is the same resource. This is why format belongs in a header and not in the path (§2.5).
  • (c) Representation. An ETag identifies these bytes. Ask for the CSV rendering and you get a different ETag for the same customer — which is correct, and is exactly why Vary exists (§10.3). It is also why an ETag derived from a database updated_at alone is subtly wrong when you serve more than one format.
  • (d) Resource. The URI is the identity. Changing it creates a different resource, which is why URI-based versioning (§14.5) is a heavier commitment than it looks.
  • (e) Representation. Same customer, French rendering, negotiated via Accept-Language.

The practical test: if I changed this, would the client be looking at a different thing, or the same thing rendered differently?

Exercise 1.2 — Diagnose the maturity level. Classify each interface on the Richardson ladder and name the one concrete capability it is giving up.

POST /api/v1/gateway HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{"op": "fetchInvoice", "params": {"id": 771}}
Solution

Level 0. One URI, one verb, the operation in the body.

The concrete loss is cacheability. A read that is safe and repeatable is invisible as such: no CDN, no browser cache, no proxy and no conditional request can help, because POST to a single URI is uncacheable by definition (RFC 9110 §9.3.3) and there is no per-invoice cache key to work with. Expressed as GET /invoices/771, the same read gains an ETag, a Cache-Control policy, safe automatic retry, and a URL that can appear in a log, a bookmark or a bug report.

Secondary losses: every intermediary sees one endpoint, so per-resource metrics, per-resource authorization and per-resource rate limits all have to be re-invented inside the application.

Exercise 1.3 — Find the state. This handler works in development and fails in production roughly one time in three. Explain precisely why, and fix it without removing the feature.

UPLOADS: dict[str, list[bytes]] = {}

def start_upload(upload_id: str) -> None:
    UPLOADS[upload_id] = []

def append_chunk(upload_id: str, chunk: bytes) -> None:
    UPLOADS[upload_id].append(chunk)

def finish_upload(upload_id: str) -> bytes:
    return b"".join(UPLOADS.pop(upload_id))
Solution

UPLOADS is process-local. In development there is one process, so every chunk lands in the same dictionary. Production runs several workers behind a load balancer, so start_upload may run on worker 1 and append_chunk on worker 2, where the key does not exist — a KeyError, at a rate determined by how many workers there are. "One time in three" is the signature of three workers.

The fix is to move the state out of the process. Chunks go to shared storage keyed by upload ID; the handler becomes stateless.

class ChunkStore:                      # Redis, S3 or a table in production
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._data: dict[str, list[bytes]] = {}
    def create(self, key: str) -> None:
        self._data[key] = []
    def append(self, key: str, part: bytes) -> None:
        if key not in self._data:
            raise KeyError(key)
        self._data[key].append(part)
    def assemble(self, key: str) -> bytes:
        return b"".join(self._data.pop(key))

def append_chunk(store: ChunkStore, upload_id: str, chunk: bytes) -> None:
    store.append(upload_id, chunk)

store = ChunkStore()
store.create("u1")
append_chunk(store, "u1", b"hello ")
append_chunk(store, "u1", b"world")
print(store.assemble("u1"))

b'hello world'

The class is still a dictionary, but it is now a dependency rather than a global, which means it can be replaced with Redis without touching append_chunk, and the test above proves the handler in isolation. For real file uploads the usual production answer avoids the problem entirely: hand the client a presigned URL and let it upload directly to object storage (§4.7).

Exercise 1.4 — Draw the dependency arrows. In §1.6, http_withdraw imports withdraw_use_case, which uses the Accounts protocol, which InMemoryAccounts satisfies. Which single import, if added, would break the architecture — and what would go wrong that a test would not immediately catch?

Solution

Importing anything HTTP-shaped into the domain or application layer — for example having Account.withdraw raise an HTTPException(status_code=409) instead of ValueError.

It would still pass every test, because the tests run in the same process with the framework installed. What breaks is everything that is not an HTTP request: a nightly batch job, a queue worker (§12.5), a CLI backfill, a data migration. Each of those now either depends on a web framework it has no use for, or catches an exception whose type is a lie about where the failure came from. The status code has become a property of the domain rule rather than of the transport, so the answer to "what should a queue consumer do with a 409?" is undefined.

The rule that prevents it: the domain raises domain errors; exactly one layer translates them into status codes. In §1.6 that translation is the try block in http_withdraw, and §5.3 turns it into a table rather than a pile of except clauses.

SECTION 2

HTTP as a Contract

Most of what people call "API design" was designed in 1997 and refined until 2022. Before inventing a mechanism, it is worth knowing what HTTP already guarantees — because a guarantee written in RFC 9110 is implemented by every browser, proxy, CDN, load balancer and HTTP client on earth, and one written in your documentation is implemented by whoever read it. This section is the working subset of that specification: the properties of methods, the meaning of status codes, how format is negotiated, and how a browser decides whether it is even allowed to show your response to a page.

2.1 The Three Properties of a Method

RFC 9110 §9.2 defines exactly three properties of an HTTP method, and every design argument about "should this be a POST or a PUT" is really an argument about one of them. There are only nine registered methods and you will use six; the leverage comes not from how many there are but from the fact that every intermediary on the internet already knows what each HTTP method promises.

Safe (§9.2.1) — the request is read-only in intent. The client is promising nothing; the server is promising that a client which issues this request cannot be held responsible for a change. Prefetchers, crawlers and link-checkers rely on this.
Idempotent (§9.2.2) — issuing the request N times has the same intended effect on the server as issuing it once. Note "intended effect on the server", not "identical response": two DELETEs legitimately return 204 then 404, and the method is still idempotent.
Cacheable (§9.2.3) — a response to this method may be stored and reused. In practice this means GET and HEAD; POST is cacheable only with explicit freshness information and almost no implementation supports it.
Method Safe Idempotent Cacheable Request body
GET ✅ ✅ ✅ no
HEAD ✅ ✅ ✅ no
OPTIONS ✅ ✅ ❌ no
PUT ❌ ✅ ❌ yes
DELETE ❌ ✅ ❌ rarely
POST ❌ ❌ in theory yes
PATCH ❌ ❌ (by default) ❌ yes

Every safe method is also idempotent, and the implication does not run the other way: PUT and DELETE are idempotent without being safe, because they change state. So the three columns are a hierarchy of promises — a safe method promises no change, an idempotent one promises no additional change on repetition, and a cacheable one promises the response may be reused — and each is weaker than the one before it.

The table is not trivia. It is a set of promises other people's software already acts on:

  • A client library, a proxy or a service mesh will automatically retry an idempotent request after a network error, and will not retry a POST. That behaviour is why §11.2 exists: if your POST needs to be retry-safe, you must add the mechanism yourself.
  • A browser will re-issue a safe method freely — on back-navigation, on prefetch, on a link hover. A GET with side effects will be triggered by things you never wrote. The classic version of this bug is GET /users/12/delete, "protected" by being an unguessable URL, discovered by a corporate link-prefetcher.
  • A cache may serve a stored GET response without asking you. If the response was user-specific and you forgot Cache-Control: private, a shared cache can hand one user's data to another (§10.1).

PATCH deserves a note: RFC 5789 defines it as not idempotent, because a patch document may be relative ("increment by 1"). If your patch format is a set of absolute field assignments — which JSON Merge Patch is (§4.4) — then your PATCH is idempotent in effect, but no generic client knows that. Say so in your documentation, and if it matters, support If-Match (§10.6) so the client can make it safe explicitly.

# The properties are about *intent*, and a small state machine makes the
# difference between "idempotent" and "identical response" concrete.
STORE: dict[str, str] = {"a1": "Acme"}

def request(method: str, key: str, body: str | None = None) -> int:
    if method == "PUT":                      # idempotent: same end state every time
        created = key not in STORE
        STORE[key] = body or ""
        return 201 if created else 200
    if method == "DELETE":                   # idempotent: end state is "absent"
        return 204 if STORE.pop(key, None) is not None else 404
    if method == "POST":                     # not idempotent: a new key each time
        new = f"a{len(STORE) + 1}"
        STORE[new] = body or ""
        return 201
    raise ValueError(method)

print("PUT   x2 :", request("PUT", "a2", "Beta"), request("PUT", "a2", "Beta"))
print("state    :", sorted(STORE))
print("DELETE x2:", request("DELETE", "a2"), request("DELETE", "a2"))
print("state    :", sorted(STORE))
print("POST   x2:", request("POST", "", "Gamma"), request("POST", "", "Gamma"))
print("state    :", sorted(STORE))

PUT   x2 : 201 200
state    : ['a1', 'a2']
DELETE x2: 204 404
state    : ['a1']
POST   x2: 201 201
state    : ['a1', 'a2', 'a3']

The two PUTs return different codes and leave identical state — idempotent. The two DELETEs do the same. The two POSTs return the same code and leave different state — not idempotent, and that is precisely the case a retry can corrupt.

2.2 Choosing the Method

GET — retrieve a representation, and the safe method you will use most. No request body: RFC 9110 §9.3.1 says a body has no defined semantics and some intermediaries strip it. This is a genuine problem for complex search queries, and the two accepted answers are to encode the query in the query string, or to POST the query to a /searches resource and GET the result (§3.7).

HEAD — identical to GET but the response has no content. Useful for existence checks and for reading Content-Length or ETag before deciding to download. Frameworks usually derive it automatically; if yours does not, an unimplemented HEAD is a small but real interoperability gap.

POST — "process this representation according to the resource's own semantics". It is the general-purpose method and deliberately the least constrained. Use it to create a member in a collection (the server assigns the identity) and for operations that are genuinely not idempotent. On creation, return 201 Created with a Location header pointing at the new resource.

PUT — "replace the target resource's state with this representation". The client chooses the URI, and the body is the whole new state; anything omitted is removed. Use it when the client can name the resource (a settings document, a slug-keyed record, an upload to a pre-agreed path). PUT on a collection URL, meaning "replace the entire collection", is legal and almost always a mistake.

PATCH — apply a partial modification described by a patch document. Requires you to specify the patch format, which §4.4 covers. A PATCH that takes an arbitrary partial object is common, works, and is technically JSON Merge Patch whether or not you call it that.

DELETE — remove the resource. Return 204 No Content when you have nothing to say, or 200 with a body when you have something useful to return. Deleting an already-absent resource is a design choice: 404 is the more informative answer and 204 is the more idempotent-feeling one; both are defensible, and consistency across your API matters more than which you pick.

OPTIONS — describe the communication options for a resource. In practice you will encounter it almost exclusively as the CORS preflight (§2.7).

A rule that resolves most arguments: if the client knows the URI, PUT; if the server assigns it, POST. And if you find yourself wanting POST /orders/91/cancel, §3.6 explains when that is fine and when it means you have missed a resource.

2.3 Status Codes That Mean Something

RFC 9110 §15 defines five classes. The class is the part every client understands, so getting the class right matters more than picking the perfect member of it.

1xx informational — the request was received, processing continues. You will use 100 Continue rarely and 101 Switching Protocols for WebSocket upgrades (§13.4).
2xx success — understood, accepted, and acted on.
3xx redirection — further action is needed to complete the request.
4xx client error — the request is faulty. Repeating it unchanged will fail again.
5xx server error — the request may have been fine; the server failed. Repeating it later may succeed.

The working set — the codes a well-designed API actually emits:

Code Name Use it when
200 OK A successful GET, or a write that returns the resulting state
201 Created A resource was created. Always include Location
202 Accepted Work was queued, not done. Point at a status resource (§12.4)
204 No Content Success with genuinely nothing to send. No body, ever
206 Partial Content A satisfied Range request
301 / 308 Moved Permanently The resource lives elsewhere for good. 308 preserves the method
302 / 307 Found / Temporary Redirect Temporary relocation. 307 preserves the method
304 Not Modified A conditional GET whose validator still matches (§10.5)
400 Bad Request Malformed syntax — unparseable JSON, a missing required parameter
401 Unauthorized No credentials, or invalid ones. Must include WWW-Authenticate
403 Forbidden Credentials understood, permission refused. Re-authenticating will not help
404 Not Found No such resource — or a deliberate lie to hide one from an unauthorised caller (§9.2)
405 Method Not Allowed Resource exists, method does not apply. Must include Allow
406 Not Acceptable Nothing you can produce matches Accept
409 Conflict The request conflicts with current state — a duplicate key, a version clash
410 Gone Deliberately removed, and you want the client to stop asking
412 Precondition Failed An If-Match/If-Unmodified-Since precondition failed (§10.6)
415 Unsupported Media Type You cannot parse the request's Content-Type
422 Unprocessable Content Syntactically valid, semantically wrong — validation failure
428 Precondition Required You require a conditional request and none was sent
429 Too Many Requests Rate limit exceeded. Include Retry-After (§11.6)
500 Internal Server Error An unhandled fault. Never carries a stack trace (§5.6)
502 / 503 / 504 Bad Gateway / Unavailable / Gateway Timeout An upstream failed, you are down, an upstream timed out

Four distinctions cause more confusion than the rest combined.

400 versus 422. 400 means "I could not parse this". 422 means "I parsed it and it is wrong". {"age": "abc"} where an integer is required is a judgement call — most frameworks including FastAPI report 422, and that is defensible because the JSON parsed. {"age": is unambiguously 400. Consistency matters more than the boundary.

401 versus 403. 401 says who are you? and obliges you to send WWW-Authenticate (RFC 9110 §11.6.1) telling the client how to authenticate. 403 says I know who you are and the answer is no. Sending 401 for a permission failure invites clients into a refresh-token loop that can never succeed.

403 versus 404. If revealing that a resource exists is itself a leak — a private repository, another tenant's invoice — 404 is the correct response to an unauthorised caller. See §9.2.

409 versus 422. 422 is about the representation: this data is invalid on its own terms. 409 is about the state: the data is fine but the world is not in a position to accept it — the username is taken, the order is already shipped, the version you edited is stale.

2.4 Whose Fault Is It, Really?

The 4xx/5xx line is a claim about fault, and clients act on it. A retrying client backs off and tries again on 5xx; on 4xx it gives up, because repeating an identical request is pointless by definition. Monitoring makes the same split: a 4xx rate is usually a client integration problem, a 5xx rate pages someone.

Which makes two mistakes expensive:

Returning 500 for a validation failure — usually by letting an exception escape. Your error rate now includes bad input, your alerts fire on somebody's typo, and clients retry a request that can never succeed, multiplying the load.

Returning 200 with an error inside. {"success": false, "error": "not found"} under a 200 defeats every generic mechanism in the stack: retry logic, circuit breakers (§11.5), dashboards, alerting, and the caller's own if response.ok. If a request failed, the status code must say so.

There is one genuinely awkward case: a partial failure, such as a batch where three of ten items succeeded. 207 Multi-Status (WebDAV) exists but is poorly supported. The pragmatic options are to return 200 with a per-item result array and document loudly that callers must inspect it, or — better — to make the batch atomic so there is only one outcome. Prefer atomicity when you can afford it; ambiguity here is expensive forever.

2.5 Content Negotiation and Media Types

Content negotiation (RFC 9110 §12) lets one resource have several representations and lets the client say which it prefers. The client sends Accept; the server picks; the response says what it picked with Content-Type, and says what it varied on with Vary (§10.3).

GET /reports/2026-Q1 HTTP/1.1
Accept: text/csv;q=1.0, application/json;q=0.8
Accept-Language: en-GB, en;q=0.7

The q values are relative weights from 0 to 1, defaulting to 1. Here the client would rather have CSV but will take JSON. If the server can produce neither, RFC 9110 §15.5.7 allows 406 Not Acceptable — though in practice sending your default representation anyway is often kinder, and is explicitly permitted.

A media type is type/subtype plus optional parameters: application/json, text/csv;charset=utf-8, application/vnd.acme.order+json;version=2. Three rules make them useful rather than decorative:

  • Use the registered type when one exists. application/json, not text/json. application/problem+json for errors (§5.2). text/event-stream for SSE (§13.3).
  • The +json suffix means "this is JSON, and also something more specific". A parser can handle application/vnd.acme.order+json as JSON without knowing what an Acme order is. That suffix convention is what makes vendor types safe to introduce.
  • Parameters are part of the type. ;charset=utf-8 is not decoration; ;version=2 is a versioning strategy (§14.5).

Negotiation applies to the request too. Content-Type on a POST tells the server how to parse the body, and if the server cannot, the answer is 415 Unsupported Media Type — not 400. Accepting a body whose declared type you do not support, on the grounds that it "looks like JSON", is how content-type-confusion vulnerabilities start.

Here is the whole mechanism in a runnable service, negotiated properly:

import asyncio
import httpx
from fastapi import FastAPI, Header, Response

app = FastAPI()
ROWS = [{"region": "EMEA", "revenue": 41_200}, {"region": "APAC", "revenue": 28_950}]

def best(accept: str, offered: list[str]) -> str | None:
    """Pick the offered type with the highest client q-value. Ignores wildcards
    beyond */* for brevity; a real implementation handles type/* as well."""
    ranked: list[tuple[float, str]] = []
    for part in accept.split(","):
        bits = [b.strip() for b in part.split(";")]
        mime = bits[0]
        q = 1.0
        for b in bits[1:]:
            if b.startswith("q="):
                q = float(b[2:])
        for candidate in offered:
            if mime in (candidate, "*/*"):
                ranked.append((q, candidate))
    if not ranked:
        return None
    return max(ranked, key=lambda pair: pair[0])[1]

@app.get("/reports/2026-Q1")
def report(accept: str = Header(default="application/json")) -> Response:
    chosen = best(accept, ["application/json", "text/csv"])
    if chosen is None:
        return Response(
            status_code=406,
            media_type="application/problem+json",           # §5.2
            content='{"title":"No acceptable representation",'
                    '"offered":["application/json","text/csv"]}',
            headers={"Vary": "Accept"},
        )
    if chosen == "text/csv":
        body = "region,revenue\n" + "\n".join(f"{r['region']},{r['revenue']}" for r in ROWS)
    else:
        body = str(ROWS).replace("'", '"')
    return Response(content=body, media_type=chosen, headers={"Vary": "Accept"})

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://test") as client:
        for accept in ("application/json",
                       "text/csv;q=1.0, application/json;q=0.8",
                       "application/xml"):
            r = await client.get("/reports/2026-Q1", headers={"Accept": accept})
            print(f"{accept:<42} -> {r.status_code} {r.headers.get('content-type')}")
            print(f"   vary={r.headers.get('vary')}  body={r.text.splitlines()[:1]}")

asyncio.run(main())

application/json                           -> 200 application/json
   vary=Accept  body=['[{"region": "EMEA", "revenue": 41200}, {"region": "APAC", "revenue": 28950}]']
text/csv;q=1.0, application/json;q=0.8     -> 200 text/csv; charset=utf-8
   vary=Accept  body=['region,revenue']
application/xml                            -> 406 application/problem+json
   vary=Accept  body=['{"title":"No acceptable representation","offered":["application/json","text/csv"]}']

One resource, one URI, three answers. Note that Vary: Accept is set on every response including the 406 — without it, a shared cache that stored the CSV would serve it to a client that asked for JSON.

2.6 The Headers That Carry the Contract

Bodies get the attention; headers carry the parts of the contract that machines act on without reading your documentation. The ones worth deliberate design:

Header Direction Why it matters
Content-Type both How to parse the bytes. Wrong here means wrong everywhere
Accept request What the client can handle (§2.5)
Authorization request Credentials (§8). Never in a query string — query strings land in logs
Location response Where the created resource lives (201), or where to go (3xx)
ETag / Last-Modified response Validators for conditional requests (§10.4)
If-Match / If-None-Match request Preconditions: concurrency control and cache revalidation (§10.5)
Cache-Control both Who may store this, and for how long (§10.1)
Vary response Which request headers this response depends on (§10.3)
Retry-After response On 429 and 503, when to come back (§11.6)
Allow response On 405, which methods the resource does support
WWW-Authenticate response On 401, how to authenticate
Idempotency-Key request De-facto standard for safe POST retries (§11.2)
traceparent request W3C Trace Context, for distributed tracing (§15.3)
Deprecation / Sunset response This endpoint is going away, and when (§14.6)

Two rules about custom headers. First, stop prefixing with X-: RFC 6648 deprecated the convention in 2012, because a header that succeeds has to keep its unofficial-looking name forever. Use a plain name in a namespace you control (Acme-Tenant-Id). Second, anything a cache must key on must appear in Vary — a per-tenant response varying on Acme-Tenant-Id and not saying so is a cross-tenant data leak waiting for a shared cache to appear.

2.7 CORS, From the Server's Side

A browser will happily send a cross-origin request; what it will not do is let the calling page read the response unless the server opts in. CORS is that opt-in, and it exists because the browser attaches the user's cookies to same-site requests. Without it, any page could read your bank's API using your session.

Two shapes of request:

Simple requests — GET, HEAD or POST with a body type of text/plain, application/x-www-form-urlencoded or multipart/form-data, and no unusual headers. These go straight out. The browser checks Access-Control-Allow-Origin on the response and, if it does not match, throws away a response the server already sent and computed.

Preflighted requests — everything else, which includes essentially every JSON API call, because Content-Type: application/json alone triggers it. The browser first sends an OPTIONS request asking permission.

OPTIONS /orders HTTP/1.1
Origin: https://app.example.com
Access-Control-Request-Method: POST
Access-Control-Request-Headers: content-type, authorization

HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.example.com
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: content-type, authorization
Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true
Access-Control-Max-Age: 600
Vary: Origin

Five things to get right, each of which is a real outage or a real vulnerability:

  • Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * and Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true are mutually exclusive. Browsers reject the combination outright. If you need cookies, you must echo a specific origin.
  • Echoing Origin back is not validation. Access-Control-Allow-Origin: <whatever they sent> with credentials enabled is equivalent to allowing every site on the internet to read authenticated responses. Check against an allowlist and echo only on a match.
  • Vary: Origin is mandatory when you echo. Otherwise a cache stores the response for origin A and serves it, headers and all, to origin B.
  • CORS is not authorization. It restricts browsers. curl, a mobile app and a server-side script ignore it entirely. Anything protected only by CORS is not protected.
  • Access-Control-Max-Age is worth setting. Without it every JSON request becomes two round trips. Browsers cap it (Chrome at 7200 seconds, Firefox at 86400), so ten minutes is a safe, effective value.

The corresponding response headers on the actual request matter too: Access-Control-Allow-Origin must be present there as well, and Access-Control-Expose-Headers is required for the page to read any response header beyond the seven CORS-safelisted ones — which is why a client cannot see your ETag or your X-Request-Id until you expose it, a failure that looks like the header is missing.

Exercises

Exercise 2.1 — Pick the code. Give the status code and one required header for each, and say why the obvious alternative is wrong.

  1. A POST /orders succeeds and creates order 91.
  2. A GET /orders/91 from a user who owns no such order — but order 91 exists and belongs to someone else.
  3. A PUT /profile whose body is valid JSON but has "email": "not-an-email".
  4. A DELETE /orders/91 on an order that has already shipped.
  5. A GET /orders/91 with If-None-Match: "9f4a" where the current ETag is still "9f4a".
Solution
  1. 201 Created, with Location: /orders/91. 200 is not wrong but throws away the one piece of information the client most needs; without Location the client has to guess the URI or re-read your documentation.
  2. 404 Not Found (with no distinguishing header). 403 is the honest answer and also a disclosure: it confirms order 91 exists. Where existence is itself private, 404 is the correct lie — see §9.2. Be consistent, or the difference in response time or shape becomes the oracle instead.
  3. 422 Unprocessable Content, with Content-Type: application/problem+json (§5.2). Not 400: the JSON parsed perfectly. Not 409: nothing about the current state is in conflict, the value is simply invalid.
  4. 409 Conflict. The request is well-formed and permitted in principle; the resource's state forbids it. Not 400 (nothing is malformed), not 403 (permission is not the issue — the same user could have deleted it an hour earlier).
  5. 304 Not Modified, with the ETag repeated. No body — a 304 with a body is a protocol error, and it defeats the entire purpose, which was not sending the body.

Exercise 2.2 — The retry that duplicated 40 orders. A mobile client sends POST /orders. The server commits the order and then the connection drops before the response arrives. The client's HTTP library retries automatically. Explain what the library did wrong, what the server did wrong, and give both fixes.

Solution

The library was wrong to retry. POST is not idempotent (RFC 9110 §9.2.2), so a client cannot know whether a lost response means "never happened" or "happened, answer lost". A well-behaved library retries GET, PUT, DELETE and HEAD on a transport error, and never POST unless explicitly told to.

The server was wrong to make that the client's problem. Order creation is exactly the operation for which retries are inevitable — mobile networks drop connections constantly — and an API that offers no retry-safe path is offloading an unsolvable problem onto every client that integrates with it.

Two fixes, and mature APIs ship both:

  • Accept an Idempotency-Key. The client generates a UUID per logical order and sends it; the server records key → response on first use and replays the stored response for any repeat. Stripe, Adyen and PayPal all work this way. §11.2 builds it.
  • Let the client choose the URI where it can. PUT /orders/{client-generated-uuid} is idempotent by construction: the second PUT overwrites with identical content and creates nothing. This is cleaner when the client can mint a stable ID, and it needs no extra machinery.

Note that a natural-key uniqueness constraint in the database is a partial fix. It prevents the duplicate row, but the retry then gets a 409 for an order it actually created — which is a confusing outcome, not a correct one. Idempotency keys return the original 201.

Exercise 2.3 — Fix the CORS configuration. This is real, and it is a vulnerability. Name it, and rewrite it.

@app.middleware("http")
async def cors(request, call_next):
    response = await call_next(request)
    response.headers["Access-Control-Allow-Origin"] = request.headers.get("origin", "*")
    response.headers["Access-Control-Allow-Credentials"] = "true"
    return response
Solution

It reflects any origin and enables credentials. Any page on the internet can therefore make an authenticated request with the user's cookies and read the response — an account takeover primitive, and the classic CORS misconfiguration. It also omits Vary: Origin, so a shared cache can serve one origin's allowance to another, and it never answers the preflight, so it does not actually work for JSON requests.

ALLOWED = {"https://app.example.com", "https://admin.example.com"}

def cors_headers(origin: str | None) -> dict[str, str]:
    headers = {"Vary": "Origin"}
    if origin in ALLOWED:                       # membership, never reflection
        headers |= {
            "Access-Control-Allow-Origin": origin,
            "Access-Control-Allow-Credentials": "true",
            "Access-Control-Allow-Methods": "GET, POST, PATCH, DELETE",
            "Access-Control-Allow-Headers": "content-type, authorization",
            "Access-Control-Expose-Headers": "etag, x-request-id",
            "Access-Control-Max-Age": "600",
        }
    return headers

for origin in ("https://app.example.com", "https://evil.test", None):
    got = cors_headers(origin)
    print(f"{str(origin):<26} allow-origin={got.get('Access-Control-Allow-Origin')!r}")

https://app.example.com    allow-origin='https://app.example.com'
https://evil.test          allow-origin=None
None                       allow-origin=None

Three further points. Use your framework's middleware (CORSMiddleware in Starlette) rather than hand-rolling — it handles the preflight, the Vary header and the null-origin case correctly. Never put "null" in the allowlist; sandboxed iframes and file:// pages send it, so it is attacker-controllable. And remember the last bullet of §2.7: none of this restrains a non-browser client, so the authorization check in §9 is still doing the real work.

Exercise 2.4 — Make PATCH idempotent. Your PATCH /counters/{id} accepts {"increment": 1}. A client retries after a timeout and the counter is now 2. Redesign so a retry is safe, in two different ways, and say when each is appropriate.

Solution

(a) Make the patch absolute rather than relative. {"value": 7} produces the same end state however many times it is applied. This is the general rule that makes JSON Merge Patch idempotent, and it is the right answer whenever the client can know the intended final value.

(b) Guard with a precondition. Keep the relative operation but require If-Match: "<etag>". The first request succeeds and the resource's ETag changes; the retry carries a now-stale validator and gets 412 Precondition Failed, so the increment cannot be applied twice. Respond 428 Precondition Required when the header is absent, so the safety is not optional. §10.6 builds this.

Choose (a) when the client holds the desired state — a settings toggle, a status field, a quantity the user typed. Choose (b) when the operation is genuinely a read-modify-write over a value the client does not control, such as a counter, a balance or a version-numbered document; (b) also gives you lost-update protection between different clients, which (a) does not.

A third option exists for the specific case of counters that must never be lost: model the increment as a resource. PUT /counters/{id}/increments/{uuid} is idempotent by construction, and the counter's value becomes the count of increment resources — at the cost of storing them.

SECTION 3

Resource Modelling and URI Design

This is the part of an API that cannot be regenerated, refactored or hidden behind a facade. Handlers get rewritten, databases get replaced, frameworks get swapped — but the day you publish /customers/{id}/invoices you have made a promise that outlives all of it. This section is about making that promise deliberately: how to find the resources, how to name them, when to nest, when a noun does not exist, and what to do about the operations that genuinely refuse to be nouns.

3.1 Finding the Resources

Resource-oriented design starts from a question that sounds obvious and almost never gets asked: what are the things? Not what does the system do — what does it have.

Three sources of candidates, in decreasing order of reliability:

  • What the business names. If people in the company say "chargeback", "shipment", "seat", those are resources. Domain vocabulary is worth far more than schema vocabulary, because it is stable: the table might get split, but the concept of a shipment does not.
  • What the client needs to address individually. Anything a user can link to, bookmark, refresh or permission separately wants an identity.
  • What has a lifecycle. If something is created, changes state, and ends, it is a resource — including things you may think of as events. A "password reset" has a lifecycle: requested, sent, used or expired.

Three tempting sources that mislead:

  • Your tables. A join table is not automatically a resource; a resource may span five tables. Mirroring the schema publishes your refactoring constraints to every client you have.
  • Your screens. A dashboard is not a resource; it is a view, and if you want to serve it in one call, that is a BFF concern (§1.7) or a deliberately-designed report resource, not a reason to reshape the domain.
  • Your functions. calculateShippingEstimate is a function. The resource is the shipping estimate (§3.7).

The test that settles most cases: can I imagine GET-ting it, and would the result be meaningful on its own? GET /shipments/88 — yes. GET /calculateShipping — no, because there is nothing there until you supply inputs, which is the signal that the inputs constitute the resource.

3.2 URI Structure and Naming

Good URI design starts from a grammar rather than from taste. A resource name is the URI path that identifies a resource, and Google's AIP-122 states the rule that most conventions converge on: a resource name is an alternating sequence of collection identifiers and resource identifiers. Everything else in URI design is a convention layered on top of that structure.

/publishers
collection
/oreilly
resource id
/books
collection
/9781098136703
resource id

Which gives a small, unambiguous grammar: even segments name collections, odd segments name members, and any prefix of a valid resource name is itself a valid resource name — /publishers/oreilly and /publishers both mean something.

The conventions worth adopting, and the reasoning behind each:

Convention Do Not Because
Nouns for resources /orders /getOrders The method is the verb (§2.2)
Plural collections /orders/91 /order/91 The collection is plural; consistency beats grammar debates
Lowercase, hyphenated /purchase-orders /purchaseOrders Hosts are case-insensitive, paths are not — mixed case invites 404s
IDs opaque to the client /orders/91 /orders/91?type=web The identifier identifies; qualifiers are not identity
Query string for filtering /orders?status=open /orders/open open is not a member of orders
No format in the path /reports/7 + Accept /reports/7.csv Format is a representation concern (§1.2)
No verbs in paths, except §3.7 /orders/91/cancellation /orders/91/doCancel Verbs multiply without limit; nouns do not
No trailing slash, consistently /orders /orders/ Two URIs for one resource splits caches and breaks relative links

A few more that are less often stated:

Do not encode hierarchy you might want to change. /regions/emea/customers/4183 looks tidy until a customer moves region and every stored URL breaks. Nest only where the parent is genuinely part of the child's identity and cannot change.

Do not put secrets or PII in a path. URIs appear in access logs, browser history, Referer headers and error trackers. /users/ada@example.com leaks an email address into a dozen systems that were never meant to hold one.

Prefer opaque IDs over sequential ones in public APIs. /orders/91 tells any customer roughly how many orders you have taken and invites enumeration (§9.2). UUIDs or prefixed random strings (ord_9f4a1e...) cost nothing and remove a class of problem — the prefix is worth adding, because it makes a misused identifier obvious at a glance.

Decide about versioning now, not later. Whether the version lives at /v1/orders, in a header, or in a media type is §14.5's argument; the point here is that retrofitting it is far more painful than including it.

3.3 Collections and Members

A collection is a resource whose representation is a list of members. The two-URI pattern is the backbone of almost every API:

URI GET POST PUT PATCH DELETE
/orders (collection) list, paginated create a member → 201 + Location replace the whole collection — legal, almost never right — delete everything — almost never right
/orders/91 (member) one representation — replace this member's state modify part of it remove it

Three details that separate a collection that works from one that does not:

A collection response is never a bare array. [{...}, {...}] has nowhere to put the pagination cursor, the total, or the links, so the first time you need any of those you break every client. Return an object with the array inside it: {"items": [...], "nextPageToken": "..."}. There was once a security argument for this too — JSON hijacking via array constructor overriding — long since fixed in browsers, but the extensibility argument alone settles it.

A collection is always paginated. Not "paginated when it gets big": the first version, on day one, with a default limit. A collection that returns everything works fine in development, ships, and then someone creates the ten-thousandth order. §7 covers how.

A collection's members and the collection itself have different authorization. Listing orders and reading one order are different permissions, and — crucially — a list endpoint must filter by what the caller may see, not check permission on a whole collection. That is the mistake §9.2 is about.

3.4 Sub-Resources, and When to Nest

A sub-resource is a resource whose identity includes its parent: /customers/4183/invoices. Nesting communicates ownership and scopes the collection automatically, which is genuinely useful. It also creates a commitment, so the rule is:

Nest when the child cannot exist without the parent and is never addressed without it. Otherwise, use a top-level collection and a filter.

Nest. /orders/91/items/3 — a line item has no meaning outside its order, is never listed across orders, and its ID need only be unique within the order.
Do not nest. /customers/4183/orders/91 as the only way to reach order 91 — support agents search orders globally, orders get reassigned, and now every client needs the customer ID to fetch an order it already has the ID of.

The productive compromise, and what most mature APIs do:

  • /orders/91 is the canonical URI. One identity, one cache entry, one place in the documentation.
  • /customers/4183/orders exists as a scoped collection — a convenience for listing, which returns members whose self links point at /orders/91.

That gives you the ergonomics of nesting without two identities for one thing. Never let both /orders/91 and /customers/4183/orders/91 return a full representation; pick one, and if you must keep the other, 301 it to the canonical one.

Depth is a smell past two levels. /a/1/b/2/c/3 means a client cannot fetch a c without knowing an a, and your route table is now combinatorial. If you find yourself there, the c almost certainly deserves a top-level collection.

Many-to-many needs a name. The relationship between a user and a project is not "the user's project"; it is a membership, with its own attributes (role, joined date) and its own lifecycle. /projects/12/members/ada is a real resource that can be PUT, PATCHed and DELETEd — and modelling it as one is what gives you a place to put role.

3.5 Singleton Resources

A singleton resource is one that exists exactly once within its parent scope and therefore has no identifier of its own (AIP-156). They are underused, and they are the right answer more often than people expect.

/me                          the authenticated principal
/settings                    this application's settings
/users/ada/preferences       exactly one preferences document per user
/orders/91/shipping-address  an order has one, always

The signature of a singleton is that GET needs no ID and POST makes no sense — there is nothing to add to. The methods that apply are GET, PUT and PATCH, and sometimes DELETE if "absent" is a meaningful state.

Two things singletons buy you. First, they remove a pointless indirection: GET /users/ada/preferences rather than GET /preferences?user=ada and then extracting the single element of a list. Second, /me in particular decouples the client from identity — the client does not need to know its own user ID to fetch its own profile, which means a token rotation or an identity migration does not break it.

The trap is the singleton that turns out to be plural. An order has one shipping address until international orders need two, and a user has one API key until they want to rotate without downtime. Ask is one an intrinsic property of the concept, or is one just how many we have today? When it is the second, model the collection from the start — moving /users/ada/api-key to /users/ada/api-keys/{id} is a breaking change (§14.4).

3.6 The Standard Methods

Across a well-designed API, almost every operation is one of five standard methods. Google's AIP-130 names them Get, List, Create, Update and Delete; the value of the vocabulary is that once a client has learned them for one resource, it has learned them for all of them.

Standard method HTTP On Returns
Get GET member the resource
List GET collection a page of members (§7)
Create POST collection 201 + Location + the created resource
Update PATCH (or PUT) member the updated resource
Delete DELETE member 204, or the resource, or a long-running operation (§12.4)

Two design decisions worth making once, globally, and then never revisiting per-endpoint:

Do writes return the resource? Returning it saves the client a round trip and lets it see server-computed fields — timestamps, derived totals, the new ETag. Returning 204 keeps responses small and avoids the awkward question of which representation to return when the caller's Accept was for something else. Returning the resource is the more common and generally kinder choice; the important thing is that it is the same choice everywhere.

PUT or PATCH for update? PUT is idempotent and unambiguous but demands the client send the whole object — and a client that read the object, changed one field and PUT it back will silently revert any field added by a newer version of the API. PATCH avoids that and is what most APIs offer. Offering both is fine; offering only PUT for a resource with server-managed fields is a lost-update generator.

3.7 When the Operation Is Not a Noun

Some operations refuse to be CRUD. Cancelling an order, sending an email, rebooting a machine, retrying a payment. There are three answers, and they are ranked.

First: look for the missing resource. This works far more often than it seems, and it is worth genuinely trying before giving up.

The verb The resource hiding inside it
cancel an order a cancellation: PUT /orders/91/cancellation
publish a document the published state: PUT /documents/7/publication
search a search: POST /searches → GET /searches/{id}
send a password reset a reset request: POST /password-resets
reboot a machine a reboot: POST /machines/vm-3/reboots

The payoff is not aesthetic. A cancellation resource can be GET — when was it cancelled, by whom, why — and that information had nowhere to live in POST /orders/91/cancel. A reboot resource gives you a history and something to point a 202 at (§12.4). A search resource gives you a cacheable, shareable, paginable result set, which is the standard answer to "my search query is too big for a query string".

Second: a custom method, named consistently. When there is genuinely no resource — the operation is an imperative with no state of its own — use a custom method and mark it clearly as one. The two established conventions are a colon (POST /orders/91:cancel, AIP-136) or a trailing verb segment (POST /orders/91/cancel). Both are fine. The colon has the advantage of being visually unmistakable: nobody will confuse :cancel for a sub-resource. Whichever you choose, apply it everywhere, and keep the verb list small — a resource with eleven custom methods is a resource that was never modelled.

Third: accept that some things are RPC, and be honest about it. A /rpc namespace with a documented set of operations is better than pretending an operation is a resource by giving it a contrived noun. POST /rpc/recalculate-all-prices is clear. PUT /price-recalculations/global is a fiction that will confuse everyone who reads it.

Here is the difference made concrete — the same operation, modelled both ways:

import asyncio
import httpx
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException, Response

app = FastAPI()
ORDERS = {"91": {"id": "91", "status": "pending", "cancellation": None}}

# --- the custom-method form: an imperative, and nothing to GET afterwards ---
@app.post("/orders/{oid}:cancel")
def cancel_rpc(oid: str) -> dict:
    order = ORDERS.get(oid)
    if order is None:
        raise HTTPException(404)
    if order["status"] == "cancelled":
        raise HTTPException(409, "already cancelled")
    order["status"] = "cancelled"
    return {"status": "cancelled"}

# --- the resource form: the cancellation is a thing, with its own state ------
@app.put("/orders/{oid}/cancellation", status_code=200)
def put_cancellation(oid: str, body: dict, response: Response) -> dict:
    order = ORDERS.get(oid)
    if order is None:
        raise HTTPException(404)
    if order["cancellation"] is None:                    # first time: created
        order["cancellation"] = {"reason": body["reason"], "at": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"}
        order["status"] = "cancelled"
        response.status_code = 201
        response.headers["Location"] = f"/orders/{oid}/cancellation"
    return order["cancellation"]                          # idempotent on repeat

@app.get("/orders/{oid}/cancellation")
def get_cancellation(oid: str) -> dict:
    order = ORDERS.get(oid)
    if order is None or order["cancellation"] is None:
        raise HTTPException(404)
    return order["cancellation"]

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://test") as client:
        r = await client.put("/orders/91/cancellation", json={"reason": "duplicate"})
        print("first  PUT :", r.status_code, r.headers.get("location"), r.json())
        r = await client.put("/orders/91/cancellation", json={"reason": "duplicate"})
        print("second PUT :", r.status_code, r.json())
        r = await client.get("/orders/91/cancellation")
        print("GET        :", r.status_code, r.json())
        r = await client.post("/orders/91:cancel")
        print("RPC repeat :", r.status_code, r.json())

asyncio.run(main())

first  PUT : 201 /orders/91/cancellation {'reason': 'duplicate', 'at': '2026-03-04T10:02:00Z'}
second PUT : 200 {'reason': 'duplicate', 'at': '2026-03-04T10:02:00Z'}
GET        : 200 {'reason': 'duplicate', 'at': '2026-03-04T10:02:00Z'}
RPC repeat : 409 {'detail': 'already cancelled'}

Read the last two lines together. The resource form is idempotent for free — a retried PUT returns 200 and the same body, so a dropped response is harmless. The custom-method form has to answer 409 on the retry, which the client cannot distinguish from "someone else cancelled it", and which is exactly the ambiguity §11.2 has to solve with extra machinery. Modelling the noun did the work that an idempotency key would otherwise have to do.

Exercises

Exercise 3.1 — Redesign the URI set. A team ships this. Rewrite it, and for each change say what capability the original gave up.

POST /api/getUserOrders
GET  /api/order.php?id=91&format=json
POST /api/order/91/setStatus
GET  /api/orders/open
POST /api/deleteOrder
Solution 
GET    /orders?customer=4183          (or GET /customers/4183/orders)
GET    /orders/91                     with Accept: application/json
PUT    /orders/91/status              or PATCH /orders/91
GET    /orders?status=open
DELETE /orders/91

What each original gave up:

  1. POST /api/getUserOrders — cacheability and safety. A read expressed as POST cannot be cached, cannot be safely retried, and cannot be linked to. It also hides the customer ID in a body, so per-customer rate limiting and logging have to parse the body to work.
  2. order.php?id=91&format=json — identity. Three things are wrong: the implementation technology is in the URI (so changing language is a breaking change), the identifier is in the query string rather than the path (so /orders/91 cannot be a cache key or a link), and format is in the URI rather than negotiated (§2.5), which mints a separate identity per format.
  3. POST .../setStatus — idempotency. A status change is a state assignment; PUT /orders/91/status is naturally repeatable, and POST setStatus is not.
  4. /api/orders/open — composability. open reads as a member of orders, colliding with any order whose ID is the string open, and there is no way to combine it with a second filter. A query parameter composes: ?status=open&currency=GBP.
  5. POST /api/deleteOrder — the entire method vocabulary. The ID is not even in the URI, so there is nothing to authorize, log, cache-invalidate or rate-limit per resource.

Also dropped: the /api prefix, which is usually redundant when the API has its own hostname, and the .php.

Exercise 3.2 — Nest or not? For each pair, decide whether the child should be nested, top-level, or both, and justify it in one sentence.

  1. A comment on a blog post.
  2. An employee in a department.
  3. A message in a conversation.
  4. A file in a folder.
  5. A payment against an invoice.
Solution
  1. Comment on a post — both. /posts/7/comments for listing (that is how they are always read) with canonical /comments/{id}, because a comment is permalinked, reported and moderated on its own.
  2. Employee in a department — top-level with a filter. /employees?department=eng. An employee outlives, and can move between, departments; nesting would make a transfer a change of identity.
  3. Message in a conversation — nested, and only nested. /conversations/12/messages/88. A message has no existence outside its conversation, is never listed across conversations without the conversation as context, and its ordering is conversation-scoped.
  4. File in a folder — top-level with an ID, and a separate path abstraction. This is the case that looks nested and must not be: files move, folders get renamed, and a URI containing the path breaks on both. /files/{id} with a path attribute, plus /folders/{id}/children for browsing.
  5. Payment against an invoice — both, canonical top-level. /invoices/44/payments reads naturally, but a payment appears in reconciliation, in refunds and in the ledger, so /payments/{id} is canonical. This is also the many-to-many warning in §3.4: one payment can settle two invoices, at which point nesting was never true in the first place.

The recurring test: can this child move to a different parent? If yes, do not put the parent in its identity.

Exercise 3.3 — Find the noun. Model each as a resource rather than a verb, and name one capability the resource form gains.

  1. POST /users/12/sendVerificationEmail
  2. POST /reports/generate (takes 40 seconds)
  3. POST /carts/9/applyCoupon
  4. POST /vms/3/reboot
Solution
  1. POST /users/12/verification-emails → a verification email is a thing that was sent at a time, to an address, with a token that expires. Gains: GET the collection to see how many were sent (rate limiting and support both need this), and a 409 when one was sent 30 seconds ago becomes meaningful rather than arbitrary.
  2. POST /reports → 202 Accepted + Location: /reports/f81c → the report is the resource; the request creates it in a pending state. Gains: the client can poll, resume after a crash, share the URL, and re-download the result later without regenerating it. §12.4 is this pattern in full.
  3. PUT /carts/9/coupon (a singleton, since a cart takes one) → gains idempotency: applying the same coupon twice is 200 with the same body rather than a double discount or a confusing 409. It also gains DELETE /carts/9/coupon for free, which the verb form would have needed a second endpoint for.
  4. POST /vms/3/reboots → a reboot has a start time, a requester, a duration and an outcome. Gains: an audit trail, a status resource for a long-running operation, and a natural answer to "is it already rebooting?" — GET /vms/3/reboots?status=in-progress — instead of a boolean field bolted onto the VM.

The pattern in all four: the verb form has nowhere to put the metadata, so it ends up in logs, in a separate table nobody exposes, or nowhere at all.

Exercise 3.4 — The singleton that grew. You ship GET/PUT /users/{id}/api-key. A year later customers need two keys so they can rotate without downtime. Describe the migration, and say what you should have done.

Solution

The migration. /users/{id}/api-key cannot simply become a collection — existing clients GET it and expect one object. The workable path:

  1. Add /users/{id}/api-keys as a new collection, returning all keys. New clients use it.
  2. Keep /users/{id}/api-key working, defined precisely as "the oldest active key", and document that definition. It is a lie you can maintain.
