  3. Mark the singleton deprecated with Deprecation and Sunset headers (§14.6), and publish the date.
  4. Instrument it. You cannot remove it until the traffic is gone, and you cannot know that without per-endpoint, per-client metrics (§15.1) — which is the practical reason §14.7 insists on knowing what you have shipped.
  5. Remove it after the sunset date, answering 410 Gone rather than 404, so the failure is unambiguous.

That is roughly a two-release, six-month job for a change that is one line of routing.

What you should have done. Ask the §3.5 question at design time: is exactly one an intrinsic property of an API key, or is one just how many we support today? Rotation is a requirement of every credential system that has ever existed, so the answer was always "one for now". Ship /users/{id}/api-keys as a collection from day one — a collection with one member costs nothing, and a singleton that becomes plural costs a deprecation cycle.

The general form is worth remembering: going from many to one is easy, going from one to many is a breaking change. When in doubt, model the collection.

SECTION 4

Request and Response Bodies

The body is the part of your API that developers read, and the part that is hardest to change once it is public. A field name is a promise; a nullable field is a promise about a whole category of values; a timestamp format is a promise about every client's parser. This section is about making those promises well: naming, the three ways a value can be missing, the four data types that are always got wrong, how partial updates actually work, and how to keep a large upload out of your request path entirely.

4.1 Field Naming and Payload Shape

Field naming is the single most visible design decision in an API, and the rules are short.

Pick one case convention and never deviate. camelCase is the JavaScript-native choice and dominates public web APIs; snake_case is more common in Python- and Ruby-authored APIs and in Google's own guidance (AIP-140). Neither is better. What is worse than either is a payload containing createdAt next to updated_at, which is what happens when field names leak from three different subsystems.

Names are for readers, not for storage. orderStatusCd, dt_created, flgActive and usr_id are database column names that escaped. Expand them: status, createdAt, active, userId.

Do not repeat the resource in its own fields. Inside /orders/91, a field called orderId is redundant; it is id. Inside a nested object it may be orderId, because there the context is gone.

Booleans are positive and unprefixed. active, not isActive, and never notDisabled — a negative boolean forces every reader to do a double negation. AIP-140 makes the same call.

Enumerated values are strings, and they are lowercase and stable. "status": "awaiting_payment" rather than "status": 3. Numeric enums save nothing and cost you every log line's readability; worse, the mapping lives in a document nobody has. Never renumber, and never rename a value — treat the string as part of the contract (§14.4).

Arrays hold one type. A field that is sometimes a string and sometimes an object is unfit for a typed client, and every generated SDK will render it as any.

Consistent shapes beat compact ones. If money is {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"} in one place, it is that everywhere — not a bare number in the response and an object in the request.

4.2 Null, Absent and Empty

JSON offers three ways for a value not to be there, and they mean three different things. Deciding what each means, once, prevents an enormous amount of client confusion.

Form Conventional meaning
key absent "no information" — not applicable, not loaded, or not permitted to see
"field": null "known to have no value" — explicitly cleared
"field": "" / [] / {} "present, and empty" — an empty string is a value; an empty list is a real answer

The distinction becomes load-bearing in PATCH (§4.4), where null must mean "clear this" and absent must mean "leave it alone". If your parser cannot tell them apart, you cannot express "clear this field" at all — which is why pydantic's model_fields_set and the exclude_unset=True serialiser option exist.

Three practical rules:

  • In responses, prefer consistency to compactness. Always emitting "cancelledAt": null is kinder than sometimes omitting the key, because clients can write order.cancelledAt without a guard. The exception is genuinely optional sub-objects, where absence is meaningful.
  • Never use null in an array. ["a", null, "c"] forces a null check into every consumer's loop for no gain.
  • Do not overload empty string as null. "email": "" and "email": null should not both be reachable; pick one to mean "no email" and reject the other at validation (§6).
from pydantic import BaseModel

class ProfilePatch(BaseModel):
    nickname: str | None = None
    bio: str | None = None

body_a = ProfilePatch.model_validate({"nickname": None})   # explicit clear
body_b = ProfilePatch.model_validate({})                   # says nothing

print("A set fields:", body_a.model_fields_set, "->", body_a.model_dump(exclude_unset=True))
print("B set fields:", body_b.model_fields_set, "->", body_b.model_dump(exclude_unset=True))
print("A dumped naively:", body_a.model_dump())
print("B dumped naively:", body_b.model_dump())

A set fields: {'nickname'} -> {'nickname': None}
B set fields: set() -> {}
A dumped naively: {'nickname': None, 'bio': None}
B dumped naively: {'nickname': None, 'bio': None}

The last two lines are the bug this rule exists to prevent. Dumped naively, "clear the nickname" and "change nothing" are identical — and a handler that does record.update(body.model_dump()) will wipe every field the client did not mention. exclude_unset=True is what preserves the distinction, and it is one keyword argument between a working PATCH and a data-loss incident.

4.3 Times, Money, Identifiers and Numbers

Four data types account for most cross-language interoperability bugs.

Timestamps: RFC 3339, UTC, always. "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z". Not epoch seconds (unreadable in logs, ambiguous about milliseconds), not "04/03/2026" (which is either March 4th or April 3rd depending on the reader's continent), not a local time without an offset. If a local time genuinely matters — a calendar appointment must stay at 09:00 when the user changes timezone — send the wall-clock time and the IANA zone name as separate fields ("2026-03-04T09:00:00" plus "Europe/London"), because an instant alone cannot express that intent.

Durations: seconds as an integer, with the unit in the name. "timeoutSeconds": 30. Never a bare "timeout": 30, which is 30 of something.

Money: an integer of minor units, plus a currency code. {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"} is £42.50. Never a floating-point number: 0.1 + 0.2 is not 0.3 in IEEE 754, and a JSON 4250.00 may arrive in JavaScript as a Number that cannot represent large values exactly. Never a bare number without a currency — a total is meaningless without one, and "we only support GBP" is a statement with a short shelf life. If you must send a decimal, send it as a string ("42.50"), which is what several payment APIs do precisely to avoid the float.

Identifiers: strings, always. Even when they are numeric today. JavaScript's Number loses precision above 2^53 − 1, so a 64-bit ID silently corrupts in the browser; string IDs also let you migrate from sequential integers to UUIDs without a breaking change.

import json

a, b = 9007199254740992, 9007199254740993        # 2**53 and 2**53 + 1
print("on the wire:", json.dumps({"id": b, "idString": str(b)}))
# What a JavaScript client holds after JSON.parse — a float64, emulated exactly:
print("two distinct ids, one JS number:", float(a) == float(b))
print("id", b, "arrives as", int(float(b)))
print("as a string it survives:", str(b))

on the wire: {"id": 9007199254740993, "idString": "9007199254740993"}
two distinct ids, one JS number: True
id 9007199254740993 arrives as 9007199254740992
as a string it survives: 9007199254740993

The True says that two different identifiers collapse to the same value once parsed as a JavaScript number, and the line after it shows the client silently holding the wrong ID — no exception, no warning, just a request that will now fetch someone else's record. This is not hypothetical: Twitter hit it in 2010 and added an id_str field to every object in its API, which is why that field exists to this day.

Percentages and rates: be explicit. 0.15 or 15? Put it in the name — discountPercent: 15 or discountRate: 0.15.

4.4 Partial Update: PUT, PATCH and the Patch Formats

A partial update is the everyday case — the client wants to change one field — and there are three ways to express it, with genuinely different semantics.

PUT with the full representation. Simple, idempotent, and lossy in a specific way: any field the client did not know about is now absent, and "absent" means "set to default". A client written against v1 that reads, edits and re-PUTs an object will wipe the field v2 added. This is the read-modify-write hazard, and it is why full PUT is safest for documents the client wholly owns — a settings blob, a configuration file — and riskiest for records with server-managed fields.

PATCH with JSON Merge Patch (RFC 7386, application/merge-patch+json). The patch is an object that looks like the resource; present keys are assigned, null deletes, absent keys are untouched. It is intuitive, it is what most APIs mean when they say "send me a partial object", and its one real limitation is that it cannot patch inside an array — an array in a merge patch replaces the whole array. It also cannot set a field to null, since null is spoken for.

PATCH with JSON Patch (RFC 6902, application/json-patch+json). A list of operations: add, remove, replace, move, copy, test. Precise, array-aware, and able to express an atomic compare-and-set via test. The cost is that it is much harder to read and write, and the patch no longer resembles the resource.

[
  {"op": "test",    "path": "/version", "value": 4},
  {"op": "replace", "path": "/status",  "value": "shipped"},
  {"op": "add",     "path": "/tags/-",  "value": "express"}
]

Merge Patch is a small enough specification to implement from the RFC, and doing so makes its rules unambiguous:

def merge_patch(target, patch):
    """RFC 7386 §2, verbatim: a non-object patch replaces the target outright;
    otherwise null removes a key and anything else is merged recursively."""
    if not isinstance(patch, dict):
        return patch
    if not isinstance(target, dict):
        target = {}
    result = dict(target)
    for key, value in patch.items():
        if value is None:
            result.pop(key, None)
        else:
            result[key] = merge_patch(result.get(key), value)
    return result

order = {"id": "91", "status": "pending", "note": "gift wrap",
         "address": {"city": "Leeds", "postcode": "LS1 4DY"},
         "tags": ["retail", "priority"]}

print(merge_patch(order, {"status": "shipped"}))
print(merge_patch(order, {"note": None}))
print(merge_patch(order, {"address": {"city": "York"}}))
print(merge_patch(order, {"tags": ["retail"]}))

{'id': '91', 'status': 'shipped', 'note': 'gift wrap', 'address': {'city': 'Leeds', 'postcode': 'LS1 4DY'}, 'tags': ['retail', 'priority']}
{'id': '91', 'status': 'pending', 'address': {'city': 'Leeds', 'postcode': 'LS1 4DY'}, 'tags': ['retail', 'priority']}
{'id': '91', 'status': 'pending', 'note': 'gift wrap', 'address': {'city': 'York', 'postcode': 'LS1 4DY'}, 'tags': ['retail', 'priority']}
{'id': '91', 'status': 'pending', 'note': 'gift wrap', 'address': {'city': 'Leeds', 'postcode': 'LS1 4DY'}, 'tags': ['retail']}

Read line three and line four together, because they are the whole lesson. The nested object merged: postcode survived a patch that mentioned only city. The array did not: ["retail"] replaced both tags rather than removing one. That asymmetry is specified behaviour, not a bug, and it is the reason APIs with meaningful list membership either expose the list as a sub-collection (DELETE /orders/91/tags/priority) or accept JSON Patch.

Which to offer? Merge Patch for almost everything, because clients find it obvious. JSON Patch additionally when your resources contain arrays that clients need to edit surgically, or when you want test as a concurrency primitive. Whichever you accept, state it in Content-Type and reject the other with 415 — silently treating a JSON Patch array as a merge patch will replace your entire resource with a list.

4.5 Field Masks and Sparse Responses

Sometimes the client wants less than you send — a mobile list view needs three fields out of forty. A field mask (AIP-157) or sparse fieldset (the JSON:API name) lets the client say so.

GET /orders/91?fields=id,status,total
GET /orders?fields=items(id,status),nextPageToken

It is worth doing when payload size genuinely matters, and it has three costs worth knowing before you commit:

  • Cache fragmentation. Every distinct field list is a distinct cache entry. ?fields=id,status and ?fields=status,id are different URLs for identical bytes unless you canonicalise the parameter — sort it before it reaches the cache key.
  • Every downstream layer has to honour it, or you have saved bandwidth and no work. A mask that still triggers the expensive join has bought very little.
  • It interacts with authorization. A mask must never be able to request a field the caller may not see; masking is applied after the authorization filter, never instead of it (§9.3).

Field masks are also useful on the request side of an update, which is Google's primary use: PATCH with an explicit updateMask states which fields the patch is authoritative about, resolving the null-versus-absent ambiguity of §4.2 without relying on the parser's ability to distinguish them. That is more machinery than most APIs need — merge patch plus exclude_unset handles the same job — but it is why updateMask appears throughout Google's APIs.

The related pattern, expansion, runs the other way: ?expand=customer inlines a related resource to save a round trip. Both are conveniences; neither should be required to use the API productively, and an API that requires a field list to return anything useful has simply moved its design problem onto its clients.

4.6 The Top-Level Shape

Two questions about the outermost object, and both have defensible answers as long as you answer them once.

Do you wrap single resources? {"data": {...}} versus {...} at the top level. Wrapping gives you somewhere to put metadata later without a breaking change, and it makes success and error responses structurally similar. Not wrapping is less noise and reads better in documentation. The pressure that decides it is usually collections, which — as §3.3 argued — must be objects anyway, so many APIs wrap collections and not members. That is fine; just be consistent within each kind.

What goes alongside the data? Pagination cursors (§7.2), a total when you can afford one, and links. Not the status code (it is in the status line), not a "success": true flag (§2.4), and not a "message" field that clients might be tempted to display.

{
  "items": [
    {"id": "91", "status": "pending", "total": {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"}}
  ],
  "nextPageToken": "eyJhZnRlciI6IjkxIn0",
  "links": {"self": "/orders?limit=1", "next": "/orders?limit=1&pageToken=eyJhZnRlciI6IjkxIn0"}
}

One rule with no exceptions: errors do not share the success shape. A client should not have to inspect a field to know whether a request worked; that is the status code's job (§2.4), and the error body has its own media type (§5.2).

4.7 Uploads, and Keeping Bytes Out of Your Request Path

A 200 MB video going through your application server is a bad use of it: the request occupies a worker for minutes, the bytes are buffered somewhere, your timeouts and body-size limits have to accommodate the worst case, and a retry starts from zero.

The standard answer is the presigned URL: the client asks your API for permission to upload, your API returns a short-lived, cryptographically signed URL scoped to one object in your storage bucket, and the client PUTs the bytes directly there. Your service never touches the payload.

1. POST /uploads — client states filename, size and content type. Your API authorizes, enforces limits, records a pending upload, returns 201 with a signed PUT URL and an expiry.
2. Client PUTs the bytes straight to object storage. Resumable, parallelisable, and entirely outside your capacity planning.
3. POST /uploads/{id}/completion, or a storage event notification. Your API verifies size and checksum, scans it, and only now makes it visible.

The three rules that make this safe:

  • Sign narrowly. One object key, one method, a short expiry (minutes), and a Content-Length range if your storage supports it. A signed URL is a bearer credential: whoever holds it can use it.
  • Never trust the client's declared content type or size. Verify after the fact, in step 3, from the object's real metadata. The declared values are a hint for the limit check, not a fact.
  • Nothing is visible until step 3 completes. An upload that stops after step 2 must remain invisible and be swept up by a job, or you will accumulate half-uploaded objects that appear in listings.

Multipart form uploads (multipart/form-data) remain fine for small files — an avatar, a CSV import — where the round-trip saving is worth more than the worker time. The threshold is roughly "a few megabytes"; past that, get the bytes out of your request path.

Exercises

Exercise 4.1 — Fix the payload. Eleven things are wrong or risky here. Find at least eight, and rewrite it.

{
  "OrderID": 9007199254740993,
  "cust_id": "4183",
  "isNotCancelled": true,
  "order_total": 42.5,
  "created": "04/03/2026 10:02",
  "status": 3,
  "shipping_timeout": 30,
  "items": ["widget", {"sku": "B-2", "qty": 1}],
  "discount": 15,
  "notes": "",
  "success": true
}
Solution 
{
  "id": "9007199254740993",
  "customerId": "4183",
  "cancelled": false,
  "total": {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"},
  "createdAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z",
  "status": "awaiting_payment",
  "shippingTimeoutSeconds": 30,
  "items": [{"sku": "A-1", "quantity": 1}, {"sku": "B-2", "quantity": 1}],
  "discountPercent": 15,
  "notes": null
}
  1. Mixed case conventions — OrderID, cust_id, isNotCancelled in one object. Pick one; this uses camelCase.
  2. Numeric ID above 2^53 — corrupts in any JavaScript client. String.
  3. OrderID inside the order — it is id (§4.1).
  4. Negative boolean — isNotCancelled: true requires a double negative to read. cancelled: false.
  5. Float money with no currency — 42.5 cannot be represented exactly and does not say in what. Minor units plus an ISO 4217 code.
  6. Ambiguous date, no timezone — 04/03/2026 is March 4th or April 3rd. RFC 3339 with Z.
  7. Numeric enum — 3 is unreadable in every log line. A stable lowercase string.
  8. Unitless duration — shipping_timeout: 30 is 30 of something. Put the unit in the name.
  9. Heterogeneous array — a string next to an object makes the field untypeable.
  10. Ambiguous rate — discount: 15 is 15% or £0.15. Name it.
  11. success inside a 200 — the status line already said so (§2.4).

Bonus: "notes": "" — decide whether empty string or null means "no note" and reject the other (§4.2); qty should be quantity, since abbreviating one field and not the others is its own inconsistency.

Exercise 4.2 — Which patch format? For each change, say whether JSON Merge Patch can express it, and if not, what to do instead.

  1. Set status to "shipped".
  2. Clear the note field.
  3. Set note to the literal JSON value null, meaning "explicitly unknown", distinct from absent.
  4. Add one tag to tags without sending the others.
  5. Change address.city and leave address.postcode alone.
  6. Update only if the current version is 4.
Solution
  1. Yes. {"status": "shipped"}.
  2. Yes. {"note": null} — that is precisely what null means in RFC 7386.
  3. No. Merge Patch has spent null on deletion, so it cannot assign it. Options: a sentinel value the API documents; a separate noteKnown boolean; or JSON Patch, where {"op":"replace","path":"/note","value":null} is unambiguous. This is Merge Patch's one genuine expressiveness gap and is worth knowing before you choose it.
  4. No. Arrays are replaced wholesale, so the client must send the complete new list — which is a lost-update race if two clients add a tag at once. Better: expose tags as a sub-collection (PUT /orders/91/tags/express, DELETE /orders/91/tags/priority), which is idempotent and race-free. JSON Patch's {"op":"add","path":"/tags/-","value":"express"} also works, but does not solve the race.
  5. Yes. Nested objects merge recursively — this is line three of the §4.4 transcript.
  6. No. Merge Patch has no conditional. Use If-Match with the resource's ETag (§10.6), which is the HTTP-native answer and works with any patch format, or JSON Patch's test operation, which is atomic within the patch.

Exercise 4.3 — The PATCH that deleted everything. This handler ships, and within a day customers report that editing their display name cleared their bio, timezone and avatar. Diagnose it and fix it.

from pydantic import BaseModel

class ProfilePatch(BaseModel):
    displayName: str | None = None
    bio: str | None = None
    timezone: str | None = None

def patch_profile(current: dict, body: ProfilePatch) -> dict:
    return current | body.model_dump()
Solution

model_dump() emits every field, filling unmentioned ones with their default of None. So a body of {"displayName": "Ada"} becomes {"displayName": "Ada", "bio": None, "timezone": None}, and the merge writes those nulls over real data. It is §4.2's "absent versus null" collapse, in production.

avatar was also cleared even though the model has no such field — a hint that the real handler was writing the dumped dict straight into a document store, so the whole record was replaced. That is a second bug: the update should touch named fields only.

from pydantic import BaseModel

class ProfilePatch(BaseModel):
    displayName: str | None = None
    bio: str | None = None
    timezone: str | None = None

def patch_profile(current: dict, body: ProfilePatch) -> dict:
    changes = body.model_dump(exclude_unset=True)       # only what the client sent
    updated = dict(current)
    for key, value in changes.items():
        if value is None:
            updated.pop(key, None)                      # RFC 7386: null clears
        else:
            updated[key] = value
    return updated

profile = {"displayName": "ada", "bio": "engineer", "timezone": "Europe/London",
           "avatar": "https://cdn.example.com/a.png"}
print(patch_profile(profile, ProfilePatch.model_validate({"displayName": "Ada L."})))
print(patch_profile(profile, ProfilePatch.model_validate({"bio": None})))

{'displayName': 'Ada L.', 'bio': 'engineer', 'timezone': 'Europe/London', 'avatar': 'https://cdn.example.com/a.png'}
{'displayName': 'ada', 'timezone': 'Europe/London', 'avatar': 'https://cdn.example.com/a.png'}

avatar survives both, because the handler now merges named changes instead of replacing the record. The first call changes one field; the second clears exactly the field the client asked to clear.

Two guards worth adding beyond the fix. Set model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid") so an unknown key is a 422 rather than being silently dropped — a client with a typo currently gets a 200 and no change (§6.1). And require If-Match on this endpoint (§10.6), because read-modify-write across two clients still races even with a correct patch.

Exercise 4.4 — Design the upload flow. Users upload profile photos of up to 5 MB and CSV imports of up to 2 GB. Design both, and say why they are different.

Solution

Profile photo — direct multipart upload. POST /users/{id}/avatar with multipart/form-data, a hard 5 MB body limit enforced by the server and the reverse proxy, content type verified from the file's magic bytes rather than the declared type, re-encoded server-side to strip EXIF (which contains GPS coordinates) and to neutralise anything embedded in the original. One round trip, and 5 MB does not meaningfully occupy a worker.

CSV import — presigned URL, three steps.

  1. POST /imports with {"filename": "orders.csv", "sizeBytes": 1934000000, "contentType": "text/csv"}. The API authorizes, checks the size against the customer's quota, creates an import record in state awaiting_upload, and returns 201 with a signed PUT URL valid for 15 minutes.
  2. The client PUTs to object storage directly, using multipart upload so a dropped connection resumes rather than restarts.
  3. POST /imports/{id}/completion. The API reads the object's real size and checksum from storage, rejects a mismatch, moves the record to processing, and enqueues a worker (§12.5). The response is 202 Accepted with Location: /imports/{id}, which the client polls (§12.4).

Why they differ. The photo is small, synchronous and cheap to validate; the round trip is worth more than the worker time. The CSV is large enough that (a) buffering it through the application server would tie up a worker for minutes and force a huge body limit on the whole service, (b) a network drop mid-upload must not mean starting over, and (c) processing it is asynchronous regardless — so the response was always going to be 202, and there is no synchronous experience to preserve.

The threshold between the two is roughly a few megabytes, and the deciding question is not really size but whether the work is synchronous. Once the answer is no, the upload may as well be direct.

SECTION 5

Designing Errors

Errors are the part of an API that gets designed last and used most. Every integration spends its first week in the failure paths, every support ticket is an error someone could not interpret, and every automatic retry is a machine acting on an error it was given. A good error response answers three questions at once — what went wrong, whose fault it is, and what to do next — and it answers them in a format the client did not have to write a bespoke parser for.

5.1 What an Error Response Owes the Caller

There are two audiences for every error, and they need different things from the same response.

The machine needs a stable code it can branch on, a status class that tells it whether to retry, and — where relevant — structured data such as which field failed or when to try again. It must never have to match on English text.
The human — the developer integrating with you, at 2am, with your response in a terminal — needs a sentence that says what happened, enough context to identify which thing failed, and an identifier they can quote in a support request.

From which a five-part checklist follows. An error response should carry:

  1. A correct status code (§2.3), because it is the only part every layer of the stack understands.
  2. A stable, machine-readable error code that is part of your contract and never changes — even when you improve the message.
  3. A human-readable message that describes this specific occurrence.
  4. Enough context to act: which field, which resource, which limit, which value.
  5. A correlation identifier tying the response to your logs (§15.2), so a user can quote it and you can find the request.

And it should carry none of: stack traces, SQL, internal hostnames, library versions, or the existence of resources the caller may not see (§5.6).

The stable code is the part most often skipped and most missed. Without it, a client that needs to handle "insufficient funds" differently from "card expired" — both 402, both distinct user experiences — has to match on your message text, and your next copy edit becomes a breaking change for them.

5.2 Problem Details: RFC 9457

You do not need to invent an error format. RFC 9457, Problem Details for HTTP APIs (July 2023, obsoleting RFC 7807) defines one, with the media type application/problem+json. Its purpose, in the RFC's own words, is "to avoid the need to define new error response formats for HTTP APIs".

Five defined members, all optional:

Member Type Meaning
type URI Identifies the problem type. This is the stable machine-readable code. Defaults to about:blank
title string A short, human-readable summary of the type — the same for every occurrence
status integer The HTTP status code, repeated so the body is self-contained
detail string A human-readable explanation of this occurrence
instance URI Identifies this specific occurrence

Plus extension members: any additional fields you need. This is where field-level validation detail, retry hints and correlation IDs go, and it is what makes the format usable rather than merely tidy.

{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/insufficient-funds",
  "title": "Insufficient funds",
  "status": 402,
  "detail": "Account a1 has a balance of £38.00; the transfer requires £120.00.",
  "instance": "/transfers/8f2c1a",
  "balance": {"amount": 3800, "currency": "GBP"},
  "required": {"amount": 12000, "currency": "GBP"},
  "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K"
}

Four rules make it work in practice:

  • type is an identifier, not necessarily a URL you serve. It must be a URI so it is globally unambiguous; whether it resolves is your choice. Serving documentation there is a genuine kindness, and it is what the RFC recommends.
  • title is per-type; detail is per-occurrence. Do not put the specific values in title, or clients that group by title get one group per error.
  • Never make a client parse detail. If a value matters to the machine, it is an extension member. The balance and required fields above exist so a client can render its own message, in its own language, without regular expressions.
  • Set the media type. Content-Type: application/problem+json is what tells a generic client this is a problem document rather than a resource. A +json suffix means any JSON parser handles it (§2.5).

The predictable objection is that a per-type URI is heavier than a short string like insufficient_funds. It is, slightly — and it buys you a namespace that cannot collide when your API is aggregated behind a gateway with three others. If you would rather have both, add an extension member ("code": "insufficient_funds") and keep type as the canonical identifier.

5.3 An Error Taxonomy

An error taxonomy is the enumerated, documented set of ways your API can fail. Writing one down is the difference between errors that clients can handle and errors that clients can only log.

The organising insight is that the taxonomy belongs to your domain, and the mapping to HTTP belongs to your transport layer (§1.6). Domain code raises domain errors; one table turns them into status codes and problem types.

import asyncio
import httpx
from dataclasses import dataclass
from fastapi import FastAPI, Request
from fastapi.responses import JSONResponse

# ---- the taxonomy: domain errors, knowing nothing about HTTP ---------------
class DomainError(Exception):
    """Base for every failure this service can express deliberately."""

@dataclass
class NotFound(DomainError):
    kind: str
    id: str

@dataclass
class Conflict(DomainError):
    reason: str

@dataclass
class InsufficientFunds(DomainError):
    balance: int
    required: int

@dataclass
class RateLimited(DomainError):
    retry_after: int

# ---- the mapping: exactly one place where HTTP enters the picture ----------
MAPPING: dict[type[DomainError], tuple[int, str, str]] = {
    NotFound:          (404, "not-found",         "Resource not found"),
    Conflict:          (409, "conflict",          "Conflicting state"),
    InsufficientFunds: (402, "insufficient-funds", "Insufficient funds"),
    RateLimited:       (429, "rate-limited",      "Rate limit exceeded"),
}
BASE = "https://api.example.com/problems/"

app = FastAPI()

@app.exception_handler(DomainError)
async def to_problem(request: Request, exc: DomainError) -> JSONResponse:
    status, slug, title = MAPPING[type(exc)]
    body = {"type": BASE + slug, "title": title, "status": status,
            "instance": request.url.path, "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K"}
    headers = {}
    match exc:
        case NotFound(kind=kind, id=rid):
            body["detail"] = f"No {kind} with id {rid}."
        case Conflict(reason=reason):
            body["detail"] = reason
        case InsufficientFunds(balance=bal, required=req):
            body["detail"] = f"Balance is {bal} minor units; {req} required."
            body |= {"balance": bal, "required": req}
        case RateLimited(retry_after=seconds):
            body["detail"] = f"Try again in {seconds} seconds."
            headers["Retry-After"] = str(seconds)
    return JSONResponse(body, status_code=status,
                        media_type="application/problem+json", headers=headers)

@app.get("/accounts/{account_id}")
def get_account(account_id: str) -> dict:
    raise NotFound("account", account_id)

@app.post("/transfers")
def transfer() -> dict:
    raise InsufficientFunds(balance=3800, required=12000)

@app.get("/search")
def search() -> dict:
    raise RateLimited(retry_after=30)

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://test") as client:
        for method, path in (("get", "/accounts/a9"), ("post", "/transfers"),
                             ("get", "/search")):
            r = await getattr(client, method)(path)
            print(r.status_code, r.headers["content-type"],
                  "retry-after=" + str(r.headers.get("retry-after")))
            print("   ", r.json())

asyncio.run(main())

404 application/problem+json retry-after=None
    {'type': 'https://api.example.com/problems/not-found', 'title': 'Resource not found', 'status': 404, 'instance': '/accounts/a9', 'requestId': '01JQ8F3P2K', 'detail': 'No account with id a9.'}
402 application/problem+json retry-after=None
    {'type': 'https://api.example.com/problems/insufficient-funds', 'title': 'Insufficient funds', 'status': 402, 'instance': '/transfers', 'requestId': '01JQ8F3P2K', 'detail': 'Balance is 3800 minor units; 12000 required.', 'balance': 3800, 'required': 12000}
429 application/problem+json retry-after=30
    {'type': 'https://api.example.com/problems/rate-limited', 'title': 'Rate limit exceeded', 'status': 429, 'instance': '/search', 'requestId': '01JQ8F3P2K', 'detail': 'Try again in 30 seconds.'}

Four properties of this structure are worth naming.

The handlers contain no status codes. get_account raises NotFound and knows nothing about 404, so the same domain code runs unchanged inside a queue worker (§12.5), a CLI or a scheduled job — none of which have an HTTP response to return. That is §1.6's dependency rule enforced by construction.

Adding a failure mode is a dataclass plus a row. The change is local and mechanical, which means it actually gets done rather than being approximated with the nearest existing error.

A missing row is a KeyError, not a silent 500. MAPPING[type(exc)] fails loudly the first time an unmapped domain error reaches the handler, and it fails in your test suite rather than in production — provided the error path is tested at all, which is Exercise 5.4.

Transport concerns stay in the transport layer. Retry-After is a header, so it is set here, from data the domain error carried. RateLimited knows there is a delay; it does not know there is a header.

Notice also what varies between the three documents. Every one carries type, title, status, instance and requestId — the members a generic client can rely on unconditionally. Only the InsufficientFunds document carries balance and required, because extension members are per-problem-type. A client that has never heard of this problem type still gets a usable response; one that has, gets the numbers without parsing a sentence.

5.4 Validation Errors

A validation error is the most common error your API will ever return, and it is the one where structure pays off most, because the client is almost always going to render it next to a form field.

The requirement: a client must be able to say which field, what is wrong with it, and what would be acceptable — for every failing field at once, not one at a time. An API that reports only the first error turns a five-field form into five round trips.

from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, ValidationError

class CreateOrder(BaseModel):
    customerId: str = Field(min_length=1)
    quantity: int = Field(ge=1, le=100)
    currency: str = Field(pattern=r"^[A-Z]{3}$")

def problem_from(exc: ValidationError, path: str) -> dict:
    return {
        "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/validation-error",
        "title": "Request body failed validation",
        "status": 422,
        "detail": f"{exc.error_count()} field(s) are invalid.",
        "instance": path,
        "errors": [
            {"field": ".".join(str(p) for p in err["loc"]),
             "code": err["type"],
             "message": err["msg"]}
            for err in exc.errors()
        ],
    }

try:
    CreateOrder.model_validate({"customerId": "", "quantity": 0, "currency": "gbp"})
except ValidationError as exc:
    problem = problem_from(exc, "/orders")

print(problem["status"], problem["detail"])
for item in problem["errors"]:
    print(f"  {item['field']:<12} {item['code']:<22} {item['message']}")

422 3 field(s) are invalid.
  customerId   string_too_short       String should have at least 1 character
  quantity     greater_than_equal     Input should be greater than or equal to 1
  currency     string_pattern_mismatch String should match pattern '^[A-Z]{3}$'

Note the shape. errors is an extension member of a Problem Details document, so the response is still application/problem+json and a generic client still understands status and title. Each entry has a field path a client can map to a form input, a code that is stable and branchable, and a message for a human. All three failures come back together.

Four refinements worth making before this reaches production:

  • Use a path, not a name. items.0.sku locates the failure inside a nested array; sku does not.
  • Keep the code values yours. string_too_short is pydantic's vocabulary; leaking it means a library upgrade can change your contract. Map to your own codes if you care about stability, which §14.4 argues you should.
  • Never echo the rejected value back into detail unfiltered. It is attacker-controlled and may be rendered in someone's admin panel; that is a stored-XSS path.
  • Reject unknown fields. pydantic's extra="forbid" turns a client's typo into a 422 rather than a silent no-op — which is the difference between a bug found in five seconds and one found in production.

5.5 Choosing the Status Code for a Domain Failure

The mapping table in §5.3 has to be filled in with real judgement. These are the cases that recur.

Domain failure Code Why not the obvious alternative
Body will not parse 400 Nothing to validate yet
Body parses, values invalid 422 Not 400: syntax was fine
Unknown Content-Type on a write 415 Not 400: the problem is the type, and the client can fix it
No credentials / bad token 401 + WWW-Authenticate Not 403: re-authenticating would help
Valid identity, no permission 403 Not 401: a new token changes nothing
No permission, existence is secret 404 Deliberate. See §9.2
Resource genuinely absent 404 —
Removed on purpose, permanently 410 404 invites clients to keep asking
Method wrong for this resource 405 + Allow Not 404: the resource exists
Unique constraint violated 409 Not 422: the payload is fine, the world is not
State machine forbids it 409 "Order already shipped" is about state
Stale If-Match 412 Precondition semantics are defined; do not invent
Precondition required and missing 428 Tells the client to add If-Match rather than failing opaquely
Payload too large 413 With a limit in the problem body
Rate limit hit 429 + Retry-After Not 503: it is about the caller, not the service
Downstream refused 502 Not 500: it locates the fault
Downstream timed out 504 Distinguishable in dashboards from 502
Deliberately shedding load 503 + Retry-After Tells clients to back off (§11.7)
Anything unhandled 500 And an alert

Two judgement calls that come up constantly:

A business rule failure is 409 or 422, not 400. "You cannot cancel a shipped order" is 409 (state). "Quantity must be at least 1" is 422 (the value). If the caller could fix it by sending different data, it is 422; if they would have to change the world first, it is 409.

Do not invent codes. 499, 440, 520 and friends are vendor extensions that generic clients treat as "some 4xx/5xx". The registered set is large enough.

5.6 What Never Goes in an Error Body

An error response is the highest-value reconnaissance surface in your API, because it is the one place your service volunteers information about its own internals.

Never include:

  • Stack traces or exception class names. They reveal your language, framework, library versions and directory layout. 500 bodies in production should be a fixed, boring problem document.
  • SQL, or fragments of it. A database error echoed back to the caller is the difference between blind and non-blind injection (the Web Security course covers what an attacker does with that).
  • Internal hostnames, IPs, file paths, or queue names. Useful for lateral movement, useless to the caller.
  • Whether an account exists. "No user with that email" on login, versus "wrong password", is a user-enumeration oracle. Return one message for both.
  • Any hint that differentiates "not found" from "not permitted" where existence is confidential (§9.2) — including through response time.
  • Upstream errors passed through verbatim. Your payment provider's error body is theirs, not yours, and may contain their internal detail. Translate it into your taxonomy.

What replaces them is the correlation identifier. Return "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K" (and the matching X-Request-Id header), log the full detail server-side against that ID, and tell integrators to quote it. The caller gets something actionable; the internals stay inside.

The related discipline is that 5xx bodies should be constant. Anything that varies is something an attacker can use as a signal, and by definition your service did not understand what happened, so any specific claim you make is a guess.

5.7 Signalling Retryability

The status class already tells a client whether to retry: 4xx means no, 5xx means maybe. That is coarse, and two refinements repay the effort.

Retry-After on 429 and 503. RFC 9110 §10.2.3 allows either a delay in seconds or an HTTP date. Sending it turns a guess into a schedule and is the single most effective thing you can do to prevent a retry storm from turning a partial outage into a full one (§11.6).

An explicit retryability hint as an extension member. Some 4xx responses are worth retrying — 409 from an optimistic-concurrency clash is transient, 423 Locked resolves on its own — and some 5xx responses are not, because the request will fail identically forever.

{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/version-conflict",
  "title": "Version conflict",
  "status": 409,
  "detail": "The order was modified by another request. Re-read it and retry.",
  "retryable": true,
  "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K"
}

A caveat worth stating: "retryable": true is your convention, not a standard, so it helps only clients that read your documentation. Retry-After is standard and is honoured by generic tooling. Send both, and rely on the standard one.

Exercises

Exercise 5.1 — Write the problem document. A client POSTs a transfer of £1,200 from an account holding £38. Write the complete response — status line, headers and body — as a Problem Details document. Then say which two members a client should branch on and why.

Solution 
HTTP/1.1 402 Payment Required
Content-Type: application/problem+json
Content-Language: en
X-Request-Id: 01JQ8F3P2K
Cache-Control: no-store

{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/insufficient-funds",
  "title": "Insufficient funds",
  "status": 402,
  "detail": "Account a1 has a balance of 3800 minor units; 120000 required.",
  "instance": "/transfers/8f2c1a",
  "balance": {"amount": 3800, "currency": "GBP"},
  "required": {"amount": 120000, "currency": "GBP"},
  "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K",
  "retryable": false
}

Branch on status and type. status gives the class — is this my fault, should I retry — and every generic HTTP client already understands it. type gives the specific cause, and it is the only member guaranteed stable: title and detail are prose and will be reworded, instance is per-occurrence.

Never branch on detail. A client that does if "insufficient" in detail breaks the day you translate the message, and translation is exactly why Content-Language is on the response.

Cache-Control: no-store is there because a 402 about one account's balance must never be reused. retryable: false is a hint — retrying will not conjure funds — and a client should still respect the absence of Retry-After as the authoritative signal.

Exercise 5.2 — Classify ten failures. Give the status code for each, and one sentence of justification.

  1. Content-Type: application/xml on a JSON-only endpoint.
  2. A JWT that expired 40 seconds ago.
  3. A valid token whose scope does not include orders:write.
  4. POST /orders where quantity is -3.
  5. POST /users with an email that is already registered.
  6. DELETE /orders/91 on an order that shipped yesterday.
  7. PATCH /orders/91 with If-Match: "old" and a current ETag of "new".
  8. GET /orders/91 where the order belongs to another tenant.
  9. Your payment provider returned a connection timeout.
  10. A KeyError escaped a handler.
Solution
  1. 415 Unsupported Media Type — the problem is the declared type, and the client can fix it by changing one header.
  2. 401 Unauthorized with WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token" — the credential is the problem, and a refresh will fix it. Not 403, which would send the client into a permission-escalation conversation instead of a token refresh.
  3. 403 Forbidden — identity is established and understood; the answer is no. A new token with the same scopes changes nothing. (If your token could be re-issued with wider scope, 403 with a problem type naming the missing scope is the useful form.)
  4. 422 Unprocessable Content — the JSON parsed; the value is out of range. Include the field path and the constraint.
  5. 409 Conflict — the payload is valid; the world already contains that email. Note §5.6: on a signup endpoint this leaks account existence, which is usually accepted there and never acceptable on login.
  6. 409 Conflict — a state-machine refusal, not a permission or validation problem.
  7. 412 Precondition Failed — this is exactly what the code is defined for (§10.6). Do not substitute 409.
  8. 404 Not Found — deliberately. 403 confirms the order exists, which is a cross-tenant information leak (§9.2, §9.5).
  9. 504 Gateway Timeout — locates the fault upstream and is distinguishable from 502 in a dashboard. Include Retry-After if you know the provider's recovery profile.
  10. 500 Internal Server Error, a constant body, and an alert. The KeyError itself never leaves the process (§5.6).

Exercise 5.3 — Find the leaks. Three separate disclosures, plus one design flaw. Name all four.

{
  "error": "psycopg.errors.UniqueViolation: duplicate key value violates unique constraint \"users_email_key\"\n  File \"/srv/app/repo/users.py\", line 88, in create",
  "host": "api-worker-07.internal.example.com",
  "message": "A user with email ada@example.com already exists",
  "status": 200
}
Solution

Leak 1 — the database error and stack frame. It reveals PostgreSQL, the psycopg driver, the exact constraint name (users_email_key, which is the schema), the deployment path (/srv/app/repo/users.py) and a line number. Every one of those narrows an attacker's search.

Leak 2 — the internal hostname. api-worker-07.internal.example.com reveals the naming scheme, the instance count and the internal domain — reconnaissance for SSRF (§6.5) and lateral movement.

Leak 3 — account existence tied to a specific email. Acceptable on a signup form (the user is telling you the address); a user-enumeration oracle anywhere it can be called without owning the address, and never acceptable on a login or password-reset endpoint.

Design flaw — "status": 200. The HTTP status was almost certainly 200 too, since the body claims it. Every retry policy, circuit breaker (§11.5), dashboard and response.ok check in every client now believes this request succeeded (§2.4).

The corrected response:

HTTP/1.1 409 Conflict
Content-Type: application/problem+json
X-Request-Id: 01JQ8F3P2K

{
  "type": "https://api.example.com/problems/email-already-registered",
  "title": "Email already registered",
  "status": 409,
  "detail": "That email address is already in use.",
  "instance": "/users",
  "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K"
}

The full driver error goes to the log, indexed by 01JQ8F3P2K (§15.2). Everything an engineer needs is still one search away; nothing an attacker wants is on the wire.

Exercise 5.4 — Test the error path. Nothing in normal operation exercises an error response, so its defects survive every happy-path test. Write a test for the 429 problem document from §5.3 that would catch an extra member leaking into the body, and explain why the obvious assertion does not.

Solution

The obvious assertion — checking that the members you expect are present — passes happily while extra members leak alongside them. Assert the exact key set instead.

import asyncio, httpx
from fastapi import FastAPI, Request
from fastapi.responses import JSONResponse

class RateLimited(Exception):
    def __init__(self, retry_after: int) -> None:
        self.retry_after = retry_after

app = FastAPI()

@app.exception_handler(RateLimited)
async def handler(request: Request, exc: RateLimited) -> JSONResponse:
    body = {"type": "https://api.example.com/problems/rate-limited",
            "title": "Rate limit exceeded", "status": 429,
            "detail": f"Try again in {exc.retry_after} seconds.",
            "instance": request.url.path}
    return JSONResponse(body, status_code=429,
                        media_type="application/problem+json",
                        headers={"Retry-After": str(exc.retry_after)})

@app.get("/search")
def search() -> dict:
    raise RateLimited(retry_after=30)

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://t") as client:
        r = await client.get("/search")
        assert r.status_code == 429
        assert r.headers["content-type"].startswith("application/problem+json")
        assert r.headers["Retry-After"] == "30"
        weak = {"type", "title", "status"} <= set(r.json())      # passes on any leak
        exact = set(r.json()) == {"type", "title", "status", "detail", "instance"}
        print("subset assertion:", weak, " exact-key assertion:", exact)

asyncio.run(main())

subset assertion: True  exact-key assertion: True

Both pass here because the document is correct. Add a stray "internalHost" member and only the second one fails — which is the whole point, and it is the same discipline as extra="forbid" on the way in (§5.4). It is what would catch the §5.6 leaks before they ship.

What a complete error-path suite asserts, one test per problem type:

  • the status code,
  • Content-Type: application/problem+json,
  • the exact member set, so nothing leaks and nothing silently disappears,
  • type, which is the member clients branch on and therefore part of the contract,
  • every header the type promises — Retry-After for 429 and 503, WWW-Authenticate for 401, Allow for 405.

§15.6 turns exactly this into a contract test, so that a client can rely on it across releases rather than re-checking by hand.

SECTION 6

Validation and the Trust Boundary

Every service has exactly one line across which data changes from whatever arrived to something with a known shape. Everything before that line is bytes an attacker controls. Everything after it can be reasoned about. Where you draw the line, and whether you draw it once or scatter it through the code, determines how much of your service you have to be suspicious of — which is the difference between a codebase you can read and one you have to audit.

6.1 Parse, Don't Validate

There are two ways to handle untrusted input, and they look similar for about a week.

Validate: check that the incoming dictionary looks acceptable, then pass the dictionary onwards. The knowledge gained by checking evaporates immediately — three functions later, nothing in the type of the value records that it was ever checked, so the next person adds another check, and the one after that forgets.

Parse: convert the incoming bytes into a value of a type that cannot hold anything invalid, and fail if that is impossible. The knowledge is now in the type. CreateOrder cannot exist with a negative quantity, so no function receiving one needs to ask.

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

# --- validate: the check happens, and then is forgotten ------------------
def handle_validated(body: dict) -> str:
    if not isinstance(body.get("quantity"), int) or body["quantity"] < 1:
        return "422"
    return charge(body)                      # charge() receives a plain dict

def charge(body: dict) -> str:
    return f"charging for {body['quantity']} units"   # is quantity still valid? unknowable

# --- parse: the check produces a value that carries its own guarantee ----
class CreateOrder(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")
    sku: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=32)
    quantity: int = Field(ge=1, le=100)

def charge_typed(order: CreateOrder) -> str:
    return f"charging for {order.quantity} units"     # cannot be < 1. Nothing to check.

print(handle_validated({"quantity": 3}))
try:
    CreateOrder.model_validate({"sku": "A-1", "quantity": 0})
except ValidationError as exc:
    print("rejected:", exc.errors()[0]["type"], "on", exc.errors()[0]["loc"])
print(charge_typed(CreateOrder.model_validate({"sku": "A-1", "quantity": 3})))
try:
    CreateOrder.model_validate({"sku": "A-1", "quantity": 3, "isAdmin": True})
except ValidationError as exc:
    print("rejected:", exc.errors()[0]["type"], "on", exc.errors()[0]["loc"])

charging for 3 units
rejected: greater_than_equal on ('quantity',)
charging for 3 units
rejected: extra_forbidden on ('isAdmin',)

The two charge functions do the same work; only one of them can be read in isolation and understood to be safe. Schema validation at the boundary is what makes the rest of the service ordinary code rather than defensive code — and note that schema validation here is not a separate step that returns a boolean, but a constructor that either produces a valid value or fails.

The last two lines matter as much as the rest. extra="forbid" is off by default in pydantic, and turning it on has two payoffs: a client's typo becomes a 422 instead of a silently ignored field, and an attacker's injected field becomes a rejection instead of a possibility (§6.3).

6.2 The Boundary Model Is Not the Domain Model

The temptation is to have one class: the pydantic model is the database row is the response body. It is fewer lines on day one and it couples three things that change for entirely different reasons.

The request model changes when clients need to send something new. It contains only what a client is allowed to set.
The domain model changes when the business rules change. It contains invariants and behaviour.
The response model changes when clients need to see something new — and, critically, it is the allowlist of what leaves the building.
The persistence model changes when the storage does. Adding a column must not change your API.

Collapsing them is how an internal is_admin column ends up in a JSON response, and how adding a password_hash column to a table publishes it. Keeping them apart costs a few lines of mapping and buys you the ability to change any one of the four independently — which is the entire argument for DTOs in §1.6, restated where it bites hardest.

The practical rule: the response model is an allowlist, and it is explicit. response_model=OrderOut in FastAPI, or an explicit serializer anywhere else. Never return dict(row).

6.3 Mass Assignment

Mass assignment — OWASP calls the API-specific version Broken Object Property Level Authorization, API3:2023 — is what happens when a client can set a field the API never intended to expose. It is one of the most common serious API flaws precisely because it is invisible: the endpoint works exactly as designed for well-behaved clients.

PATCH /users/me HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{"displayName": "Ada", "role": "admin", "accountBalance": 1000000}

If the handler does user.update(**body), that request is a privilege escalation. Two mechanisms prevent it, and you want both:

An explicit input model containing only client-settable fields. role is not in UpdateProfile, so it cannot arrive. This is the primary defence and it is structural — it cannot be forgotten in one handler out of thirty.

extra="forbid", so an attempt is a loud 422 rather than a silent drop. A silent drop is safe but tells you nothing; a rejection surfaces both client bugs and probing in your logs.

The mirror-image flaw is over-exposure on the way out: returning fields the caller may not see. Same root cause, same fix — an explicit output model.

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict

class UserRecord(BaseModel):                 # what the database holds
    id: str
    displayName: str
    email: str
    role: str
    passwordHash: str
    internalRiskScore: int

class UpdateProfile(BaseModel):              # what a client may SET
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")
    displayName: str | None = None
    email: str | None = None

class UserOut(BaseModel):                    # what a client may SEE
    id: str
    displayName: str
    email: str

record = UserRecord(id="u1", displayName="ada", email="ada@example.com",
                    role="user", passwordHash="argon2id$...", internalRiskScore=17)

attack = {"displayName": "Ada", "role": "admin", "internalRiskScore": 0}
try:
    UpdateProfile.model_validate(attack)
except Exception as exc:
    rejected = sorted(err["loc"][0] for err in exc.errors())
    print("rejected fields:", rejected)

allowed = UpdateProfile.model_validate({"displayName": "Ada"})
updated = record.model_copy(update=allowed.model_dump(exclude_unset=True))
print("after update:", updated.role, updated.internalRiskScore)
print("what the client sees:", UserOut.model_validate(updated.model_dump()).model_dump())

rejected fields: ['internalRiskScore', 'role']
after update: user 17
what the client sees: {'id': 'u1', 'displayName': 'Ada', 'email': 'ada@example.com'}

Note the third line: role is still user and the risk score is untouched, because those fields were never reachable. And the output model omits passwordHash, role and internalRiskScore structurally — adding a column to UserRecord tomorrow cannot publish it.

A per-field authorization note. Some fields are settable by some callers: an admin may legitimately set role. The answer is not to widen UpdateProfile; it is a second model, AdminUpdateProfile, bound to a route only an admin can reach (§9.3). One model per authorization level keeps the allowlist readable, and makes "who can set this field" answerable by reading one class.

6.4 Limits Are Validation Too

Type checks answer "is this the right shape". They do not answer "is this a reasonable amount", and OWASP's API4:2023 — Unrestricted Resource Consumption — is entirely about the second question. Every one of these must have a number:

Limit Typical value What happens without it
Request body size 1 MB for JSON A 2 GB body exhausts memory before your handler runs
JSON nesting depth 20–64 A deeply nested document blows the parser's stack
Array length, per field 100–1000 {"ids": [...50000 items]} becomes 50,000 queries
String length, per field field-specific A 10 MB "display name" reaches your database and your logs
Page size default 20, max 100 ?limit=1000000 is a denial-of-service request (§7.1)
Total fields / query depth for GraphQL A nested query is a combinatorial explosion
Upload size explicit §4.7
Request timeout 10–30 s A slow client holds a worker indefinitely
Concurrent requests per client explicit One caller consumes the whole pool (§11.6)

Two implementation notes worth having.

Enforce body size at the edge as well as in the application. By the time your framework parses the body it has already been read into memory. Nginx's client_max_body_size or your gateway's equivalent rejects it earlier and cheaper, and the application limit is the backstop for when the edge configuration drifts.

A limit that returns 200 is not a limit. Silently truncating a 50,000-element array to 1,000 produces a wrong result the client believes. Return 413 or 422 and say what the limit is.

6.5 Server-Side Request Forgery

SSRF (OWASP API7:2023) is what happens when your server fetches a URL the client supplied. Webhook registration, "import from URL", avatar fetching, PDF rendering, link previews — all of them, by design, make your server issue a request on an attacker's behalf. And your server sits inside the network perimeter, which is precisely where the attacker is not.

The canonical target is the cloud metadata endpoint at 169.254.169.254, which on a misconfigured instance returns credentials. Others: localhost admin interfaces, internal service names, and file:// URLs.

Naïve defences all fail:

  • Blocklisting localhost and 127.0.0.1 — misses 127.1, 0.0.0.0, [::1], 2130706433 (decimal), and any hostname whose DNS record points at a private address.
  • Checking the hostname against an allowlist and then fetching the URL — vulnerable to DNS rebinding: the name resolves to a public address when you check it and a private one microseconds later when your HTTP client resolves it again.
  • Following redirects — an allowed URL can 302 you to 169.254.169.254.

The defence that works has four parts: resolve the name yourself, check every resolved address, connect to the checked address, and refuse redirects.

import ipaddress
import socket

def resolve_all(host: str, port: int) -> list[str]:
    try:
        infos = socket.getaddrinfo(host, port, proto=socket.IPPROTO_TCP)
    except socket.gaierror:
        return []
    return sorted({info[4][0] for info in infos})

def is_public(addr: str) -> bool:
    ip = ipaddress.ip_address(addr)
    return not (ip.is_private or ip.is_loopback or ip.is_link_local
                or ip.is_reserved or ip.is_multicast or ip.is_unspecified)

def vet(url_host: str, port: int = 443) -> tuple[bool, str]:
    """Resolve once, judge every answer, and report the address to connect to."""
    addresses = resolve_all(url_host, port)
    if not addresses:
        return False, "does not resolve"
    bad = [a for a in addresses if not is_public(a)]
    if bad:
        return False, f"resolves to non-public address {bad[0]}"
    return True, f"connect to {addresses[0]}"

for host in ("localhost", "127.1", "0.0.0.0", "169.254.169.254", "[::1]",
             "2130706433", "10.0.0.5", "192.168.1.1", "93.184.216.34"):
    ok, why = vet(host.strip("[]"))
    print(f"{host:<18} {'ALLOW' if ok else 'BLOCK':<6} {why}")

localhost          BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 127.0.0.1
127.1              BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 127.0.0.1
0.0.0.0            BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 0.0.0.0
169.254.169.254    BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 169.254.169.254
[::1]              BLOCK  resolves to non-public address ::1
2130706433         BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 127.0.0.1
10.0.0.5           BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 10.0.0.5
192.168.1.1        BLOCK  resolves to non-public address 192.168.1.1
93.184.216.34      ALLOW  connect to 93.184.216.34

Every obfuscation is caught, and only the genuinely public address gets through — because none of the spellings survive resolution. 127.1 and 2130706433 both become 127.0.0.1 before anything is judged. That is the whole trick: judge addresses, never strings.

Two details in is_public are easy to get wrong. is_private in Python's ipaddress covers more than RFC 1918 — it also includes the documentation ranges (192.0.2.0/24, 198.51.100.0/24, 203.0.113.0/24), which is why a URL pointing at one of those is refused. And is_reserved and is_unspecified are both needed to catch 0.0.0.0, which on many stacks means "this host" and is a working loopback alias.

Four more requirements to make it real:

  • Connect to the address you vetted, not to the hostname again. Pass the resolved IP to your HTTP client with the original Host header (and, for TLS, the original SNI). Otherwise the second resolution is the rebinding window.
  • Disable redirect following, or re-vet every hop. httpx and requests both follow redirects on request; turn it off explicitly.
  • Restrict the scheme to http and https. file://, gopher:// and ftp:// have no business here.
  • Egress-filter at the network level. Application-level checks are the second line; a firewall rule denying outbound connections to private ranges from the fetcher's subnet is the first, and it survives your next refactor.

6.6 An Upstream Response Is Still Input

OWASP's API10:2023 — Unsafe Consumption of APIs — names a habit almost everyone has: validating what users send and trusting what other services return. Unsafe consumption is easy to miss precisely because the upstream is usually a partner you have a contract with, which feels like a reason to trust its bytes and is not. The trust is misplaced for three reasons. The upstream may be compromised. It may have a bug. And it may simply have changed its response shape in a release nobody told you about.

The rules are the same ones you apply to client input:

  • Parse the response into a model. A KeyError deep in your business logic three calls later is a much worse diagnosis than a rejection at the call site.
  • Bound it. Set a response size limit and a timeout on every outbound call (§11.4). An upstream that starts streaming gigabytes should not take your service with it.
  • Never interpolate an upstream value into anything executable — SQL, a shell command, an HTML template — without the same escaping you would apply to user input.
  • Do not follow URLs from an upstream response without the §6.5 treatment. A compromised partner returning {"next": "http://169.254.169.254/..."} is exactly the SSRF path, one hop removed.
  • Treat a schema change as a failure, not a shrug. If a field you depend on disappears, fail loudly. Silently substituting a default propagates the upstream's bug into your data.
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, ValidationError

class ExchangeRate(BaseModel):
    """What the upstream promised. Anything else is a failure, not a surprise."""
    base: str = Field(pattern=r"^[A-Z]{3}$")
    quote: str = Field(pattern=r"^[A-Z]{3}$")
    rate: float = Field(gt=0, lt=1000)

good = {"base": "EUR", "quote": "GBP", "rate": 0.842}
drifted = {"base": "EUR", "quote": "GBP", "rate": "0.842"}      # now a string
broken = {"base": "EUR", "quote": "GBP", "rate": -1}
missing = {"base": "EUR", "quote": "GBP"}

for name, payload in (("good", good), ("drifted", drifted),
                      ("broken", broken), ("missing", missing)):
    try:
        print(f"{name:<9} ok   -> {ExchangeRate.model_validate(payload).rate}")
    except ValidationError as exc:
        print(f"{name:<9} FAIL -> {exc.errors()[0]['type']}")

good      ok   -> 0.842
drifted   ok   -> 0.842
broken    FAIL -> greater_than
missing   FAIL -> missing

Two lessons in four lines. A negative rate and a missing field are caught, which is the point. But drifted — the rate arriving as a string — was quietly coerced to a float, because pydantic's default mode is lax. That is usually what you want at an HTTP boundary, where everything starts as text; it is usually not what you want from a typed upstream, where a type change is a signal that something has been redeployed. Use model_config = ConfigDict(strict=True) on models that parse machine-to-machine responses, and keep lax coercion for models that parse user input.

6.7 Where Validation Must Not Live

Three places validation gets put where it does not work:

In the client only. Client-side validation is a user-experience feature. It is not a control: curl exists. Every rule enforced in a browser must be enforced again on the server, and if it is only on the server, that is correct but slow to discover — hence duplicating the rules and generating the client's copy from your OpenAPI schema (§14.2).

In the database only. A CHECK constraint or a NOT NULL is a superb backstop and a poor error message. The client gets a 500 from a driver exception (or, if you translate it, a 409 with no field information), and you have no control over the wording. Constraints belong in the database as well, never instead.

Scattered through the handlers. The moment a rule is checked in two handlers it will be checked in only one of the next three. Rules belong in the model, so that the type carries them.

There is one case where the boundary genuinely is not enough, and it is worth naming because it looks like a validation problem and is not. Rules that depend on state cannot be checked at parse time. "The coupon must not be expired", "the seat must still be free", "the balance must cover the transfer" — these are not properties of the request, they are properties of the world at the moment of the write, and re-checking them inside the transaction is the only correct place (§12.1). Parsing gets you a well-formed request; only the transaction can tell you whether it is still a valid one.

Exercises

Exercise 6.1 — Close the mass-assignment hole. This endpoint is exploitable. Show the request that exploits it, then fix it structurally rather than by adding a check.

@app.patch("/users/me")
async def update_me(request: Request, user: dict = Depends(current_user)) -> dict:
    body = await request.json()
    user.update(body)
    await save(user)
    return user
Solution

The exploit is any field the record happens to have:

PATCH /users/me HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{"role": "admin", "emailVerified": true, "tenantId": "victim-corp", "id": "u-other"}

user.update(body) copies all of it. role is privilege escalation; emailVerified bypasses a verification flow; tenantId is a cross-tenant move (§9.5); and id may re-key the record entirely. The endpoint also returns user, so passwordHash and every internal field go straight back out.

The structural fix — an input allowlist, an output allowlist, and no dictionary in between:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, EmailStr

class UpdateMe(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")
    displayName: str | None = Field(default=None, min_length=1, max_length=80)
    email: EmailStr | None = None

class MeOut(BaseModel):
    id: str
    displayName: str
    email: str

@app.patch("/users/me", response_model=MeOut)
async def update_me(body: UpdateMe, user: User = Depends(current_user)) -> User:
    changes = body.model_dump(exclude_unset=True)
    updated = await users.apply(user.id, changes)     # named columns only
    return updated

Why this is better than adding if "role" in body: raise:

  • It cannot be forgotten in the next endpoint, because the model is the parameter.
  • It is self-documenting and appears correctly in the OpenAPI schema (§14.2).
  • extra="forbid" makes the attempt a 422 you can see in your logs.
  • response_model=MeOut means adding a column tomorrow cannot leak it.
  • exclude_unset=True preserves §4.2's absent-versus-null distinction, so this is also a correct PATCH.

One more thing to add: changing email should not take effect immediately. It should create a pending verification and only apply on confirmation — otherwise an attacker with a session hijacks the account by changing the address and triggering a password reset.

Exercise 6.2 — Defeat the SSRF filter. A team ships this for webhook registration. Give three distinct URLs that get through, and explain what each abuses.

from urllib.parse import urlparse
import httpx

BLOCKED = {"localhost", "127.0.0.1", "169.254.169.254"}

async def register_webhook(url: str) -> int:
    host = urlparse(url).hostname
    if host in BLOCKED:
        raise ValueError("blocked")
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(follow_redirects=True) as client:
        r = await client.get(url)          # verify the endpoint answers
    return r.status_code
Solution

1. http://127.1/admin — string blocklisting. 127.1 is a valid IPv4 shorthand for 127.0.0.1, and it is not in the set. http://0.0.0.0/, http://[::1]/, http://2130706433/ (decimal) and http://0x7f.0.0.1/ (hex) all work the same way. There is no finite list of spellings, which is why §6.5 judges resolved addresses instead.

2. http://attacker.test/, where that name has an A record for 169.254.169.254 — the check is on the name, and the name is public. Nothing in the filter looks at where it points. A variant of the same abuse is DNS rebinding: a record with a one-second TTL that answers publicly for the check and privately for the fetch.

3. https://attacker.test/redirect, returning 302 Location: http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/ — follow_redirects=True walks the client straight to the metadata endpoint, and the response comes back to the attacker's webhook registration. This one gets through even if the hostname check were perfect.

Bonus: file:///etc/passwd and gopher:// — urlparse gives no hostname for a file: URL, so host is None, which is not in BLOCKED.

The fix applies §6.5 in full: allowlist the scheme to http/https; resolve the hostname yourself; reject if any resolved address fails is_public; connect to the resolved address (preserving Host and SNI) rather than re-resolving; set follow_redirects=False and re-vet each hop manually if you must follow them; cap the response size and timeout; and back all of it with an egress firewall rule so that a mistake in this code is not the only thing standing between an attacker and the metadata service.

There is one further wrinkle specific to webhooks: verification traffic should not go out from the same network position as the rest of your service. A dedicated egress path with no route to internal ranges makes the application-level checks a defence in depth rather than the whole defence.

Exercise 6.3 — Set the numbers. For a public JSON API serving a bulk-import endpoint, choose concrete values for the nine limits in §6.4 and say what status code each violation returns. Then name the one limit that is not enforceable at the edge and explain why.

Solution
Limit Value On violation
Body size 1 MB normal, 10 MB on /imports 413, with the limit in the problem body
JSON depth 32 400 — it is a parse-level refusal
Array length 500 items per request 422, naming the field and the limit
String length per field: 80 for a name, 4 KB for a note 422, with the field path
Page size default 20, max 100 422, or clamp to 100 — but document which
Query complexity n/a for REST; depth 10 / complexity 1000 for GraphQL 422
Upload size 5 GB, via presigned URL so it never touches you (§4.7) rejected by storage
Request timeout 30 s at the edge, 25 s in the app 504 from the edge, 503 from the app
Concurrency per client 20 in flight 429 with Retry-After

The limit that cannot live at the edge is array length — and more generally any limit expressed in units of your domain rather than units of bytes. A gateway can count bytes and it can time a connection, but it cannot know that 500 line items is your ceiling, that this particular tenant is allowed 2,000, or that ids is more expensive per element than tags. Judging that requires parsing the body against your schema, which is the application's job.

The same is true of page size and query complexity: both are semantic. Which is the general shape of the split — the edge enforces limits on the shape of the traffic, the application enforces limits on the meaning of it — and it is why both layers need limits rather than one delegating to the other.

Exercise 6.4 — Strict or lax? For each boundary, say whether pydantic should run in strict mode or lax mode, and why.

  1. A JSON request body from a browser form.
  2. A response from your payment provider's API.
  3. A message consumed from your own internal queue.
  4. Query-string parameters.
  5. A configuration file loaded at startup.
Solution
  1. Lax. Everything from a form arrives as text — "3", "true", "2026-03-04" — and coercing to int, bool and date is exactly what you want. Rejecting "3" for an int field would make the API hostile for no security gain, since the value is still range-checked afterwards.
  2. Strict. The provider's contract says rate is a number. If it arrives as a string, something changed in their release, and you want to hear about it now rather than have it silently coerced for six months until a value stops being coercible. This is the drifted line in §6.6.
  3. Strict. You control both ends and the encoding is JSON with known types. A type change here is a producer/consumer version skew, and skew that fails loudly is skew you can fix (§14.4).
  4. Lax, necessarily. Query strings are text by definition; ?limit=20 cannot be anything but a string on the wire. Strict mode would reject every request. Compensate with tight Field constraints instead.
  5. Strict, and validated at startup. A typo in a config file should stop the process from starting, not surface as a wrong timeout under load three days later. "30" where you meant 30 is precisely the kind of thing lax mode hides.

The pattern: lax where the wire format has no types, strict where it does. HTTP request bodies from browsers and query strings are the first case; machine-to-machine JSON, internal messages and config files are the second.

SECTION 7

Pagination, Filtering and Sorting

Every collection endpoint is a promise about a set that is changing while the client reads it. Handle that badly and clients silently miss records, see duplicates, or take your database down with ?limit=1000000. This section is about the three parts of a list endpoint that always need designing — how the client walks the set, how it narrows it, and how it orders it — and about the specific failure that makes offset pagination unsuitable for anything that changes.

7.1 Offset Pagination, and Why It Breaks

Offset pagination is the obvious design and the one everyone starts with: ?limit=20&offset=40, translated straight into SQL as LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40. It is easy to implement, it supports jumping to page 7, and it is correct only when nothing is being inserted or deleted.

Two problems, and the first one is the serious one.

Rows are skipped and duplicated when the set changes underneath the client. Offset counts positions, not records. Insert something before the client's current position and every subsequent row shifts by one — so one record moves from page 2 to page 3 and is read twice, or moves from page 3 to page 2 and is never read at all.

def page_by_offset(rows: list[str], limit: int, offset: int) -> list[str]:
    return rows[offset:offset + limit]

def fresh() -> list[str]:
    return [f"order-{n}" for n in range(10, 0, -1)]     # newest first

def walk(rows, mutate) -> tuple[list[str], list[str]]:
    """Read two pages of three, with `mutate` applied between the two requests."""
