    page1 = page_by_offset(rows, limit=3, offset=0)
    mutate(rows)
    page2 = page_by_offset(rows, limit=3, offset=3)
    return page1, page2

def insert_one(rows: list[str]) -> None:
    rows.insert(0, "order-11")                          # someone creates an order

def delete_one(rows: list[str]) -> None:
    rows.remove("order-10")                             # someone deletes one

rows = fresh()
p1, p2 = walk(rows, insert_one)
seen = p1 + p2
print("insert  page1:", p1, "page2:", p2)
print("        duplicated:", sorted({r for r in seen if seen.count(r) > 1}))

rows = fresh()
p1, p2 = walk(rows, delete_one)
seen = p1 + p2
print("delete  page1:", p1, "page2:", p2)
print("        skipped:   ", [r for r in fresh()[:6] if r not in seen])

insert  page1: ['order-10', 'order-9', 'order-8'] page2: ['order-8', 'order-7', 'order-6']
        duplicated: ['order-8']
delete  page1: ['order-10', 'order-9', 'order-8'] page2: ['order-6', 'order-5', 'order-4']
        skipped:    ['order-7']

One insert, and order-8 appears on both pages: a client reading a two-page list has processed one order twice. One delete, and order-7 appears on neither: a client synchronising your data never learns that record exists. In both cases there is no error, no warning, and nothing in the response that would let the client detect it — the pages look perfectly well-formed.

The severity depends on what the client is doing. For a human scrolling a list, a repeated row is a shrug. For a client mirroring your orders into its own system, a skipped record is a permanent, silent divergence that will be discovered weeks later as a totals mismatch (Exercise 7.2).

Deep offsets are slow. OFFSET 100000 requires the database to produce and discard 100,000 rows. The cost grows linearly with page number, so the last page of a large collection is the most expensive request your API serves — which is exactly the request a scraper makes.

Offset pagination is nonetheless the right answer in two cases: when the underlying set is genuinely static (a finished report, an archived export), and when the interface truly requires numbered pages that users can jump between. If you use it, cap the offset — offset > 10000 returns 422 — because an uncapped one is a denial-of-service parameter.

7.2 Cursor Pagination

Cursor pagination — also called keyset or seek pagination — replaces "skip 40 rows" with "give me what comes after this record". The page token encodes the sort key of the last row returned, and the query becomes a range scan.

-- offset: read and discard 40 rows
SELECT * FROM orders ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40;

-- keyset: seek straight to the position, using the index
SELECT * FROM orders
WHERE (created_at, id) < ('2026-03-04T10:02:00Z', 'ord_9f4a')
ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC
LIMIT 20;

The second query costs the same on page 500 as on page 1, because the index takes it directly to the starting point. And an insert elsewhere in the set cannot shift anything, because the position is defined by a value rather than by a count.

The API shape:

{
  "items": [{"id": "ord_9f4a", "createdAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"}],
  "nextPageToken": "eyJjcmVhdGVkQXQiOiIyMDI2LTAzLTA0VDEwOjAyOjAwWiIsImlkIjoib3JkXzlmNGEifQ"
}

Four rules for tokens:

  • Opaque, always. Base64 of a JSON object is fine — the point is that clients treat it as a string they hand back, not as something to construct or inspect. The moment a client parses your token, its internals are part of your contract (§14.4).
  • Sign or encrypt them if they contain anything sensitive. A token embedding a tenant ID or a filter that a client could tamper with is an authorization bypass. HMAC it, or store the cursor server-side and hand out a random key.
  • Include the sort and the filter in the token, and reject a token whose parameters do not match the request's. Otherwise a client can page through one ordering with a token minted for another and get nonsense.
  • Expire them. A token is a position in a result set; a month-old one may point into data that has been deleted, re-sorted or re-permissioned.

Here is the mechanism in full, including the token round-trip:

import base64
import json

ORDERS = [{"id": f"ord_{n:02d}", "created": f"2026-03-{n:02d}"} for n in range(1, 13)]
ORDERS.sort(key=lambda o: (o["created"], o["id"]), reverse=True)   # newest first

def encode(cursor: dict) -> str:
    raw = json.dumps(cursor, separators=(",", ":")).encode()
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(raw).decode().rstrip("=")

def decode(token: str) -> dict:
    padded = token + "=" * (-len(token) % 4)
    return json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(padded))

def list_orders(limit: int = 3, page_token: str | None = None) -> dict:
    after = decode(page_token) if page_token else None
    rows = ORDERS
    if after is not None:
        key = (after["created"], after["id"])
        rows = [o for o in rows if (o["created"], o["id"]) < key]    # the seek
    window = rows[:limit + 1]                       # one extra: is there a next page?
    items, more = window[:limit], len(window) > limit
    token = encode({"created": items[-1]["created"], "id": items[-1]["id"]}) if more else None
    return {"items": [o["id"] for o in items], "nextPageToken": token}

page = list_orders(limit=5)
print("page 1:", page["items"])
ORDERS.insert(0, {"id": "ord_99", "created": "2026-03-31"})   # concurrent insert
page = list_orders(limit=5, page_token=page["nextPageToken"])
print("page 2:", page["items"])
page = list_orders(limit=5, page_token=page["nextPageToken"])
print("page 3:", page["items"], "next:", page["nextPageToken"])

page 1: ['ord_12', 'ord_11', 'ord_10', 'ord_09', 'ord_08']
page 2: ['ord_07', 'ord_06', 'ord_05', 'ord_04', 'ord_03']
page 3: ['ord_02', 'ord_01'] next: None

Twelve orders, twelve results, no duplicates and no gaps — despite an insert between pages 1 and 2, which is exactly the operation that broke §7.1. The new record does not appear, because it sorts before the client's cursor; that is correct behaviour for a snapshot walk, and clients that need to catch new records poll page 1 again rather than continuing the walk.

Two implementation details in that code are worth stealing. Fetch limit + 1 rows to determine whether a next page exists, rather than running a second count query. And emit nextPageToken: null on the last page, so the client's loop terminates on a value rather than on an empty items array — which saves it one wasted round trip.

7.3 Ordering Must Be Total

Both pagination schemes depend on a sorting order that is total: every row must have a distinct position. If two rows can compare equal, the database is free to return them in either order on any given query, and a cursor sitting between them can skip or repeat records.

ORDER BY created_at DESC                      -- WRONG: timestamps collide
ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC             -- correct: id breaks every tie

Timestamps collide far more often than people expect — a bulk import inserts thousands of rows in the same millisecond — and the resulting bug is intermittent, data-dependent and nearly impossible to reproduce. Always append a unique tie-breaker, normally the primary key, and make sure your cursor comparison uses the same composite key.

Two related rules:

The index must match the sort. ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC with a LIMIT is fast only if there is an index on (created_at DESC, id DESC), or an index the planner can walk backwards. Otherwise the database sorts the whole table for every page.

Allowed sort fields are an allowlist. ?sort= maps user input to a column name, which is a direct injection path and also a performance one — sorting by an unindexed column is a full scan. Define the permitted set explicitly:

SORTABLE = {"createdAt": "created_at", "total": "total_cents", "status": "status"}

def order_clause(sort: str) -> str:
    field, _, direction = sort.partition(":")
    column = SORTABLE.get(field)
    if column is None:
        raise ValueError(f"cannot sort by {field!r}; allowed: {sorted(SORTABLE)}")
    if direction not in ("", "asc", "desc"):
        raise ValueError(f"bad direction {direction!r}")
    return f"{column} {(direction or 'asc').upper()}, id {(direction or 'asc').upper()}"

print(order_clause("createdAt:desc"))
print(order_clause("total"))
try:
    order_clause("password_hash:asc")
except ValueError as exc:
    print("rejected:", exc)

created_at DESC, id DESC
total_cents ASC, id ASC
rejected: cannot sort by 'password_hash'; allowed: ['createdAt', 'status', 'total']

Note that the tie-breaker is appended automatically, so a caller cannot produce a non-total ordering even by accident.

7.4 Totals, and the Honest Alternatives

Clients want "total": 4183 so they can render "Page 3 of 210". It is a more expensive request than it looks: SELECT COUNT(*) with the same WHERE clause is a full scan of the matching set on most databases, and it runs on every page request, often costing more than the page itself.

Four options, in the order to consider them:

Option Cost When
Do not return a total free Cursor pagination with infinite scroll — the common case
hasMore: true free (you fetched limit+1 anyway) Any cursor API. Enough for "Load more"
An estimate cheap (pg_class.reltuples, or a cached count) Dashboards, "about 4,200 results"
An exact count a full scan per request Small or heavily-filtered sets, or when a user genuinely needs it

If you must offer an exact total, make it opt-in — ?includeTotal=true — so the expensive query runs only when someone asked for it, rather than on every page of every listing. And be clear that a total taken alongside a page is already slightly stale: it describes the set at the moment of the count, not the set the client is walking.

7.5 Filtering

Filtering is where list endpoints accrete complexity fastest, and where it is worth being deliberately conservative early. Four levels, in increasing order of power and cost:

1. Named parameters. ?status=open¤cy=GBP. Trivial to validate, trivial to index, self-documenting in OpenAPI. Repeat a parameter for OR: ?status=open&status=paid.
2. Operator suffixes. ?createdAt[gte]=2026-01-01&total[lt]=5000. Covers ranges without a grammar. Still a fixed field set.
3. A filter expression language. ?filter=status="open" AND total>5000 (AIP-160). Powerful, and now you own a parser, a planner and a cost model.
4. Arbitrary query objects in a POST body. Maximum power, no caching, and effectively a database console on the internet unless you are extremely careful.

Start at level 1 and move up only when a real client need forces it. Whichever level you pick, four rules hold:

  • Every filterable field is an allowlist entry, mapped to a column by a dictionary, never by string interpolation.
  • Every filterable field is indexed, or documented as slow and rate-limited harder. An unindexed filter is a full scan a client can trigger at will.
  • Filters are part of the cursor. Changing the filter invalidates the token (§7.2).
  • Filtering happens after the authorization scope, never instead of it. ?tenantId=other-corp must be impossible, because the tenant filter is applied by the server from the token, not from the query string (§9.5).

A useful convention for the common case: treat repeated parameters as OR within a field and AND across fields. ?status=open&status=paid&currency=GBP means (open or paid) and GBP, which covers most real queries without any grammar at all.

7.6 Soft Delete and Visibility

Soft delete — marking a row deleted_at rather than removing it — is the normal choice for anything a user might want restored, anything referenced by other records, and anything an audit requires you to keep. It has consequences for every list endpoint, and they are usually discovered by accident.

Decide these five things explicitly, and document them:

  • GET of a soft-deleted resource returns what? 404 is the usual answer, and 410 Gone is better when the client should stop asking. Returning 200 with "deleted": true is defensible for an admin API and wrong for a public one.
  • DELETE of an already-deleted resource? 204 (idempotent) or 404 (informative). Pick one; §2.2's note applies.
  • Do list endpoints exclude them? Yes, by default, always. An ?includeDeleted=true parameter is fine for admin callers, and it must be authorized.
  • Do unique constraints still apply? This is the one that bites. A soft-deleted user with email = ada@example.com still occupies the unique index, so the address can never be reused. The fix is a partial index — CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ... WHERE deleted_at IS NULL — and it needs to be decided at the same time as the soft delete, not after the first support ticket.
  • What eventually happens to the row? "Soft delete" with no expiry policy is "never delete", which is a data-protection problem: a user exercising a right to erasure needs the record actually gone. Define a retention period and a job that enforces it.

The most common bug is the one hiding in the fourth point's neighbour: forgetting the deleted_at IS NULL predicate in one query out of forty. The structural fix is to make the default query path exclude them — a repository method, a database view, or a query-builder scope — so that including deleted rows is the thing you have to ask for.

7.7 Making a Walk Consistent

Cursor pagination gives a client a stable position; it does not give it a consistent snapshot. Records modified while the walk is in progress are seen in their new state, and a record whose sort key changes can move across the cursor — appearing twice, or not at all. For a UI listing, nobody cares. For a client synchronising your data into its own store, it matters a great deal.

Three approaches, increasing in strength and cost:

Sort by an immutable key. If the walk orders by (created_at, id) and those never change, no record can move relative to the cursor. Deletions still remove records mid-walk, and updates are still seen late, but nothing is skipped. This is cheap and covers most cases.

Offer a change feed instead. For synchronisation, the right primitive is not a list — it is a monotonically increasing sequence: GET /orders/changes?since=<token>, returning creations, updates and deletions in commit order, including tombstones for deletes. The client's position is durable, resumable and exact, and it never needs to re-walk the collection. If clients are building mirrors of your data, build this rather than making them abuse your list endpoint.

Give the walk a snapshot. Some databases can pin a read view for a bounded period (a repeatable-read transaction, or PostgreSQL's exported snapshots), so every page sees the set as it was at the start. It is genuinely consistent and genuinely expensive — a long-lived transaction blocks vacuuming and holds resources — so it suits a bounded export job, not a public listing.

Whatever you choose, say which one it is in your documentation. A client synchronising records has to know whether a walk can miss things; if you do not tell them, they will assume it cannot, and be wrong.

Exercises

Exercise 7.1 — Design the list endpoint. Specify GET /orders completely for a multi-tenant system with 40 million orders: parameters, defaults, limits, response shape, sort, and the index it needs. Justify each choice in a phrase.

Solution 
GET /orders?status=open&status=paid
           &createdAt[gte]=2026-01-01
           &sort=createdAt:desc
           &limit=20
           &pageToken=eyJjcmVhdGVkQXQiOiIyMDI2LTAzLTA0VDEwOjAyOjAwWiIsImlkIjoib3JkXzlmNGEifQ
Element Choice Why
Tenant not a parameter — taken from the token A tenant filter a client can set is a cross-tenant read (§9.5)
limit default 20, max 100, 422 above An uncapped page size is a DoS parameter (§6.4)
pageToken opaque, signed, encodes sort + filters + position Keyset, so page 2000 costs the same as page 1
sort allowlist of 3 fields, tie-broken by id Injection and full-scan protection; total order (§7.3)
status repeatable = OR Covers the common query with no grammar
createdAt[gte] operator suffix Ranges without owning a parser
Totals omitted; nextPageToken: null ends the walk A count over 40M rows per request is unaffordable
Deleted excluded by default §7.6 
{
  "items": [{"id": "ord_9f4a", "status": "open", "createdAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"}],
  "nextPageToken": "eyJjcmVhdGVkQXQiOiIyMDI2..."
}

The index: CREATE INDEX ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC, id DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

tenant_id leads because it is in every query. (created_at, id) matches the sort exactly, so the keyset predicate is a seek rather than a sort. The partial clause keeps soft-deleted rows out of the index entirely, which makes it smaller and removes the risk of a query that forgets the predicate. Filtering by status as well would want it in the index too — but adding it before created_at breaks the sort, so the usual answer is a second index on (tenant_id, status, created_at DESC, id DESC) and letting the planner choose.

Exercise 7.2 — Explain the missing records. A client synchronises your orders nightly by walking GET /orders?sort=updatedAt:desc with offset pagination. Their totals never match yours, and the discrepancy is always small. Explain precisely what is happening, and give the fix.

Solution

Two compounding faults, either of which alone would cause it.

Offset over a changing set (§7.1). Any order updated during the walk moves to the front of an updatedAt DESC ordering, shifting everything behind it by one. A record that was at position 41 becomes position 42 and is read twice, or a record ahead of the cursor is pushed behind it and is never read. Records that appear later in the sort than the cursor and get updated are pushed past it in the opposite direction — vanishing from the walk entirely. That is why the discrepancy is small and always different: it is proportional to the write rate during the walk.

Sorting by a mutable key (§7.7). updatedAt is precisely the field the walk mutates the meaning of. Even with a perfect cursor, a record whose sort key changes mid-walk moves relative to the cursor.

The fix, in order of preference:

  1. Give them a change feed. GET /orders/changes?since=<token> in commit order, with tombstones for deletes. This is what they are actually trying to do, and it is exact, resumable, and cheap for both sides. Everything else is a workaround.
  2. If it must be a list walk, sort by an immutable total key — (createdAt, id) — and use cursor pagination. New records appear at the front and are picked up by the next run; nothing already in the set can move.
  3. Do not tell them to increase the page size. It reduces the number of windows in which the race can occur and does not remove it, so the bug becomes rarer and harder to diagnose.

Also worth saying explicitly in the documentation: a list walk is not a snapshot (§7.7). This client assumed it was, which is a reasonable assumption when nobody says otherwise.

Exercise 7.3 — The soft delete that blocked re-registration. Users who delete their account cannot sign up again with the same email; they get 409 Conflict. Diagnose it, and give the fix — including what to do about the existing rows.

Solution

Diagnosis. users.email carries a plain UNIQUE constraint, and soft delete leaves the row in the table. The deleted row still occupies the index entry, so the address is reserved forever. The signup handler correctly reports a duplicate; the constraint is simply asking the wrong question, because it does not know about deleted_at.

The fix is a partial unique index, so only live rows compete:

ALTER TABLE users DROP CONSTRAINT users_email_key;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX users_email_live
    ON users (lower(email)) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;

lower(email) is worth adding at the same time — email addresses are case-insensitive in practice, and Ada@example.com versus ada@example.com is the same bug wearing a different hat.

The existing rows are the awkward part, and there are two cases. Where a soft-deleted row is the only holder of an address, the new index will simply ignore it and the address becomes available — nothing to do. Where a soft-deleted row and a live row share an address, the index still builds, because only the live one is indexed. The real risk is the reverse: two live rows sharing an address, created while some other bug was live. CREATE UNIQUE INDEX will fail on those, so find them first:

SELECT lower(email), count(*) FROM users
WHERE deleted_at IS NULL GROUP BY 1 HAVING count(*) > 1;

Resolve those by hand before the migration, and use CREATE UNIQUE INDEX CONCURRENTLY on a live table so the build does not hold a write lock.

One more decision this forces. If a user can re-register with the same address, what happens to the old account's data? Re-linking it silently is a privacy failure — the address may have changed owner. The usual answer is that re-registration creates a genuinely new account, and the old one is anonymised or purged on the retention schedule §7.6 says you must have.

Exercise 7.4 — Bound the filter surface. A client asks for arbitrary filtering on any field, with AND, OR and NOT. Give the counter-proposal, and name the three costs of saying yes.

Solution

The counter-proposal. Start at §7.5 level 2 — a fixed allowlist of filterable fields with operator suffixes ([gte], [lte], [in]), repeated parameters for OR within a field, AND across fields. That covers the large majority of real queries. Ask the client for the ten queries they actually need; in practice they name four, and three are already supported. If a genuine case remains that this cannot express, add a named endpoint or a saved-query resource for it (§3.7) — which you can index and cost deliberately — rather than a general grammar.

The three costs of saying yes:

  1. You now own a query planner. Arbitrary boolean expressions over arbitrary fields means arbitrary execution plans, including ones that are full scans, ones that use no index, and ones whose cost depends on data the client cannot see. You will need a complexity limiter, a statement timeout, and a way to explain to a client why their query is the slow one.
  2. You cannot index for it. Indexes are built for known access patterns. An open grammar has no known access patterns, so either you index everything — paying on every write, and multiplying storage — or some filters are silently slow, which is worse because it is unpredictable.
  3. The contract becomes unbounded. Every field the grammar can reach is now public, so a column you add for internal reasons is immediately filterable, and a column you rename is a breaking change (§14.4). Field-level authorization also becomes far harder: ?filter=internalRiskScore>50 must be refused, and refusing it without confirming the field exists is fiddly.

There is a fourth cost worth mentioning to the client rather than to yourself: filter expressions make responses effectively uncacheable, because the key space is unbounded (§10.7). If their motivation was latency, a grammar may make it worse.

SECTION 8

Authentication

Authentication is the question who is calling? — and nothing else. It is not what they may do, which is §9, and conflating the two is the origin of a large fraction of API security incidents. This section covers the credentials an API actually accepts, the flows that issue them, the structure of the tokens they produce, and the specific ways each is got wrong. OWASP ranks broken authentication second on its API Security Top 10 (API2:2023) for a reason: the mechanisms are standardised, well documented, and still routinely assembled incorrectly.

8.1 Sessions, Tokens, and Choosing Between Them

Two families of credential, and the choice has consequences all the way down.

Opaque session identifier. A random string in a cookie or header; the server looks it up in a store on every request. State lives on the server. Revocation is a DELETE. The cost is a store lookup per request and a store that must be highly available.
Self-contained token. A signed document carrying the claims; the server verifies the signature and reads them. No lookup. The cost is that a token is valid until it expires — revocation is genuinely hard (§8.7).

The decision usually comes down to one question: can you afford a lookup per request? If your identity store is in the same datacentre and answers in a millisecond, opaque sessions are simpler and strictly safer. If you are verifying tokens across service boundaries, in edge functions, or in a system where the issuer is a separate provider, self-contained tokens earn their complexity.

Where the credential travels matters as much as its form:

  • Cookies are automatically attached by browsers, which is convenient and is also what makes CSRF possible. Use them with HttpOnly (no JavaScript access), Secure (HTTPS only), SameSite=Lax or Strict, and a CSRF token for state-changing requests if you are on Lax.
  • The Authorization header is not sent automatically, so CSRF does not apply. It must be attached by the client, which means the token has to be stored somewhere JavaScript can reach — and therefore somewhere XSS can reach.
  • Never in the query string. URLs land in access logs, browser history, Referer headers and error trackers. OAuth 2.1 explicitly forbids bearer tokens in query strings for exactly this reason.

The honest summary for a browser-facing API: an HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookie holding an opaque session ID is the safest default, and the pattern of "store the JWT in localStorage" is popular mainly because it is easy, not because it is good — anything that can run script in your origin can exfiltrate it.

8.2 API Keys and Machine-to-Machine Callers

An API key is a long random string identifying an application rather than a user. It is the right primitive for server-to-server callers: no user is present, no consent is needed, and nothing about OAuth's user-delegation machinery applies.

Doing it properly takes six decisions:

  • Generate with a CSPRNG, 256 bits. secrets.token_urlsafe(32). Not a UUID (structured, and v1 encodes a timestamp and MAC address), not a hash of anything predictable.
  • Prefix it. sk_live_..., sk_test_... — the prefix tells a human what they are holding, lets you scan public repositories for leaked keys, and makes an environment mix-up obvious rather than mysterious.
  • Store a hash, not the key. A key is a password. If your database is read, hashed keys are useless to the reader. Because a key is high-entropy and verified on every request, a fast hash (SHA-256) is appropriate here — the slow-KDF argument for user passwords is about guessability, which does not apply to 256 random bits.
  • Show it once. At creation, and never again. "Reveal key" in a dashboard means it is stored recoverably somewhere.
  • Make them plural, scoped and rotatable — the §3.5 lesson. Multiple live keys per account is what makes rotation possible without downtime.
  • Record last-used-at. It is the only way to answer "can we safely revoke this?", and it turns key hygiene from guesswork into a query.
import hashlib
import hmac
import secrets

def issue_key(environment: str = "live") -> tuple[str, str]:
    """Return (key shown once to the user, digest stored in the database)."""
    key = f"sk_{environment}_{secrets.token_urlsafe(32)}"
    return key, hashlib.sha256(key.encode()).hexdigest()

def verify(presented: str, stored_digest: str) -> bool:
    digest = hashlib.sha256(presented.encode()).hexdigest()
    return hmac.compare_digest(digest, stored_digest)     # constant time

key, digest = issue_key()
print("prefix     :", key.startswith("sk_live_"), "| key length:", len(key))
print("stored     :", len(digest), "hex chars — the key itself is never stored")
print("correct key:", verify(key, digest))
print("wrong key  :", verify("sk_live_" + "A" * 43, digest))
print("test key   :", issue_key("test")[0].startswith("sk_test_"))

prefix     : True | key length: 51
stored     : 64 hex chars — the key itself is never stored
correct key: True
wrong key  : False
test key   : True

hmac.compare_digest rather than == is not superstition: string comparison exits at the first differing byte, and that timing difference is measurable over enough requests. It costs nothing to use the constant-time comparison everywhere a secret is checked.

8.3 OAuth 2.1: The Roles and the Grants

OAuth is a delegation protocol. Its purpose is to let a user authorise an application to access an API on their behalf, without the application ever seeing the user's password. It is not a login protocol — that is OpenID Connect (§8.5) — and treating it as one is the single most common OAuth mistake.

Four roles, and naming them correctly makes the flows legible:

Role Who it is
Resource owner the user who owns the data
Client the application requesting access
Authorization server issues tokens after authenticating the user and getting consent
Resource server your API, which accepts the tokens

OAuth 2.1 consolidates RFC 6749 with a decade of security best-current-practice. As of draft-15 (March 2026) it is still an Internet-Draft rather than an RFC, but the substance is settled and major providers already enforce it. Four changes matter:

  • PKCE is required for every client using the authorization code flow, public or confidential.
  • The implicit grant is removed. Returning tokens in a URL fragment was always a leak; the authorization code flow with PKCE replaces it for every client type, including single-page apps.
  • The resource owner password credentials grant is removed. Handing an application your username and password defeats the entire point of delegation.
  • Redirect URIs must match by exact string comparison, not by prefix or wildcard — a rule that closes a family of open-redirect token thefts.

Which leaves two grants you will actually use:

Authorization code with PKCE — for anything acting on behalf of a user. Web apps, mobile apps, single-page apps, CLI tools.

Client credentials — for a service acting as itself. No user, no redirect, no consent: the client presents its ID and secret and gets a token. This is the OAuth-shaped alternative to an API key, and it is worth preferring when you already run an authorization server, because it gives you short-lived tokens and scopes for free.

8.4 The Authorization Code Flow, and What PKCE Fixes

1. Client generates a random code_verifier, derives code_challenge = BASE64URL(SHA256(verifier)), and redirects the user to the authorization server with the challenge, its client_id, a redirect_uri, the scope, and a random state.
2. User authenticates at the authorization server and consents. The client never sees the password.
3. The server redirects back to redirect_uri with a short-lived, single-use code and the same state. The client checks state matches what it sent — this is the CSRF defence.
4. Client POSTs the code to the token endpoint with the original code_verifier. The server recomputes the challenge and compares. Match → access token (and refresh token).

PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange, RFC 7636) exists because step 3 delivers the code over a channel the client does not fully control — a redirect, which on mobile passes through the operating system's URL handling, and which malware or a malicious app claiming the same custom scheme can intercept. Without PKCE, whoever holds the code can redeem it. With PKCE, redeeming it also requires the verifier, which never left the client that started the flow.

import base64
import hashlib
import secrets

def pkce_pair() -> tuple[str, str]:
    """RFC 7636 §4.1–4.2: a 43–128 char verifier, and its S256 challenge."""
    verifier = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(secrets.token_bytes(32)).decode().rstrip("=")
    digest = hashlib.sha256(verifier.encode("ascii")).digest()
    challenge = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(digest).decode().rstrip("=")
    return verifier, challenge

def server_check(stored_challenge: str, presented_verifier: str) -> bool:
    digest = hashlib.sha256(presented_verifier.encode("ascii")).digest()
    return secrets.compare_digest(
        base64.urlsafe_b64encode(digest).decode().rstrip("="), stored_challenge)

verifier, challenge = pkce_pair()
print("verifier length :", len(verifier), "(RFC 7636 requires 43–128)")
print("challenge length:", len(challenge))
print("padding stripped:", "=" not in verifier and "=" not in challenge)
print("legitimate redemption:", server_check(challenge, verifier))
stolen_code_only, _ = pkce_pair()          # an attacker who intercepted the code
print("attacker's guess     :", server_check(challenge, stolen_code_only))

verifier length : 43 (RFC 7636 requires 43–128)
challenge length: 43
padding stripped: True
legitimate redemption: True
attacker's guess     : False

Three details that are specified and routinely got wrong: the encoding is base64url without padding (= stripped, +// replaced by -/_); the method must be S256, never plain, which offers no protection at all when the challenge travels the same channel as the code; and state is a separate parameter from PKCE — it defends against CSRF on the redirect, and PKCE defends against code interception. You need both.

8.5 OpenID Connect

OAuth answers "may this application access that data?". It does not answer "who is this person?", and the access token is deliberately opaque to the client — it is a credential for the resource server, not information for the client. Applications that inspect an access token to learn the user's identity are doing something the specification never promised would work.

OpenID Connect is the identity layer built on top. It adds:

  • An id_token: a JWT about the user, signed by the provider, with standard claims — sub (the stable user identifier), iss, aud, exp, iat, nonce, plus optional email, name, picture.
  • The openid scope, which is what requests it.
  • A /userinfo endpoint for claims too large or too fresh for the token.
  • Discovery, at /.well-known/openid-configuration, publishing the endpoints and the JWKS URL where the signing keys live.

Four rules for consuming an id_token, each of which is a real vulnerability when skipped:

  • Verify the signature against the provider's JWKS, fetched over HTTPS and cached by kid.
  • Check iss and aud. An id_token issued for a different application is a valid, correctly-signed token — accepting it lets any app registered with the same provider log in as your users.
  • Check exp, and nonce against the value you sent. The nonce binds the token to your flow.
  • Use sub, not email, as the identifier. Email addresses change hands. sub is stable and is what the provider guarantees.

The practical distinction to hold: id_token is for your application, to learn who logged in. The access token is for the API, and your application should treat it as an opaque string.

8.6 JWTs, and How They Go Wrong

A JWT is three base64url segments separated by dots: header, payload, signature. It is not encrypted — the payload is readable by anyone holding the token. It is signed, which means tamper-evident, not confidential.

eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9
header — {"alg":"HS256","typ":"JWT"}
.
eyJzdWIiOiJ1MSIsImV4cCI6MTc3MDAwMDAwMH0
payload — the claims, readable by anyone
.
4pQ9…
signature — over header.payload

A bearer token is any credential where possession is sufficient — "bearer" meaning the holder. JWTs are almost always used as bearer tokens, sent as Authorization: Bearer <token>, which is why the leakage rules in §8.1 matter so much: there is no second factor, no proof of possession, nothing tying the token to the client that was issued it.

Verification, written out, is the clearest way to see what the traps are:

import base64
import hashlib
import hmac
import json
import time

SECRET = b"a-256-bit-secret-from-your-key-management-system"

def b64(raw: bytes) -> str:
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(raw).decode().rstrip("=")

def unb64(segment: str) -> bytes:
    return base64.urlsafe_b64decode(segment + "=" * (-len(segment) % 4))

def sign(payload: dict) -> str:
    header = b64(json.dumps({"alg": "HS256", "typ": "JWT"}, separators=(",", ":")).encode())
    body = b64(json.dumps(payload, separators=(",", ":")).encode())
    signing_input = f"{header}.{body}".encode()
    signature = b64(hmac.new(SECRET, signing_input, hashlib.sha256).digest())
    return f"{header}.{body}.{signature}"

def verify(token: str, *, audience: str, issuer: str, now: int) -> dict:
    header_b64, body_b64, sig_b64 = token.split(".")
    header = json.loads(unb64(header_b64))
    if header.get("alg") != "HS256":            # 1. never trust the header's alg
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected alg {header.get('alg')!r}")
    expected = b64(hmac.new(SECRET, f"{header_b64}.{body_b64}".encode(),
                            hashlib.sha256).digest())
    if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, sig_b64):
        raise ValueError("bad signature")
    claims = json.loads(unb64(body_b64))
    if claims.get("iss") != issuer:             # 2. who issued it
        raise ValueError("wrong issuer")
    if claims.get("aud") != audience:           # 3. who it was issued FOR
        raise ValueError("wrong audience")
    if now >= claims.get("exp", 0):             # 4. is it still valid
        raise ValueError("expired")
    return claims

issued_at = 1_770_000_000
token = sign({"sub": "u1", "iss": "https://auth.example.com", "aud": "orders-api",
              "exp": issued_at + 900, "scope": "orders:read"})

print("claims are public:", json.loads(unb64(token.split(".")[1])))
print("valid  :", verify(token, audience="orders-api",
                         issuer="https://auth.example.com", now=issued_at + 10)["sub"])
for label, kwargs in (
        ("wrong audience", {"audience": "billing-api", "issuer": "https://auth.example.com",
                            "now": issued_at + 10}),
        ("expired       ", {"audience": "orders-api", "issuer": "https://auth.example.com",
                            "now": issued_at + 1000})):
    try:
        verify(token, **kwargs)
    except ValueError as exc:
        print(f"{label}: rejected — {exc}")

tampered = token.split(".")
tampered[1] = b64(json.dumps({"sub": "admin", "iss": "https://auth.example.com",
                              "aud": "orders-api", "exp": issued_at + 900},
                             separators=(",", ":")).encode())
try:
    verify(".".join(tampered), audience="orders-api",
           issuer="https://auth.example.com", now=issued_at + 10)
except ValueError as exc:
    print("tampered      : rejected —", exc)

claims are public: {'sub': 'u1', 'iss': 'https://auth.example.com', 'aud': 'orders-api', 'exp': 1770000900, 'scope': 'orders:read'}
valid  : u1
wrong audience: rejected — wrong audience
expired       : rejected — expired
tampered      : rejected — bad signature

The first line is the point most often missed: the claims are public. Never put anything in a JWT you would not print in a log.

The seven traps, in the order they appear in real incidents:

  1. alg: none. The original specification permitted an unsigned JWT, and several libraries accepted one. Pin the algorithm in your verifier — as above — and never read it from the token's own header.
  2. Algorithm confusion. A verifier that accepts both HS256 and RS256 and picks based on the header can be handed an HS256 token signed with the public RSA key as the HMAC secret. Public keys are public. Pin one algorithm.
  3. Not checking aud. A correctly-signed token for a different service is still correctly signed.
  4. Not checking exp — or checking it with a sloppy clock. Allow at most a small leeway (60 seconds) for skew.
  5. Long lifetimes. A JWT cannot be revoked, so its lifetime is your worst-case exposure window. Fifteen minutes is a common, defensible access-token lifetime.
  6. Putting secrets in the payload. See the first line of the output.
  7. A weak HMAC secret. HS256 with a guessable secret is offline-crackable. Use 256 bits of randomness, or use asymmetric signing so the verifier holds no signing capability at all.

In production, use a maintained library — PyJWT, jose, your provider's SDK — with the algorithm pinned and audience and issuer configured. The code above is written out to make the checks visible, not to be copied.

8.7 Refresh Tokens, Rotation and Revocation

Short access-token lifetimes create a problem: the user should not re-authenticate every fifteen minutes. A refresh token solves it — a long-lived credential, presented only to the authorization server, exchanged for a new access token.

That makes it the highest-value credential in the system, and it needs three protections:

Rotation. Every refresh returns a new refresh token and invalidates the old one. A stolen token is then usable at most once.

Reuse detection. If an already-used refresh token is presented again, one of two things happened: the legitimate client's response was lost, or a token was stolen and one of the two parties is now using a superseded token. You cannot tell which — so treat it as theft and revoke the entire token family, forcing re-authentication. This is the standard recommendation in the OAuth security BCP, and it converts a silent, indefinite compromise into a single failed login.

Binding, where you can. Sender-constrained tokens (DPoP, RFC 9449, or mTLS) tie a token to a key the client holds, so possession alone is no longer sufficient. This is the real answer to bearer-token theft, and it is increasingly available from providers.

Revocation is where self-contained tokens hurt, and it is worth being precise about the options rather than pretending there is a clean one:

Approach Revocation latency Cost
Short access-token lifetime only up to the lifetime (15 min) none — this is the default
Deny-list of revoked token IDs (jti) immediate a lookup per request — you have re-invented sessions, but only for revoked tokens, so the list stays small
Version counter per user, checked per request immediate a lookup per request
Opaque tokens with introspection (RFC 7662) immediate a call to the authorization server per request, usually cached

The pragmatic combination most systems land on: short-lived access tokens, rotating refresh tokens with reuse detection, and a small deny-list consulted on every request for the emergency case. The deny-list is tiny because entries can be discarded once the token would have expired anyway.

8.8 The Authentication Failures That Keep Happening

OWASP API2:2023 is Broken Authentication, and the recurring patterns are worth listing plainly, because each is cheap to prevent and expensive to discover.

  • An unauthenticated endpoint that was supposed to be authenticated. Almost always because authentication is opt-in per route. Make it opt-out: a global dependency that requires a principal, and an explicit marker on the handful of public routes. Then a forgotten decorator fails closed.
  • No rate limiting on login, token or password-reset endpoints. Credential stuffing is fully automated. These endpoints need much tighter limits than the rest of the API, keyed on both IP and account (§11.6).
  • Different responses for "no such user" and "wrong password". A user-enumeration oracle. One message, one status code, and a constant-time path — including the case where the user does not exist, which must still do the password hash work or the timing becomes the oracle.
  • Password reset tokens that are long-lived, reusable, or guessable. Single use, 15 minutes, CSPRNG, invalidated on use and on password change.
  • No re-authentication for sensitive operations. Changing an email address, adding an MFA device or creating an API key should require the password again, even in a valid session. It is what stops a stolen session from becoming a stolen account.
  • Tokens that outlive the session. Logging out must revoke the refresh token, not merely delete it from the client.
  • Weak password storage. Argon2id, or scrypt, or bcrypt. Never a fast hash, never unsalted, never home-made.

Exercises

Exercise 8.1 — Choose the mechanism. For each, name the credential and flow, and one reason the obvious alternative is worse.

  1. A React single-page app on app.example.com calling api.example.com.
  2. A partner's server calling your API nightly to import invoices.
  3. A CLI tool a developer installs, acting as themselves.
  4. A mobile app calling your API on behalf of a logged-in user.
  5. Your own frontend service calling your own orders service inside the cluster.
Solution
  1. Authorization code + PKCE, tokens held by a backend-for-frontend, session in an HttpOnly cookie. The alternative — the implicit flow, or storing a JWT in localStorage — is worse because any XSS on the page exfiltrates the token, and the implicit grant is removed in OAuth 2.1 anyway (§8.3). A BFF (§1.7) keeps the token out of the browser entirely.
  2. Client credentials, or a scoped API key. Both are correct; prefer client credentials if you already run an authorization server, because you get short-lived tokens and scopes without building them. A user's credential is the wrong answer: nobody is present, and the access must survive that person leaving the company.
  3. Authorization code + PKCE with a loopback redirect (http://127.0.0.1:<random port>, which RFC 8252 permits for native apps). The alternative — asking the developer to paste an API key into a config file — puts a long-lived credential in a dotfile that ends up in a repository.
  4. Authorization code + PKCE, refresh token in the platform keystore (Keychain, EncryptedSharedPreferences). Not the password grant: it is removed in OAuth 2.1, it gives the app the user's actual password, and it makes MFA impossible.
  5. mTLS, or short-lived service tokens from a workload-identity system. A shared static secret in an environment variable is worse because it never rotates, is visible to anything that can read the process environment, and is identical across every instance — so a single leak is unbounded.

Exercise 8.2 — Four wrong JWT verifiers. Each of these ships somewhere. Name the vulnerability and the fix.

def verifier_a(token):
    claims = json.loads(unb64(token.split(".")[1]))
    return load_user(claims["sub"])

def verifier_b(token):
    header = json.loads(unb64(token.split(".")[0]))
    return verify_with(algorithm=header["alg"], token=token)

def verifier_c(token):
    claims = decode(token, key=PUBLIC_KEY, algorithms=["RS256"])
    if claims["exp"] < time.time():
        raise Unauthorized()
    return claims

def verifier_d(token):
    claims = decode(token, key=SECRET, algorithms=["HS256"])
    return claims["scope"].split()
Solution

A (verifier_a) — no signature verification at all. It base64-decodes the payload and trusts it. Anyone can mint {"sub": "admin"} in a text editor; the token is not encrypted and needs no key to write. This is not a subtle bug, and it ships regularly because "decode" sounds like it validates. Fix: verify the signature before reading a single claim.

B (verifier_b) — algorithm confusion. The verifier takes its algorithm from the attacker-supplied header. Two exploits: alg: none, and switching an RS256 deployment to HS256 and signing with the RSA public key as the HMAC secret, which is public by definition. Fix: pin the algorithm in your code and reject any token whose header disagrees.

C (verifier_c) — no audience or issuer check. The signature and expiry are checked, so a token from the same provider issued for a different service passes. If your identity provider serves five applications, any of them can call this API with its own users' tokens. Fix: decode(..., audience="orders-api", issuer="https://auth.example.com"), and check both.

D (verifier_d) — authorization from an unvalidated claim, and no aud check either. The scope is read straight out of the token and used as a permission set. That is only as trustworthy as everything above it, and even with a valid signature, scopes are what the client was granted, not what the user may do (§9.4). Fix: check aud, then treat the scope as a ceiling on the request and still authorize the specific object being touched (§9.2).

The meta-lesson: all four are prevented by using a maintained library with the algorithm, audience and issuer configured — and by never having a code path that reads a claim before verification has succeeded.

Exercise 8.3 — Design refresh-token rotation. Specify the storage, the exchange, the reuse-detection response, and the logout behaviour. Then say what happens when a legitimate client's refresh response is lost in the network.

Solution

Storage. One row per issued refresh token: id, family_id, user_id, token_hash (SHA-256; the token is 256 random bits), issued_at, expires_at, used_at, replaced_by, plus user_agent and ip for forensics. A family is one login session; every rotation adds a row to the same family.

Exchange. Look up by hash. Reject if absent, expired, or belonging to a revoked family. If used_at is null: mark it used, issue a new access token and a new refresh token in the same family, set replaced_by, return both.

Reuse detection. If used_at is not null, the token has been redeemed before. Revoke the whole family — every token in it, used or not — and return 400 invalid_grant. The user must log in again. Log it as a security event with the stored ip and user_agent of both redemptions, because that is the evidence for whether it was theft.

Logout. Revoke the family and delete the session cookie. Access tokens already issued remain technically valid until they expire, which is the §8.7 trade-off; if the 15-minute window is unacceptable, add the token's jti to the deny-list on logout.

The lost response. This is the case reuse detection cannot distinguish from theft, and it is the honest weakness of the design. The client sent a valid refresh token, the server rotated it, and the response never arrived — so the client retries with a token the server has already marked used, and gets its family revoked. The user is logged out for no reason.

Three mitigations, in order of preference:

  1. A short grace window. Accept a used token for a few seconds if the request presents the same client fingerprint and the replacement has not itself been used. This is what most production implementations do, and it removes almost all false positives while leaving a real thief a window measured in seconds.
  2. Idempotent refresh. Key the exchange on a client-supplied request ID (§11.2) and return the same new pair for a repeated request. Exact, and requires client cooperation.
  3. Accept the false positive. For high-security systems — banking, admin consoles — an occasional spurious logout is a reasonable price for immediate detection.

Do not respond by removing rotation. Rotation without reuse detection still limits a stolen token to one use; reuse detection is what tells you the theft happened.

Exercise 8.4 — Fail closed. An audit finds three endpoints with no authentication. All three were meant to be authenticated; each was added in a hurry by a different person. Redesign so this class of mistake cannot recur, and name the one endpoint category that must stay public.

Solution

The root cause is that authentication is opt-in. Any mechanism where the secure behaviour requires remembering to add something will fail eventually, because forgetting is the default state of a hurried person.

Invert it. Apply authentication globally at the router or middleware, so every route requires a principal unless explicitly marked otherwise:

from fastapi import Depends, FastAPI

app = FastAPI(dependencies=[Depends(require_principal)])   # applies to every route

public = APIRouter(dependencies=[])                        # opt-out, and visible

A new route added in a hurry inherits the requirement. The failure mode of forgetting is now a 401 in the first test run rather than an open endpoint in production.

Then make it visible and enforced:

  • A test that enumerates the route table and asserts that every path either requires a principal or appears in an explicit PUBLIC_ROUTES allowlist. Adding a public route becomes a deliberate, reviewable change to a list, not an omission.
  • A CI check on the generated OpenAPI document (§14.2) that every operation carries a security requirement or is in the same allowlist. This catches the case where the dependency exists but does not actually authenticate.
  • An alert on unauthenticated 2xx responses by route, so an endpoint answering successfully without a principal is visible in production even if both checks above were bypassed.

The category that must stay public: the unauthenticated entry points — health and readiness probes (§15.4), the OpenAPI document if it is public, the OAuth endpoints themselves (/token, /authorize, /.well-known/*), and login, signup and password reset. These are exactly the endpoints that need the tightest rate limiting (§8.8), because being public is what makes them worth attacking.

SECTION 9

Authorization

Authorization answers a different question from §8: not who is calling but may this caller do this, to this specific thing, right now. It is the number one and number five entries on the OWASP API Security Top 10:2023 — Broken Object Level Authorization and Broken Function Level Authorization — and they are ranked there because the failures are trivially exploitable, undetectable from the outside without trying, and invisible in a test suite that only ever uses one account.

9.1 Two Questions, Not One

The distinction is worth stating precisely, because collapsing it is the root of most of what follows.

Authentication establishes a principal. It happens once per request, at the edge, and it is generic — the same code for every endpoint. Failure is 401.
Authorization is a decision about an action on an object. It happens once per object touched, deep in the request, and it is specific — different for every endpoint and often for every row. Failure is 403, or 404 when existence is confidential.

Three consequences follow, and they explain why authorization is harder than authentication:

A valid token proves nothing about permission. It says the caller is who they claim. Order 91 may still belong to someone else.

Authorization cannot be fully centralised. Authentication middleware can cover every route; an object-level check needs the object, which means it happens after the load, inside the handler or the service layer. Anything that claims to solve authorization purely at the gateway is solving only the coarse half of it (§9.3).

Authorization has to run on every path to the data. The GET, the PATCH, the list endpoint, the export job, the GraphQL resolver, the webhook payload. A single unguarded path is the whole vulnerability, which is why §9.7 argues for putting the check somewhere it cannot be bypassed rather than somewhere it is merely present.

9.2 Broken Object Level Authorization

BOLA (OWASP API1:2023, historically called IDOR — Insecure Direct Object Reference) is the single most common serious API vulnerability. The shape is always the same: the endpoint authenticates the caller, takes an object ID from the request, and loads that object without checking whether this caller may see that object.

from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class Invoice:
    id: str
    owner_id: str
    total_cents: int

INVOICES = {
    "inv_1": Invoice("inv_1", owner_id="u_ada", total_cents=4250),
    "inv_2": Invoice("inv_2", owner_id="u_grace", total_cents=99_000),
}

# --- vulnerable: authenticated, but not authorized -------------------------
def get_invoice_bola(caller: str, invoice_id: str) -> tuple[int, object]:
    invoice = INVOICES.get(invoice_id)
    if invoice is None:
        return 404, None
    return 200, invoice                      # never looks at `caller`

# --- correct: the ownership predicate is part of the lookup ----------------
def get_invoice(caller: str, invoice_id: str) -> tuple[int, object]:
    invoice = INVOICES.get(invoice_id)
    if invoice is None or invoice.owner_id != caller:
        return 404, None                     # 404, not 403: existence is private
    return 200, invoice

for label, fn in (("vulnerable", get_invoice_bola), ("correct   ", get_invoice)):
    own = fn("u_ada", "inv_1")[0]
    other = fn("u_ada", "inv_2")
    print(f"{label}  own invoice: {own}   someone else's: {other[0]}"
          f"   leaked: {other[1] is not None}")

vulnerable  own invoice: 200   someone else's: 200   leaked: True
correct     own invoice: 200   someone else's: 404   leaked: False

Four points about the fix.

The check is part of the lookup, not a separate step. WHERE id = ? AND owner_id = ? cannot be forgotten between the load and the return, because there is no gap for it to be forgotten in. A separate if after the load is correct today and gets refactored away eventually.

404, not 403, when existence is confidential. 403 confirms that inv_2 exists, which is itself a disclosure — an attacker can enumerate valid IDs by the difference. Use 403 only where the caller is already entitled to know the object exists.

Unguessable IDs are not a control. They raise the cost of enumeration and are worth using (§3.2), but they leak: into Referer headers, browser history, support tickets, screenshots and shared links. Treat them as a speed bump, never as the check.

List endpoints have the same bug in a different shape. GET /invoices must filter by the caller, and — critically — the caller must not be able to supply the filter. ?ownerId=u_grace taken from the query string is BOLA with extra steps; the predicate comes from the token, always.

The reliable way to find existing instances: take every endpoint that accepts an ID, and call it with two accounts. Anything that answers 200 for both is a finding. That is a test you can write once and generate (Exercise 9.1).

9.3 Broken Function Level Authorization

BFLA (OWASP API5:2023) is the coarser sibling: the caller may not perform this operation at all, regardless of which object. Deleting a user, issuing a refund, reading the audit log, changing someone's role. Where BOLA is a question about a row, BFLA is a question about a route — which is why it is the one an automated scan can plausibly find, and the one a gateway can partly enforce.

The recurring causes:

  • The UI hides the button, and that is the whole control. The endpoint is reachable by anyone who reads the JavaScript bundle, and the bundle ships to every user.
  • The admin routes are a separate router that someone forgot to guard. /admin/* mounted without the dependency that /api/* has.
  • The check is on the path, not the operation. A gateway rule allowing /orders/* to any authenticated caller silently permits DELETE /orders/91 when only GET was intended.
  • A new method on an existing route. GET /users/{id} was guarded; DELETE /users/{id} was added later, in the same file, and inherits nothing.
  • HTTP method override headers. X-HTTP-Method-Override: DELETE on a POST bypasses a gateway rule written in terms of methods. Reject the header unless you genuinely need it.

The structural fix is the same inversion as §8.8's: make authorization a required property of a route, not an optional decorator. A route declares the permission it needs; a test enumerates the route table and fails on any route that declares none. Then "forgot to add the check" becomes a test failure rather than a production incident.

from dataclasses import dataclass, field

@dataclass
class Principal:
    id: str
    roles: set[str] = field(default_factory=set)
    scopes: set[str] = field(default_factory=set)

# Every route declares what it needs. A route absent from this table cannot ship.
ROUTE_PERMISSIONS: dict[tuple[str, str], str] = {
    ("GET",    "/orders/{id}"): "orders:read",
    ("PATCH",  "/orders/{id}"): "orders:write",
    ("DELETE", "/orders/{id}"): "orders:delete",
    ("GET",    "/admin/audit"): "audit:read",
}

ROLE_PERMISSIONS: dict[str, set[str]] = {
    "customer": {"orders:read", "orders:write"},
    "support":  {"orders:read", "orders:delete"},
    "auditor":  {"audit:read"},
}

def permitted(principal: Principal, method: str, route: str) -> bool:
    needed = ROUTE_PERMISSIONS[(method, route)]        # KeyError = unregistered route
    granted = set().union(*(ROLE_PERMISSIONS.get(r, set()) for r in principal.roles)) \
        if principal.roles else set()
    if principal.scopes and needed not in principal.scopes:
        return False                                   # the token narrows the role
    return needed in granted

ada = Principal("u_ada", roles={"customer"})
sam = Principal("u_sam", roles={"support"})
app = Principal("u_ada", roles={"customer"}, scopes={"orders:read"})   # scoped token

for who, principal in (("ada    ", ada), ("support", sam), ("scoped ", app)):
    row = [f"{m} {r.split('/')[1]:<6}={permitted(principal, m, r)!s:<5}"
           for m, r in ROUTE_PERMISSIONS]
    print(who, " ".join(row))

try:
    permitted(ada, "POST", "/orders")
except KeyError as exc:
    print("unregistered route refused:", exc)

ada     GET orders=True  PATCH orders=True  DELETE orders=False GET admin =False
support GET orders=True  PATCH orders=False DELETE orders=True  GET admin =False
scoped  GET orders=True  PATCH orders=False DELETE orders=False GET admin =False
unregistered route refused: ('POST', '/orders')

Two things that table makes possible. The permission a route needs is data, so it can be listed, reviewed and diffed — a pull request that adds a route with no entry fails on the KeyError. And the scoped token row shows the correct interaction between roles and scopes: a token's scopes can only narrow what a role grants, never widen it (§9.4).

Note what this still does not do: it says nothing about which order. Function-level authorization is necessary and never sufficient — §9.2 is the other half, and both must run.

9.4 Scopes, Roles and Permissions

Three vocabularies, frequently confused, answering three different questions.

Concept Question Assigned to Set by
Role what job does this person do? the user your administrators
Permission what operation does this allow? a role your product design
Scope what did the user let this application do? the token the user, at consent time

The relationship that matters: a scope is a ceiling, not a grant. If Ada is a customer and authorises a third-party app with orders:read, the app may read Ada's orders — not everyone's, and not more than Ada could. The effective permission set is the intersection:

effective = permissions(user's roles)  ∩  token scopes  ∩  object-level rules

Skipping the intersection is a real vulnerability: an app granted orders:read by a customer, calling an endpoint that checks only the scope, gets to read every order in the system.

Practical guidance:

  • Name scopes resource:action. orders:read, orders:write, invoices:read. Predictable, greppable, and it composes.
  • Keep them coarse. A scope is something a human reads on a consent screen. Forty of them is a consent screen nobody reads; orders:read and orders:write is one they might.
  • Roles are internal, scopes are external. Do not put your role names in a token you hand to a third party; they will start depending on them.
  • Prefer RBAC until it stops working, then add attributes. Role-based access control (roles → permissions) covers most systems. When rules start depending on properties of the object — "a manager may approve expenses under £5,000 in their own department" — you need attribute-based rules (ABAC), and the honest way to add them is a policy layer, not more roles. Watch for the signal: role names like manager_eng_uk_approver mean you are encoding attributes in strings.
  • Relationship-based rules deserve their own mechanism. "Anyone with edit access to a folder has edit access to its documents" is a graph question. Encoding it in roles produces a combinatorial mess; a dedicated relationship model (the Zanzibar design) handles it.

9.5 Multi-Tenancy and the Tenant Boundary

In a multi-tenant system, the most catastrophic failure is not a user reading another user's row — it is a user reading another company's data. The blast radius is the difference between an embarrassing bug and a contract-ending one.

Three isolation models:

Model Isolation Cost
Separate database per tenant strongest — a query cannot cross migrations × N, connection pools × N, expensive below a few hundred tenants
Shared database, separate schema strong schema management × N
Shared table, tenant_id column weakest — one missing predicate crosses the boundary cheapest, and by far the most common

The shared-table model is what most systems use, and it stands or falls on one rule: the tenant filter must be structurally impossible to omit. "Remember to add WHERE tenant_id = ?" is not a control; it is an aspiration about forty query sites and everyone who will ever join the team.

Four mechanisms that make it structural, best first:

  • Row-level security in the database. PostgreSQL's CREATE POLICY applies the predicate to every query on the table, including ones written by a future colleague, including the ad-hoc one someone runs during an incident. The application sets SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = ... at the start of each transaction, and the database enforces the rest. This is the strongest option short of separate databases, and the SQL course covers the mechanics.
  • A repository layer that takes the tenant from a request context and cannot be called without it. Weaker, because it can be bypassed by writing raw SQL, but it is portable.
  • A composite primary key (tenant_id, id), so a query missing the tenant cannot even find the row by ID. Cheap, effective, and it makes the mistake a 404 instead of a leak.
  • A test that runs the whole suite as tenant B against tenant A's fixtures and asserts that nothing is visible. This is the one that catches what the others miss.

Two further rules that are learned the hard way:

The tenant comes from the credential, never from the request. Not a query parameter, not a header the client can set, not a field in the body. If a client can name a tenant, a client can name someone else's tenant (§6.3's tenantId in the mass-assignment exercise is exactly this).

Cache keys must include the tenant. A cache keyed on invoice:{id} in a system where IDs are unique per tenant will serve tenant A's invoice to tenant B. Add the tenant to every key, and add Vary for anything an intermediary caches (§10.3).

9.6 Least Privilege for the Service Itself

Authorization is usually discussed as a property of your callers. It is also a property of your service, and OWASP API8:2023 (Security Misconfiguration) is largely about this half being neglected.

Least privilege for the service means the credentials your API holds should be the smallest set that lets it work:

  • A database user with only the grants it needs. A read-mostly service does not need DELETE. Nothing needs DROP. A separate migration user, used only by migrations, keeps DDL out of the runtime credential — which means SQL injection in a handler cannot alter your schema.
  • Separate credentials per service, not one shared application user, so a compromise is scoped and an audit log can attribute an action.
  • Cloud roles scoped to specific resources. A service that writes to one bucket should not hold s3:* on all of them. The metadata endpoint an SSRF reaches (§6.5) hands out exactly the role the instance carries — so the size of that role is the size of the SSRF's blast radius.
  • Egress restrictions. A service that talks to two upstreams should not be able to open arbitrary outbound connections.
  • Short-lived credentials. Workload identity that mints minute-lifetime tokens beats a static secret in an environment variable, because rotation stops being a project.

The test to apply when reviewing a service's own permissions: if an attacker got remote code execution in this process, what could they reach? Every answer that surprises you is a grant to remove.

9.7 Where the Check Belongs

The same authorization rule can live in four places, and each has a different failure mode.

Gateway. Coarse and cheap: is there a valid token, does its scope cover this route. Cannot see objects, so it can only ever do half of §9.3 and none of §9.2. Failure mode: a false sense of completeness.
Handler. Has the request and the principal. Failure mode: one handler out of forty forgets, and nothing detects it — the endpoint works perfectly for everyone with permission.
Service / repository layer. Every path to the data goes through it — HTTP, queue workers, batch jobs, the admin console. Failure mode: the principal has to be threaded through, which people resist and then work around.
Database. Row-level security cannot be bypassed by any query, including a hand-written one during an incident. Failure mode: policies are invisible from the application code, so they surprise people, and they are hard to express for anything but row ownership.

The workable arrangement is layered, and the layers are not redundant — they catch different mistakes:

  1. Gateway: authentication and coarse scope checks. Cheap rejection of obvious garbage before it reaches you.
  2. Route table: the declared permission for the operation (§9.3), enforced by a dependency that cannot be omitted.
  3. Data access: the ownership or tenancy predicate, in the query (§9.2, §9.5). This is the one that actually protects objects.
  4. Database: row-level security for the tenant boundary, as the backstop that survives your next refactor.

And one rule that ties them together: the decision must be centralised even when the enforcement is not. A can(principal, action, object) function, or a policy engine, called from every enforcement point — so that "who may cancel an order" is answered in one place, and the four layers cannot disagree with each other.

Exercises

Exercise 9.1 — Write the BOLA test. Design a test that would catch every instance of §9.2 across an API of 60 endpoints, without writing 60 tests by hand. Say what it needs from the codebase and what it cannot catch.

Solution

The design: a table-driven cross-account test. Two fixtures — tenant A with a full set of objects, tenant B with its own — and one parametrised test that walks the route table.

import pytest

# One row per route that takes an object id. Generated from the route table, then
# reviewed — a route with no entry fails the completeness check below.
CASES = [
    ("GET",    "/orders/{id}",   "order_a"),
    ("PATCH",  "/orders/{id}",   "order_a"),
    ("DELETE", "/orders/{id}",   "order_a"),
    ("GET",    "/invoices/{id}", "invoice_a"),
]

@pytest.mark.parametrize("method,template,fixture", CASES)
def test_other_tenant_cannot_reach_object(client_b, objects, method, template, fixture):
    path = template.format(id=objects[fixture].id)
    response = client_b.request(method, path)
    assert response.status_code in (403, 404), f"{method} {path} leaked to another tenant"
    assert objects[fixture].id not in response.text        # nor in an error message

def test_every_id_route_is_covered(app):
    """A new route taking an {id} must appear in CASES or this fails."""
    id_routes = {(m, r.path) for r in app.routes for m in r.methods if "{" in r.path}
    covered = {(m, t) for m, t, _ in CASES}
    assert id_routes - covered == set(), f"uncovered id routes: {id_routes - covered}"

What it needs from the codebase: an enumerable route table (every framework has one), fixtures for two independent tenants, and a client authenticated as each.

What makes it work is the second test. Without it, the parametrised list drifts out of date the first time someone adds a route, which is precisely when the coverage is needed. With it, adding a route forces a decision.

What it cannot catch:

  • Leaks through nested objects. GET /orders/{own_id} returning an embedded customer record the caller may not see is a 200 on their own object, so it passes. Property-level authorization (§6.3) needs its own assertions on response shape.
  • Write-side leaks. A PATCH that accepts {"tenantId": "other"} moves an object across the boundary while returning 200 on the caller's own object.
  • Non-HTTP paths. Queue workers, exports, admin scripts and GraphQL resolvers are not in the route table (§9.7).
  • Timing and error-shape oracles. A 404 that takes 200 ms for a real object and 5 ms for a nonexistent one still discloses existence.

Which is why this test is the floor, not the ceiling — but it is a floor that finds real bugs in almost every codebase it is first run against.

Exercise 9.2 — Find four authorization bugs. All four are in this handler.

@app.get("/reports")
async def reports(tenant_id: str, principal: Principal = Depends(auth)) -> list[dict]:
    if "reports:read" not in principal.scopes:
        raise HTTPException(403)
    rows = await db.fetch("SELECT * FROM reports WHERE tenant_id = $1", tenant_id)
    return [dict(r) for r in rows]
Solution

1. The tenant comes from the request. tenant_id is a query parameter, so any authenticated caller reads any tenant's reports by changing it — a total cross-tenant breach (§9.5). The tenant must come from principal, and the parameter must not exist.

2. Scope is checked instead of permission. reports:read in the token means the application was granted report access by its user; it says nothing about whether that user has a role permitting it. A third-party app that a junior employee authorised now reads reports the employee could never see. The check must be the intersection (§9.4): needed in permissions(principal.roles) and needed in principal.scopes.

3. SELECT * with no output model. Every column ships, including anything added later — internal cost figures, another tenant's identifiers in a denormalised column, soft-delete flags. This is the over-exposure half of §6.3; the fix is an explicit response model.

4. No pagination. GET /reports returns every row for the tenant. That is §6.4's unrestricted resource consumption and §7's missing design: an uncapped list is a denial-of-service parameter and, at scale, a timeout.

A fifth, subtler point: soft-deleted rows are included (§7.6), because the query has no deleted_at IS NULL predicate.

@app.get("/reports", response_model=Page[ReportOut])
async def reports(principal: Principal = Depends(require("reports:read")),
                  limit: int = Query(20, ge=1, le=100),
                  page_token: str | None = None) -> Page[ReportOut]:
    rows = await reports_repo.list(
        tenant_id=principal.tenant_id,          # from the credential, not the request
        limit=limit + 1, after=decode_token(page_token))
    items = [ReportOut.model_validate(r) for r in rows[:limit]]
    return Page(items=items, nextPageToken=next_token(rows, limit))

require("reports:read") performs the intersection once, in one place, so every route that uses it agrees. reports_repo.list cannot be called without a tenant, and row-level security backs it up in the database (§9.5).

Exercise 9.3 — Model the permissions. A document system: documents live in folders; folders belong to workspaces; a user can be a workspace admin, editor or viewer; a document can additionally be shared directly with an individual at edit or view; and a public link grants view to anyone. Model this, and say why plain RBAC is not enough.

Solution

Why RBAC alone fails. Roles map a user to a set of permissions globally. Here, permission depends on the (user, document) pair and on a chain of relationships — workspace membership, folder containment, direct share, link. Encoding that in roles means a role per workspace per level (ws_42_editor), and direct shares cannot be encoded at all without a role per document. The role count grows with the data, which is the signal that the model is wrong.

The model that fits is relationship-based, in the Zanzibar style: store tuples of (object, relation, subject) and answer queries by traversing them.

(workspace:42, admin,  user:ada)
(workspace:42, viewer, user:sam)
(folder:9,     parent, workspace:42)
(document:7,   parent, folder:9)
(document:7,   editor, user:sam)          -- a direct share overrides the workspace role
(document:7,   viewer, public)            -- the public link

With rules that define how permissions flow:

can_view(d)  = viewer(d) ∪ can_edit(d) ∪ can_view(parent(d)) ∪ public_link(d)
can_edit(d)  = editor(d) ∪ can_admin(d) ∪ can_edit(parent(d))
can_admin(d) = admin(parent(d))

Sam is a workspace viewer but has a direct editor share on document 7, so can_edit(document:7) is true for Sam and false for every other document in the workspace. That is the case plain RBAC cannot express.

Three implementation notes. Cache the traversal, because a deep folder tree turns one permission check into several lookups on the hot path. Make deletion propagate — removing a workspace member must invalidate every derived answer, which is why these systems store a consistency token. And keep an explicit deny out of the model if you can: union-only rules are far easier to reason about, and "deny overrides" makes every answer depend on evaluating the whole graph.

For a system of this shape, the honest options are a dedicated authorization service (OpenFGA, SpiceDB and similar implement exactly this model) or a carefully-scoped in-house version. What does not work is adding a fourth role.

Exercise 9.4 — Scope the service down. Your orders API currently connects to PostgreSQL as the postgres superuser and runs on an instance whose cloud role has s3:* and secretsmanager:*. Rewrite both, and say what each change would have prevented in a specific incident.

Solution

Database. Three users instead of one:

CREATE ROLE orders_app  LOGIN;   -- runtime
CREATE ROLE orders_ddl  LOGIN;   -- migrations only, used by CI
CREATE ROLE orders_ro   LOGIN;   -- read replicas, analytics, on-call queries

GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON orders, order_items TO orders_app;
GRANT SELECT ON orders, order_items TO orders_ro;
-- orders_app gets no DELETE (soft delete is an UPDATE), no DDL, no access to
-- other services' tables, and no rights on the audit table beyond INSERT.
GRANT INSERT ON audit_log TO orders_app;

What it prevents: an SQL injection or a compromised process can read and modify orders — bad — but cannot DROP TABLE, cannot alter the schema, cannot read the users table belonging to another service, and cannot erase the audit trail that records what it did. It also prevents the far more common non-malicious version: a migration accidentally run against production by a runtime process.

Cloud role. Replace the two wildcards:

s3:GetObject, s3:PutObject   on arn:aws:s3:::orders-attachments/*      (that bucket only)
secretsmanager:GetSecretValue on arn:aws:secretsmanager:...:secret/orders/db-*

What it prevents: this is the SSRF blast radius from §6.5. An attacker who reaches the instance metadata endpoint gets exactly this role. With s3:* and secretsmanager:*, that is every bucket in the account and every secret — including other services' database credentials, which turns one SSRF into a full account compromise. Scoped, the same SSRF yields read/write on one attachment bucket and one database credential the attacker probably already had.

Two more changes worth making at the same time. Give the runtime a short-lived credential from workload identity rather than a static secret, so the exposure window is minutes rather than "until someone notices". And add an egress rule allowing outbound connections only to the database, the object store and the two upstream APIs — which is what stops the metadata endpoint being reachable from the fetcher in the first place.

SECTION 10

Caching and Concurrency Control
