These two subjects share a section because they share a mechanism. HTTP's caching model and its lost-update protection are both built on validators — small opaque values identifying a representation — and on conditional requests, which say "do this only if the resource is still in the state I think it is". Learn the mechanism once and you get a cache that does not serve stale data and a write path that cannot silently overwrite someone else's edit.

10.1 What HTTP Caching Actually Promises

A cache stores a response and reuses it for a later equivalent request. Three kinds exist, and they behave differently in ways that matter:

Private cache — the browser's, serving one user. Storing personal data here is fine.
Shared cache — a CDN, a reverse proxy, a corporate proxy, serving many users. Storing personal data here without saying private is how one user is served another's response.
Your own cache — Redis or memory inside your service. Not governed by HTTP at all, and therefore entirely your problem (§10.7).

Cache-Control (RFC 9111 §5.2) is how you control the first two. The directives worth knowing:

Directive Meaning
max-age=N Fresh for N seconds
s-maxage=N Fresh for N seconds in shared caches only, overriding max-age
public May be stored by a shared cache even if it would not normally be
private Only a private cache may store this. The one you need on anything user-specific
no-cache May be stored, but must be revalidated before every reuse
no-store Must not be written down at all. For credentials and payment data
must-revalidate Once stale, revalidate — do not serve stale on error
stale-while-revalidate=N May serve stale for N seconds while refreshing in the background
stale-if-error=N May serve stale for N seconds if the origin is failing
immutable Will never change. Do not even revalidate on reload

Two directives are routinely confused. no-cache does not mean "do not cache" — it means "cache, but always check first". The one that means do not store anything is no-store.

Sensible defaults by resource kind:

GET /users/me            Cache-Control: private, max-age=0, must-revalidate
GET /orders/91           Cache-Control: private, max-age=30
GET /products/summer     Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=300
GET /static/app.9f4a.js  Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
POST /transfers          Cache-Control: no-store
any 4xx/5xx problem doc  Cache-Control: no-store

The immutable line works because the filename contains a content hash: a new build is a new URL, so the old one genuinely never changes. That pattern — immutable content at versioned URLs, short-lived pointers to them — is the strongest caching arrangement available, and it applies to API payloads as well as to assets.

10.2 Freshness and Revalidation

A cached response is in one of two states. While its age is below the freshness lifetime it is fresh and is served without contacting you at all. Once past it, it is stale, and the cache must revalidate before reuse — which does not mean re-downloading.

Revalidation is a conditional request: the cache sends the validator it holds, and you answer either 304 Not Modified (headers only, no body) or 200 with the new representation. A 304 on a 40 KB response costs a few hundred bytes.

The full sequence:

GET /orders/91 HTTP/1.1
If-None-Match: "9f4a1e"

HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified
ETag: "9f4a1e"
Cache-Control: private, max-age=30

stale-while-revalidate is worth understanding because it removes the latency cost of expiry entirely: past the freshness lifetime, the cache serves the stale copy immediately and refreshes in the background, so no user ever waits for a revalidation. The price is that a response may be up to max-age + stale-while-revalidate seconds old, which for a product listing is invisible and for an account balance is unacceptable. stale-if-error is the companion: it lets a cache keep serving during an origin outage, turning a hard failure into slightly old data.

A useful rule for picking max-age: ask how stale a client can tolerate, not how often the data changes. A dashboard refreshed every 30 seconds tolerates 30 seconds of staleness regardless of whether the underlying value changes every millisecond.

10.3 Vary, and How Caches Build Their Keys

A cache keys on the method and URI. If your response depends on anything else — a header — the cache does not know, and will serve the wrong variant. Vary is how you tell it.

Vary: Accept, Accept-Language, Accept-Encoding

The rules that keep this safe:

  • Anything you negotiate on must be in Vary (§2.5). A response varying on Accept without saying so means the CSV representation gets served to a client that asked for JSON.
  • Vary: Origin whenever you echo an origin in a CORS header (§2.7). Without it, one origin's allowance is served to another.
  • Never Vary: Authorization as your privacy mechanism. It technically works and it is a trap: every distinct token becomes a distinct cache entry, so the hit rate is zero and the cache is now storing per-user data. Use Cache-Control: private instead, which keeps the response out of shared caches altogether.
  • Do not vary on User-Agent. It has effectively unbounded cardinality; you have disabled caching while paying for it.
  • Each varying header multiplies the entries. Vary: Accept, Accept-Language, Accept-Encoding with three values each is 27 cache entries per URI. Keep the list short.

The failure this prevents is a genuine cross-user data leak, and it is worth being blunt about the two ways it happens: forgetting Vary on a negotiated response, and forgetting private on a personalised one. Both put one user's bytes in a shared cache under a key another user will hit.

10.4 Validators: ETag and Last-Modified

A validator identifies a specific representation. HTTP defines two.

ETag is an opaque quoted string — usually a hash of the response body, sometimes a version number. Two flavours:

ETag: "9f4a1e"          strong: byte-for-byte identical
ETag: W/"9f4a1e"        weak: semantically equivalent, may differ in bytes

Strong validators are required for range requests and are what you want for concurrency control (§10.6). Weak ones are fine for cache revalidation and are the honest choice when your serialiser might reorder keys or your response embeds a timestamp.

Last-Modified is an HTTP-date, and its resolution is one second — which is its whole weakness. Two changes within the same second are indistinguishable, so a client can cache a version it should not. Use it as a supplement, never as your only validator.

Where to get the ETag matters more than it appears:

  • Hash the serialised response body. Always correct, because the ETag then identifies exactly what you sent — including the effect of field masks (§4.5) and content negotiation. Costs a hash over the body, which is cheap relative to producing it.
  • A version column on the row. Cheap and exact for concurrency control, and it lets you answer 304 before serialising. But it identifies the entity, not the representation, so it is wrong the moment one resource has two representations — the JSON and the CSV would share an ETag.
  • updated_at. Convenient and subject to the same one-second problem as Last-Modified, plus it does not change when a related row does.

The pragmatic combination: a version column for If-Match on writes, and a body hash for ETag on reads. They answer different questions, and using one for both is where the subtle bugs live.

10.5 Conditional Requests

RFC 9110 §13 defines the preconditions, and they are evaluated in a specified order.

Header Asks Used for
If-None-Match "only if the ETag is not one of these" cache revalidation on GET
If-Match "only if the ETag is one of these" lost-update prevention on writes
If-Modified-Since "only if changed since" revalidation, second-resolution
If-Unmodified-Since "only if unchanged since" writes, second-resolution

Three behaviours to implement correctly:

  • If-None-Match matching on a GET → 304, with the ETag and cache headers repeated and no body.
  • If-None-Match: * on a POST/PUT → create-only. It means "only if the resource does not exist", and it is the standard way to say "create, do not overwrite" without a race.
  • If-Match failing → 412 Precondition Failed. Not 409, not 400.

And one that is often skipped: 428 Precondition Required when your endpoint demands a conditional request and did not get one. Without it, a client that omits If-Match gets a silent last-write-wins, which is exactly the failure the precondition was meant to prevent. Requiring it is what makes the protection non-optional.

10.6 Optimistic Concurrency

Two clients read order 91, both edit it, both write. Without protection, the second write silently discards the first — the lost update problem. Optimistic concurrency control solves it without locks: the client sends back the validator it read, and the server rejects the write if the resource has moved on.

import asyncio
import hashlib
import json
import httpx
from fastapi import FastAPI, Header, HTTPException, Response

app = FastAPI()
ORDERS = {"91": {"id": "91", "status": "pending", "note": "gift wrap", "version": 1}}

def etag_of(order: dict) -> str:
    body = json.dumps(order, sort_keys=True, separators=(",", ":")).encode()
    return '"' + hashlib.sha256(body).hexdigest()[:12] + '"'

@app.get("/orders/{oid}", response_model=None)      # the handler returns dict OR Response
def read(oid: str, response: Response,
         if_none_match: str | None = Header(default=None)) -> dict | Response:
    order = ORDERS[oid]
    tag = etag_of(order)
    if if_none_match == tag:
        return Response(status_code=304, headers={"ETag": tag,
                                                  "Cache-Control": "private, max-age=30"})
    response.headers["ETag"] = tag
    response.headers["Cache-Control"] = "private, max-age=30"
    return order

@app.patch("/orders/{oid}")
def update(oid: str, body: dict, response: Response,
           if_match: str | None = Header(default=None)) -> dict:
    order = ORDERS[oid]
    if if_match is None:
        raise HTTPException(428, "If-Match is required on this endpoint")
    if if_match != etag_of(order):
        raise HTTPException(412, "the order has changed since you read it")
    order.update(body)
    order["version"] += 1
    response.headers["ETag"] = etag_of(order)
    return order

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://t") as c:
        r = await c.get("/orders/91")
        tag = r.headers["etag"]
        print("read      :", r.status_code, "etag", tag)

        r = await c.get("/orders/91", headers={"If-None-Match": tag})
        print("revalidate:", r.status_code, "body bytes", len(r.content))

        # Two clients read the same version; both try to write.
        alice, bob = tag, tag
        r = await c.patch("/orders/91", json={"note": "no wrapping"},
                          headers={"If-Match": alice})
        print("alice     :", r.status_code, "new etag", r.headers.get("etag"))
        r = await c.patch("/orders/91", json={"status": "cancelled"},
                          headers={"If-Match": bob})
        print("bob       :", r.status_code, r.json()["detail"])

        r = await c.patch("/orders/91", json={"status": "cancelled"})
        print("no header :", r.status_code, r.json()["detail"])
        print("final     :", ORDERS["91"])

asyncio.run(main())

read      : 200 etag "b4aec601518b"
revalidate: 304 body bytes 0
alice     : 200 new etag "80aab31212c5"
bob       : 412 the order has changed since you read it
no header : 428 If-Match is required on this endpoint
final     : {'id': '91', 'status': 'pending', 'note': 'no wrapping', 'version': 2}

Read the last two lines together: Bob's write was refused, the order still says pending, and Alice's note survived. Without the precondition, Bob's PATCH would have set status to cancelled on top of a version he never saw — and, worse, the reason the two writes conflict (Alice changed a different field) would have been invisible to everyone.

What the client does on a 412 is a design decision worth documenting: re-read, re-apply, retry is right for machine clients, and showing the user what changed is right for human ones. Silently retrying with the new ETag is wrong — it re-introduces the lost update with extra steps.

Two production notes. The etag_of above hashes the whole record including version, which is fine here but means the ETag changes when any field does — correct for concurrency, slightly over-eager for caching. And in a real system the check-and-write must be atomic: UPDATE orders SET ... WHERE id = ? AND version = ? and then checking the affected row count, rather than reading, comparing and writing in three steps with a race in the middle (§12.1).

10.7 Caching Inside Your Service

HTTP caching handles what happens between you and your clients. A cache inside your service — Redis, or an in-process dictionary — is a different problem with none of the protocol's help.

Three patterns:

Pattern How Watch out for
Cache-aside read cache, miss → load and store the thundering herd on a cold key
Write-through write to cache and store together two systems in one write path
Read-through the cache loads on miss the cache becomes a dependency of correctness

Cache-aside is the default and the right starting point. The problems to plan for:

Invalidation. Two honest strategies. Short TTLs — accept staleness, expire quickly, never invalidate explicitly. Boring, robust, and correct far more often than people expect. Explicit invalidation on write — precise, and it fails silently the day someone adds a second write path. Prefer TTLs, and add explicit invalidation only where the staleness genuinely matters.

The thundering herd. A popular key expires and a thousand concurrent requests all miss and all recompute. Mitigate with a short lock so one request recomputes while others wait or serve stale, or by refreshing early — at 90% of the TTL, in the background.

Cache stampede on cold start. A restart with an empty cache can be slower than the system can survive. Warm the hot keys, or roll deploys slowly enough that the cache refills.

Cache keys must include everything the value depends on — the tenant (§9.5), the user if the value is personalised, the version of the serialisation format. A key of order:91 in a multi-tenant system is a cross-tenant leak waiting for an ID collision, and a key that survives a deploy that changed the response shape will deserialise into the wrong type.

Negative caching. Cache "not found" too, with a short TTL. Without it, a client hammering a nonexistent ID hits your database on every request — which is a trivially available denial of service.

Exercises

Exercise 10.1 — Set the headers. Give the complete Cache-Control (and Vary, where needed) for each, with a one-line justification.

  1. GET /users/me
  2. GET /products?category=summer — a public catalogue, changes a few times a day
  3. GET /static/app.9f4a1e.js — content-hashed filename
  4. POST /payments
  5. GET /reports/2026-Q1 — negotiable as JSON or CSV, immutable once the quarter closes
  6. A 429 rate-limit problem document
Solution
  1. Cache-Control: private, max-age=0, must-revalidate — personal, so never in a shared cache; max-age=0 plus a validator means every request revalidates and usually gets a cheap 304.
  2. Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=300 and Vary: Accept-Encoding — a minute of staleness is invisible on a catalogue, and stale-while-revalidate means no user ever waits for the refresh. Vary on encoding because gzip and Brotli variants differ.
  3. Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable — the hash is in the filename, so this URL's content can never change; immutable stops even a hard reload from revalidating.
  4. Cache-Control: no-store — a payment response must not be written to disk anywhere, and it is not a GET, so nothing should be caching it in the first place. Belt and braces.
  5. Cache-Control: public, max-age=86400 plus Vary: Accept, Accept-Encoding — Vary: Accept is mandatory or the CSV gets served to a JSON client (§2.5). Once the quarter closes it could go to immutable; while it is open, a shorter max-age.
  6. Cache-Control: no-store — caching a rate-limit response would keep rejecting a client that is no longer over the limit. Every problem document should carry it.

The general rule: private on anything personal, a Vary entry for every header you negotiate on, and no-store on everything that failed.

Exercise 10.2 — The leak. A team adds a /me endpoint behind a CDN and users start seeing each other's profiles, intermittently, and only for users on the same corporate network. Explain exactly what happened, and give two independent fixes.

Solution

What happened. /me returns a different response per user but the same URI for everyone. The response carried a shared-cacheable Cache-Control — public, max-age=60, or nothing at all with a CDN configured to cache 200s by default. The CDN keyed on GET /me, stored the first user's response, and served it to the next user for the remainder of the freshness lifetime.

"Only on the same corporate network" is the diagnostic detail: a corporate proxy is a shared cache too, and it is the one users share most tightly. The CDN edge that a colleague hits is likely the same edge.

Fix 1 — Cache-Control: private (with max-age=0, must-revalidate if you want revalidation). This is the correct, protocol-level fix: it tells every shared cache that the response belongs to one user and must not be stored. It works in every intermediary without configuration.

Fix 2 — do not use a shared URI for personalised content. /users/{id} instead of /me, or a per-user cache key at the CDN. /me is genuinely useful (§3.5), so the practical version of this fix is to configure the CDN to bypass the cache for that path entirely.

Not a fix: Vary: Authorization. It appears to work — each token becomes a distinct entry — and it is wrong for two reasons. The hit rate goes to zero, so you pay for the cache and get nothing; and personal data is now stored in a shared cache, one entry per user, which is exactly the situation you were trying to avoid. A cache misconfiguration or a Vary-ignoring intermediary turns it straight back into the original bug.

Prevention. A test that asserts every authenticated response carries private or no-store. It is three lines against the route table and it catches the whole class.

Exercise 10.3 — Add lost-update protection. Retrofit If-Match onto an existing PATCH /documents/{id} that thousands of clients already use. Give the migration path, and say what to do about clients that never send the header.

Solution

You cannot start returning 428 immediately — every existing client breaks at once. Four phases:

Phase 1 — start emitting. Add ETag to every GET /documents/{id} response, and make PATCH honour If-Match when present (412 on mismatch) while still accepting requests without it. Nothing breaks, and correct clients can opt in today. Also return the new ETag on the PATCH response, so a client can chain edits without a re-read.

Phase 2 — measure. Log, per client and per API key, whether If-Match was present. You now know exactly who is affected and can talk to them. This is §14.7's argument in miniature: you cannot enforce a change without knowing who it breaks.

Phase 3 — announce and deprecate. Document that If-Match becomes mandatory on a specific date. Send Deprecation and Sunset headers on unconditional PATCH requests (§14.6). Contact the top clients by hand — the long tail is the problem, the head is a conversation.

Phase 4 — enforce. 428 Precondition Required when the header is absent, with a problem document explaining how to fix it and linking the documentation.

What to do about clients that never comply. Three options, and the choice depends on the data:

  • Enforce anyway if lost updates are a correctness or compliance problem — a financial ledger, a permissions document. Breaking a client is better than silent data loss.
  • Grandfather them by API version. v1 keeps last-write-wins, v2 requires the precondition (§14.5). Clean, and it is what a version is for.
  • Detect the conflict without the header. Take a hash on read, compare on write via a server-side session. Fragile — it requires the same client to have done the read — and worth it only when neither of the above is available.

One thing not to do: enforce it silently in a patch release because "it is more correct". A 428 to a client that has never seen one is an outage, and it will be reported as your fault regardless of who is technically right.

Exercise 10.4 — Diagnose the cache. A product listing is cached at the CDN with public, max-age=300. After a price change, some users see the new price immediately, some see the old one for five minutes, and a few see it for over an hour. Explain the three populations, and design a scheme where a price change is visible within seconds without giving up caching.

Solution

The three populations.

  • Immediate: their request missed the cache — a cold edge, an evicted entry, or an edge that had not been asked for this URL yet.
  • Up to five minutes: a normal hit on a fresh entry stored just before the change. This is max-age working as designed.
  • Over an hour: their browser cached it too. The same max-age=300 applies at every layer, and the chain compounds — a browser can hold an entry that was already four minutes old at the CDN, and any intermediary proxy in between adds another window. A user who does not reload keeps a fresh-enough copy well past the nominal lifetime.

The scheme: versioned immutable content behind a short-lived pointer.

GET /products/summer            Cache-Control: public, max-age=5, stale-while-revalidate=60
                                → {"version": "v914", "url": "/products/summer/v914"}

GET /products/summer/v914       Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
                                → the actual listing

The heavy payload is immutable at a versioned URL, so it can be cached forever at every layer with no invalidation problem at all. The pointer is tiny and expires in five seconds, so a price change is visible within about five seconds — and stale-while-revalidate=60 means nobody waits for the refresh.

Three alternatives and their trade-offs.

  • Active purge. Most CDNs offer an invalidation API. It is precise and it does not reach browser caches, so the hour-long population remains. Good as a supplement, not a solution.
  • ETag with max-age=0, must-revalidate. Every request revalidates, so changes are immediate and unchanged responses cost a 304. You give up offloading requests entirely — the origin sees every one — which for a public catalogue may be too expensive.
  • Shorten max-age. Trades cache efficiency linearly for freshness and never actually solves the browser-cache tail.

The versioned-pointer pattern is worth internalising because it converts an invalidation problem — hard, and famously so — into a naming problem, which is easy.

SECTION 11

Idempotency, Retries and Rate Limits

This section is about what your API does when things go wrong on the network rather than in your code. Requests get lost, responses get lost, upstreams get slow, and clients respond to all three by trying again — often all at once. Everything here exists to make that survivable: to make a repeated request safe, to make retries polite, to stop a slow dependency from consuming your service, and to stop one caller from consuming everyone else's capacity.

11.1 Why Retries Are Inevitable

A client that sends a request and receives nothing cannot distinguish three situations:

The request never arrived. Nothing happened. Retrying is correct and necessary.
The request arrived and is still running. Retrying starts a second copy alongside the first.
The request completed and the response was lost. Retrying does the whole thing again.

There is no way to tell these apart from the outside — that is not an implementation gap, it is a property of asynchronous networks. So a client has exactly two options: retry and risk duplication, or do not retry and risk losing work that was never done. Both are wrong sometimes.

The way out is to make the duplicate harmless, which moves the problem from the client (who cannot solve it) to the server (who can). That is what the whole of §11.2 is for, and it is why §2.1's method properties matter: for GET, PUT and DELETE, HTTP already promises the duplicate is harmless, and every well-behaved client library already retries them automatically. For POST and PATCH, you have to build the promise yourself.

A retry budget is worth naming explicitly, because unlimited retries are how a small failure becomes an outage. Three retries with backoff is a reasonable default; retrying forever means that when your service recovers, it is immediately hit by every request that failed while it was down, plus the new ones.

11.2 Idempotency Keys

An idempotency key is a client-generated unique value — a UUID — sent with a non-idempotent request. The server records the key alongside the response, and any later request with the same key returns the stored response instead of doing the work again.

POST /payments HTTP/1.1
Idempotency-Key: 8f2c1a4e-9b3d-4c7f-a1e2-5d6b8c9f0a1b
Content-Type: application/json

{"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP", "source": "card_9f4a"}

This is the de-facto standard across the payments industry — Stripe, Adyen, PayPal and Square all implement it. Worth knowing precisely: it is not a standard. The IETF draft, draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header, reached revision 07 in October 2025 and has since expired without becoming an RFC. So the header name is conventional and the semantics are yours to specify — which means you must document them, because clients cannot look them up.

The state machine has four cases, and getting the third and fourth right is what separates a working implementation from a subtly broken one:

Key seen before? Same request body? Response
No — Process it. Store key → response. Return it
Yes, completed Yes Return the stored response, unchanged, including its status code
Yes, in progress Yes 409 Conflict — a request with this key is already running
Yes No 422 — the key is being reused for a different request 
import hashlib
import json

class IdempotencyStore:
    """key -> (request fingerprint, state, stored response)."""
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self.entries: dict[str, dict] = {}

    def begin(self, key: str, body: dict) -> tuple[str, object]:
        fingerprint = hashlib.sha256(
            json.dumps(body, sort_keys=True).encode()).hexdigest()[:12]
        entry = self.entries.get(key)
        if entry is None:
            self.entries[key] = {"fingerprint": fingerprint,
                                 "state": "in_progress", "response": None}
            return "proceed", None
        if entry["fingerprint"] != fingerprint:
            return "mismatch", (422, "this Idempotency-Key was used with a different body")
        if entry["state"] == "in_progress":
            return "in_progress", (409, "a request with this key is already running")
        return "replay", entry["response"]

    def finish(self, key: str, response: tuple) -> None:
        self.entries[key]["state"] = "completed"
        self.entries[key]["response"] = response

store = IdempotencyStore()
KEY = "8f2c1a4e"
payment = {"amount": 4250, "currency": "GBP"}

action, _ = store.begin(KEY, payment)
print("first  :", action)
print("during :", store.begin(KEY, payment)[0])          # retry while still running
store.finish(KEY, (201, {"id": "pay_71", "status": "succeeded"}))
action, replayed = store.begin(KEY, payment)
print("retry  :", action, replayed)
print("reused :", store.begin(KEY, {"amount": 9999, "currency": "GBP"})[1])

first  : proceed
during : in_progress
retry  : replay (201, {'id': 'pay_71', 'status': 'succeeded'})
reused : (422, 'this Idempotency-Key was used with a different body')

The replayed response is the original 201 with the original body — not a 200, not a fresh copy of the resource. That is the whole promise: the client cannot tell whether its first attempt or its fourth produced the result, which is exactly what makes retrying safe.

Six implementation requirements, each of which is a bug if missed:

  • Fingerprint the request body. Without it, a client that reuses a key by mistake gets someone else's response. With it, the mistake is a clear 422.
  • The key must be recorded in the same transaction as the work. Otherwise a crash between the two leaves the effect applied and the key unrecorded, and the retry does it again (§12.1).
  • Store the response, not a flag. The client needs the same body and status, including the resource ID it never received.
  • Expire keys — 24 hours is typical. They are a retry mechanism, not an audit log.
  • Scope keys per client. Two clients must not be able to collide, deliberately or otherwise.
  • Decide what happens when the first attempt failed. Storing a 500 and replaying it forever is unhelpful; the usual answer is to record only successful outcomes and deterministic client errors, and to let a 5xx be retried.

An alternative worth remembering, because it needs none of this machinery: let the client choose the URI. PUT /payments/{client-uuid} is idempotent by construction (§2.2, §3.7). It works whenever the client can mint a stable ID, and it is strictly simpler.

11.3 Exponential Backoff and Jitter

A client that retries immediately, at a fixed interval, does two harmful things: it hits a struggling service hardest exactly when it is least able to cope, and it synchronises with every other client doing the same, producing a thundering herd in which all retries land in the same instant.

Exponential backoff fixes the first: wait 1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, so a persistent failure produces geometrically fewer requests. Jitter fixes the second: randomise the delay so clients spread out instead of arriving together.

import random

def backoff(attempt: int, base: float = 1.0, cap: float = 30.0) -> float:
    """Exponential, capped."""
    return min(cap, base * 2 ** attempt)

def full_jitter(attempt: int, rng: random.Random, base: float = 1.0,
                cap: float = 30.0) -> float:
    """AWS's 'full jitter': uniform over [0, exponential]. Best spread."""
    return rng.uniform(0, backoff(attempt, base, cap))

def decorrelated(previous: float, rng: random.Random, base: float = 1.0,
                 cap: float = 30.0) -> float:
    """'Decorrelated jitter': grows from the last delay, never collapses to ~0."""
    return min(cap, rng.uniform(base, previous * 3))

rng = random.Random(20260823)
print("attempt  plain   full-jitter")
for attempt in range(6):
    print(f"   {attempt}    {backoff(attempt):5.1f}s   {full_jitter(attempt, rng):5.2f}s")

delay = 1.0
chain = []
for _ in range(6):
    delay = decorrelated(delay, rng)
    chain.append(round(delay, 2))
print("decorrelated chain:", chain)

# Ten clients failing at the same instant, retrying at attempt 3:
plain = [backoff(3) for _ in range(10)]
spread = [round(full_jitter(3, rng), 1) for _ in range(10)]
print("10 clients, plain :", sorted(set(plain)), "-> all in one instant")
print("10 clients, jitter:", sorted(spread))

attempt  plain   full-jitter
   0      1.0s    0.64s
   1      2.0s    1.00s
   2      4.0s    0.55s
   3      8.0s    2.34s
   4     16.0s    0.93s
   5     30.0s    9.11s
decorrelated chain: [2.24, 5.23, 5.45, 6.95, 14.42, 30.0]
10 clients, plain : [8.0] -> all in one instant
10 clients, jitter: [0.3, 1.5, 1.6, 2.5, 3.6, 3.7, 3.9, 4.0, 4.8, 6.8]

The last two lines are the argument for jitter in one image: without it, ten clients that failed together retry together, forever, in a synchronised wave. With it, the same ten spread across the whole window, and the service sees a trickle rather than a spike.

Three further rules for a retry policy:

  • Retry 5xx, 429 and transport errors. Never retry 4xx other than 429 — the request will fail identically, and you are only adding load.
  • Honour Retry-After when it is present. It is the server telling you the answer; your backoff calculation is a guess in comparison.
  • Cap total attempts and total elapsed time. "Three attempts or ten seconds, whichever comes first" bounds the damage. Unbounded retry is how a dependency's outage becomes your outage.

11.4 Timeouts and the Budget

Every outbound call needs a timeout, and the default in most HTTP clients is either "none" or "far too long". A call with no timeout is a way for someone else's slowness to become your outage: workers pile up waiting, the pool exhausts, and requests that have nothing to do with the slow dependency start failing.

Set four separately, because they fail differently:

Timeout Typical Guards against
Connect 1–2 s An unreachable or overloaded host
Read (per socket read) 5–10 s A host that accepted the connection and went quiet
Total request 10–30 s A response that trickles forever
Pool acquisition 1 s Waiting for a connection slot, invisibly

The idea that ties them together is the timeout budget. If your handler must answer in 5 seconds and it calls three upstreams in sequence, the sum of their timeouts must be under 5 seconds — with room left to serialise a response. A common failure is a chain where every hop is configured at 30 seconds: the caller gives up after 10, so the remaining 20 seconds of work at every level downstream is now being done for nobody.

Two rules that make budgets work:

  • Propagate the deadline. Pass the remaining time downstream — a header, a gRPC deadline, an asyncio timeout — so each hop knows how long it actually has. Then work whose result can no longer be delivered is abandoned rather than completed.
  • A timeout on a write is ambiguous. The request may have succeeded. This is exactly §11.1's third case, which is why a timeout on a non-idempotent call must be followed by either an idempotent retry (§11.2) or a status query, never by a blind repeat.

11.5 Circuit Breakers

Retries help with transient failures. When a dependency is properly down, they make things worse: every request waits for its timeout, occupying a worker, and the failing service is hammered while it tries to recover.

A circuit breaker stops that. It counts failures, and once they cross a threshold it opens — failing subsequent calls instantly, without attempting them — then periodically lets one through to see whether the dependency has recovered.

Closed — normal. Calls pass through; failures are counted over a rolling window.
Open — the failure rate crossed the threshold. Calls fail immediately with no network attempt. After a cooldown, move to half-open.
Half-open — let a small number of trial calls through. All succeed → closed. Any fails → open again, with a longer cooldown.

The value is not only that it protects the dependency. It protects you: an open circuit returns in microseconds instead of occupying a worker for the timeout, so the rest of your service keeps serving. That is the difference between one degraded feature and a total outage.

Two design decisions:

What does an open circuit return? Ideally not a 500. A cached value, a default, a partial response with the missing piece marked absent — graceful degradation is what turns a dependency outage into a smaller feature rather than an error page. Where there is no sensible fallback, 503 with Retry-After is honest.

What counts as a failure? Timeouts and 5xx, yes. 4xx, no — a 404 from an upstream means your request was wrong, not that the upstream is unwell, and counting it will trip the breaker on a bug.

Related and worth knowing by name: the bulkhead pattern gives each dependency its own connection pool and concurrency limit, so a slow one cannot consume the capacity the others need. Circuit breakers stop the bleeding; bulkheads contain it in the first place.

11.6 Rate Limiting

A rate limit protects your service from a caller consuming more than their share — deliberately or through a bug — and is OWASP API4:2023's principal control.

Four algorithms:

Algorithm How Character
Fixed window N per calendar minute Simple; allows a 2N burst across a boundary
Sliding window log timestamps of recent requests Exact; memory proportional to the limit
Sliding window counter weighted blend of two windows Good approximation, cheap. A common default
Token bucket tokens refill at a rate, each request costs one Allows controlled bursts. The most useful

Token bucket is usually the right answer, because real clients are bursty and a limit that forbids all bursts is hostile:

import math

class TokenBucket:
    def __init__(self, capacity: int, refill_per_second: float) -> None:
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.rate = refill_per_second
        self.tokens = float(capacity)
        self.updated = 0.0

    def take(self, now: float, cost: int = 1) -> tuple[bool, int, int]:
        self.tokens = min(self.capacity, self.tokens + (now - self.updated) * self.rate)
        self.updated = now
        if self.tokens >= cost:
            self.tokens -= cost
            return True, int(self.tokens), 0
        deficit = cost - self.tokens
        return False, 0, math.ceil(deficit / self.rate)

bucket = TokenBucket(capacity=5, refill_per_second=1.0)
for n in range(7):                       # a burst of 7 at t=0
    allowed, left, retry = bucket.take(now=0.0)
    print(f"t=0  request {n + 1}: {'allow' if allowed else 'DENY '} "
          f"remaining={left} retry-after={retry}")
allowed, left, retry = bucket.take(now=3.0)
print(f"t=3  after refill : {'allow' if allowed else 'DENY '} remaining={left}")

t=0  request 1: allow remaining=4 retry-after=0
t=0  request 2: allow remaining=3 retry-after=0
t=0  request 3: allow remaining=2 retry-after=0
t=0  request 4: allow remaining=1 retry-after=0
t=0  request 5: allow remaining=0 retry-after=0
t=0  request 6: DENY  remaining=0 retry-after=1
t=0  request 7: DENY  remaining=0 retry-after=1
t=3  after refill : allow remaining=2

A burst of five is absorbed instantly; the sixth is refused with a concrete Retry-After; three seconds later, three tokens have refilled and the caller is served again.

Tell the client where it stands. There are two spellings of this, and it is worth knowing which is which.

The widely-deployed de-facto convention is three separate headers, popularised by GitHub, Twitter and early IETF drafts:

HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests
RateLimit-Limit: 300
RateLimit-Remaining: 0
RateLimit-Reset: 47
Retry-After: 47
Content-Type: application/problem+json

The current IETF work replaced those three with two Structured Field headers. draft-ietf-httpapi-ratelimit-headers reached revision 11 in May 2026 and is still an Internet-Draft, not an RFC; it defines RateLimit (the remaining quota for a policy) and RateLimit-Policy (the policy itself), both in RFC 9651 Structured Fields syntax, with parameters for the quota, its units, the window and a partition key.

Since the draft is not stable and the three-header form is what clients and libraries actually parse today, the pragmatic choice is to send the three-header form and treat Retry-After as the authoritative signal — because Retry-After is the one that is genuinely standardised (RFC 9110 §10.2.3) and honoured by generic tooling. Revisit if and when the draft becomes an RFC.

Send RateLimit-Remaining on successful responses too, so a well-behaved client can slow down before being refused rather than discovering the limit by hitting it.

Four design decisions:

  • What is the key? Per API key for authenticated traffic; per IP for anonymous — knowing that IPs are shared by whole offices and mobile carriers, so an IP limit must be generous or it will block real users.
  • Different limits per endpoint. Login, password reset and search deserve much tighter limits than a cheap GET (§8.8).
  • Cost-weighted limits. A request that triggers a report is not one unit; charge it ten. The cost parameter above is there for exactly this.
  • Where does the state live? Per-instance limiting means the effective limit is N × instances. Shared state in Redis is correct and adds a hop; the usual compromise is approximate local limiting plus a precise global limit at the gateway.

11.7 Quotas and Backpressure

Rate limits are about speed. A quota is about volume over a period — 100,000 requests a month, 5 GB of storage, 1,000 report generations. They are commercial rather than protective, and they behave differently: exceeding a rate limit is temporary and self-healing, while exceeding a quota lasts until the period resets or the customer upgrades.

Which means a different response. 429 is right for a rate limit; for a quota, 403 with a problem type naming the quota is usually clearer, because Retry-After: 1209600 ("come back in a fortnight") is not a useful instruction. Whichever you choose, expose the quota state as a resource — GET /usage — so a customer can see it before hitting it, and warn at 80%.

Backpressure is the third member of the family and the one people skip. Rate limits protect against one caller; backpressure protects against everyone at once — a legitimate load spike, or a stampede after an outage. The principle is to refuse work you cannot do, immediately, rather than accepting it into a queue that grows without bound.

  • Bound every queue. An unbounded queue does not absorb load, it converts a fast failure into a slow one: requests wait, time out at the client, get retried, and the queue grows with work nobody is waiting for any more.
  • Shed load deliberately. When the queue is full or latency crosses a threshold, return 503 with Retry-After straight away. A fast 503 is far better than a 30-second timeout, for both parties.
  • Shed the right requests. If you must drop, drop low-priority traffic first — batch and background callers before interactive ones. This requires knowing which is which, so classify traffic at the edge.
  • Watch queue age, not just depth. A queue of 1,000 items moving in 50 ms is healthy; a queue of 10 items whose oldest entry is 30 seconds old is not.

The pattern to avoid has a name — the death spiral: latency rises, clients time out and retry, the retries add load, latency rises further. Bounded queues, load shedding, circuit breakers and jittered backoff are all the same defence against it, applied at different points.

Exercises

Exercise 11.1 — Specify the idempotency contract. Write the documentation for Idempotency-Key on POST /payments: header, key format, retention, and every response case including the awkward ones.

Solution

Idempotency on POST /payments

Send an Idempotency-Key header on every payment request. Generate a UUID v4 per logical payment — not per HTTP attempt — and reuse it for every retry of that payment. Keys are scoped to your account, so they cannot collide with another customer's.

We store the key with the response for 24 hours. After that the key is forgotten and a request carrying it is treated as new.

Situation Response
New key Processed normally. 201 Created with the payment
Same key, same body, first attempt finished The original response, byte for byte, including its status code
Same key, same body, first attempt still running 409 Conflict, problem type: idempotency-conflict. Retry after a short delay
Same key, different body 422, problem type: idempotency-key-reuse. The key is bound to the first body we saw
Same key, first attempt returned 5xx Treated as new — a failed attempt does not reserve the key
Key absent Processed normally, with no retry protection. Not recommended
Key longer than 255 characters 400

Bodies are compared by a hash of the canonical JSON, so key order and whitespace do not matter.

Three things this specification decides that an implementation would otherwise get wrong.

A key is per payment, not per attempt. This is the single most common client misunderstanding, and it defeats the mechanism entirely — a new key per attempt makes every retry a new payment.

A failed attempt does not reserve the key. Otherwise a transient 500 poisons the key for 24 hours and the client can never complete that payment.

The replay returns the original status code. A 200 where the client expected 201 breaks clients that branch on it, and it leaks the fact that this was a replay — which the client neither needs nor should depend on.

One thing to add that is not in the table: a client should also be able to GET /payments?idempotencyKey=... to recover a payment whose response it lost and whose key has expired. Retention is 24 hours; incidents last longer.

Exercise 11.2 — Design the retry policy. Your service calls a payment provider. Specify the timeouts, the retry rule, the backoff, the circuit breaker and the behaviour when the breaker is open. Say which failures must not be retried.

Solution

Timeouts. Connect 2 s; read 8 s; total 10 s; pool acquisition 1 s. The total is bounded by our own SLO: if we promise a 15-second checkout, 10 seconds for the provider leaves room for our own work and a response.

What to retry. Connection errors and 5xx and 429 — yes. Read timeouts — yes, but only with an idempotency key, because a read timeout on a payment is §11.1's ambiguous case and a blind retry can double-charge. 4xx other than 429 — never.

Backoff. Three attempts maximum, full jitter over an exponential base of 500 ms capped at 4 s, and a hard cap of 12 seconds total elapsed. Retry-After overrides the calculation whenever present.

Circuit breaker. Per provider endpoint. Open at a 50% failure rate over a rolling 20-request window with a minimum of 10 requests (so a single early failure cannot trip it). Cooldown 30 s, then half-open with 3 trial calls; any failure reopens with the cooldown doubled to a ceiling of 5 minutes. Timeouts and 5xx count as failures; 4xx does not.

When the breaker is open. Do not return 500. Accept the payment into a pending state, return 202 Accepted with a status URL (§12.4), and let a worker complete it when the breaker closes. The customer sees "processing" instead of "error", and no work is lost. Where an answer is genuinely required synchronously, 503 with Retry-After set to the remaining cooldown.

Must not be retried: any 4xx from the provider — 400 (our request is wrong), 401 (our credentials are wrong), 402 (declined — retrying a declined card is both futile and, at volume, a way to get flagged for card testing), 422. And any non-idempotent call for which we have no idempotency key, on a timeout: query the provider for the payment's status first, then decide.

One more thing that is easy to omit: an alert on the breaker opening. A breaker that silently absorbs a provider outage for six hours is a breaker that hid an incident.

Exercise 11.3 — Pick the limits. A public API has: GET /search (expensive), GET /orders/{id} (cheap), POST /orders (writes), POST /auth/token (authentication). Anonymous and authenticated callers both exist. Specify the limits, the keys, the headers and the response.

Solution
Endpoint Authenticated Anonymous Key Cost
GET /orders/{id} 600/min, burst 100 60/min API key / IP 1
GET /search 60/min, burst 10 10/min API key / IP 10
POST /orders 120/min, burst 20 n/a API key 5
POST /auth/token — 5/min per account, 20/min per IP both 1

One shared token bucket per caller, with weighted costs, rather than four independent limits: it is simpler to reason about, and it stops a caller from exhausting every endpoint's separate budget in parallel. Search costs ten tokens because it costs roughly ten times as much to serve.

POST /auth/token is keyed on both account and IP, and both must pass. Per-account stops credential stuffing against one victim; per-IP stops spraying across many accounts from one host. This endpoint gets the tightest limits in the API (§8.8) — and it should also apply exponential lockout per account after repeated failures, which is a separate control from rate limiting.

Headers on every response, not just refusals:

RateLimit-Limit: 600
RateLimit-Remaining: 447
RateLimit-Reset: 23

and on a refusal, additionally Retry-After and a problem document:

HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests
Retry-After: 12
Content-Type: application/problem+json
Cache-Control: no-store

{"type": "https://api.example.com/problems/rate-limited",
 "title": "Rate limit exceeded", "status": 429,
 "detail": "600 requests per minute for this API key. Try again in 12 seconds.",
 "limit": 600, "remaining": 0, "resetSeconds": 12, "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K"}

Two things that are easy to get wrong. Anonymous limits keyed on IP must be generous, because a corporate NAT or a mobile carrier puts thousands of real users behind one address — and if you cannot make them generous, require authentication instead of blocking real traffic. And Cache-Control: no-store on the 429 matters: a cached rate-limit response keeps refusing a client that is no longer over the limit (§10.1).

Exercise 11.4 — Break the death spiral. After a database failover, your API stays down for 40 minutes even though the database recovered in 90 seconds. Reconstruct what happened, then give four changes that would each independently have shortened it.

Solution

The reconstruction.

  1. The failover takes 90 seconds. Every in-flight request blocks on a database call with no timeout, or a 30-second one.
  2. Workers fill up waiting. New requests queue behind them in an unbounded queue.
  3. Clients time out at 10 seconds and retry. Because there is no jitter, they retry in a synchronised wave — and because there is no attempt cap, they keep doing it.
  4. The database comes back. It is immediately hit by 90 seconds of accumulated queue plus every retry wave, which is several times normal load.
  5. It saturates. Requests slow, clients time out, and more retries arrive. The queue never drains, because work is arriving faster than it completes — and much of the queued work is for clients who gave up minutes ago.
  6. The system stays in that state until something breaks the cycle: a deploy, a restart, or an operator turning traffic off.

The key insight is that step 5 is self-sustaining. Nothing about the original fault is still present; the load is now entirely generated by the response to the fault.

Four independent fixes.

A bounded queue with load shedding. Cap the accepted queue at, say, 100 requests, and return 503 with Retry-After immediately when it is full. The service now fails fast instead of accumulating work nobody is waiting for, and it recovers the instant the database does — because there is no backlog to drain. This is the single highest-value change (§11.7).

A circuit breaker on the database. After a threshold of failures it opens, and every request fails in microseconds instead of occupying a worker for 30 seconds. Workers stay free, the service keeps answering (503, or degraded responses from cache), and the trial calls in half-open detect recovery within seconds (§11.5).

Jittered backoff with an attempt cap, on every client. Three attempts with full jitter turns a synchronised wave into a spread trickle and bounds the total retry load at roughly 3× rather than unbounded. Your own SDK should ship this by default, because most clients will use whatever you give them (§11.3).

Aggressive timeouts and deadline propagation. A 2-second database timeout means a worker is blocked for 2 seconds, not 30 — a 15× improvement in how fast the pool recovers. Propagating the client's deadline means work whose result can no longer be delivered is abandoned rather than completed (§11.4).

Two things that would not have helped, and are the usual first suggestions: adding more workers (they fill up too, and hit the database harder), and increasing the timeout (it makes each worker useless for longer). Both increase the amount of load the system generates while failing, which is precisely the wrong direction.

And one operational control worth having: a way to shed all traffic at the edge, so an operator can break the cycle deliberately in ten seconds rather than by restarting the fleet. Recovery is much easier from zero load than from saturation.

SECTION 12

Transactions and the Asynchronous Boundary

The hardest question in backend work is did it happen? — and it becomes hard the moment one request has to change two things that are not in the same database. This section is about the boundary between the synchronous request and everything that must happen around it: where a transaction starts and stops, why writing to a database and a queue in the same handler is a bug rather than a technique, how to answer a request whose work takes ten minutes, and how to deliver an event to someone else's server without losing it or sending it twice.

12.1 Where a Transaction Begins and Ends

A transaction gives you atomicity — all of it or none of it — and the design question is not whether to use one but how much to put inside it. Two failure modes, in opposite directions.

Too narrow and you have lost atomicity where you needed it. The classic version is check-then-act across two statements: read the balance, decide, write the new balance. Between the read and the write, anything can happen.

Too wide and the transaction holds locks while doing something slow — calling an HTTP API, sending an email, waiting on a queue. Locks are held for the duration of someone else's outage, and connections in your pool are consumed by requests that are not doing database work.

Three rules that settle most cases:

  • Never make a network call inside a transaction. Not to a payment provider, not to a queue, not to a cache. If the call takes 30 seconds, so does the lock.
  • Re-check state-dependent rules inside the transaction, not before it. Validation at the boundary (§6.1) proves the request is well-formed. It cannot prove the seat is still free, and a check performed before BEGIN is a check performed at a moment that has passed.
  • Prefer one statement to a read-modify-write. UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = ? AND balance >= 100 does the check and the write atomically, and the affected-row count tells you which happened. It needs no lock you have to remember to take.
import sqlite3

db = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
db.execute("CREATE TABLE accounts (id TEXT PRIMARY KEY, balance INTEGER NOT NULL)")
db.execute("INSERT INTO accounts VALUES ('a1', 10000)")
db.commit()

def withdraw_racy(conn, account_id: str, cents: int) -> str:
    balance = conn.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id = ?",
                           (account_id,)).fetchone()[0]
    if balance < cents:                       # decision made here...
        return "declined"
    conn.execute("UPDATE accounts SET balance = ? WHERE id = ?",
                 (balance - cents, account_id))     # ...acted on here. A gap.
    return "ok"

def withdraw_atomic(conn, account_id: str, cents: int) -> str:
    cursor = conn.execute(
        "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - ? WHERE id = ? AND balance >= ?",
        (cents, account_id, cents))
    return "ok" if cursor.rowcount == 1 else "declined"

# Two concurrent withdrawals of 8000 from a balance of 10000, interleaved as a
# real race would interleave them: both read before either writes.
b1 = db.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id='a1'").fetchone()[0]
b2 = db.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id='a1'").fetchone()[0]
print("both read:", b1, b2, "-> both decide they can afford 8000:", b1 >= 8000, b2 >= 8000)
db.execute("UPDATE accounts SET balance = ? WHERE id='a1'", (b1 - 8000,))
db.execute("UPDATE accounts SET balance = ? WHERE id='a1'", (b2 - 8000,))
print("racy result  :", db.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts").fetchone()[0])

db.execute("UPDATE accounts SET balance = 10000 WHERE id='a1'")
print("atomic first :", withdraw_atomic(db, "a1", 8000),
      db.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts").fetchone()[0])
print("atomic second:", withdraw_atomic(db, "a1", 8000),
      db.execute("SELECT balance FROM accounts").fetchone()[0])

both read: 10000 10000 -> both decide they can afford 8000: True True
racy result  : 2000
atomic first : ok 2000
atomic second: declined 2000

The racy version leaves the balance at 2000 after withdrawing 16000 from 10000 — money created from nothing, and no error anywhere. The atomic version declines the second withdrawal because the predicate is evaluated by the database at write time, in the same statement. Where the operation is genuinely too complex for one statement, the equivalents are SELECT ... FOR UPDATE (pessimistic) or a version column with WHERE version = ? (optimistic, and the same mechanism as §10.6).

12.2 The Request Is Not the Unit of Work

A tempting default is to wrap every request in a transaction: open at the start of the handler, commit at the end. It is convenient, and it produces two problems.

It puts non-database work inside the transaction. Serialising a response, calling an upstream, rendering a template — all now hold locks.

It makes the transaction boundary invisible. Nobody reading the handler can see where atomicity begins or what it covers, so nobody notices when a third-party call sneaks inside it.

The better arrangement makes the boundary explicit and as small as the invariant requires, and puts it in the application layer rather than the transport layer (§1.6):

handler:           parse → authorize → call use case → serialise
use case:          load → decide → [ BEGIN … write … COMMIT ] → publish/notify

Two consequences worth internalising. Everything after COMMIT is a place where a crash loses the follow-up work but keeps the write — which is exactly the problem §12.3 solves. And a use case that needs two transactions has admitted that its operation is not atomic; that is often fine, but it should be a decision rather than an accident, and the intermediate state needs a name (pending, awaiting_capture) and a way to recover from it.

12.3 The Dual-Write Problem and the Outbox

Here is the bug at the centre of most event-driven systems:

def place_order_broken(db, queue, order):
    db.execute("INSERT INTO orders ...")      # (1)
    db.commit()
    queue.publish("order.placed", order)      # (2) — crash here and the event is lost

Two writes to two systems, with no transaction spanning them. Crash between them and the order exists but nothing downstream ever hears about it: no confirmation email, no warehouse pick, no analytics row. Swap the order of the two and you get the opposite — an event for an order that does not exist.

Distributed transactions (two-phase commit) technically solve this and are almost never worth it: they need coordinator support your queue probably lacks, they hold locks across the network, and a coordinator failure blocks participants.

The practical solution is the transactional outbox. Write the event into a table in the same transaction as the business data, then have a separate process read the table and publish. Now there is only one write, so there is nothing to be inconsistent about.

import json
import sqlite3

db = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
db.executescript("""
CREATE TABLE orders (id TEXT PRIMARY KEY, total INTEGER NOT NULL);
CREATE TABLE outbox (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
    topic TEXT NOT NULL,
    payload TEXT NOT NULL,
    published_at TEXT
);
""")

def place_order(conn, order_id: str, total: int) -> None:
    """One transaction. The order and the intent to publish commit together."""
    with conn:                                   # BEGIN … COMMIT (or ROLLBACK)
        conn.execute("INSERT INTO orders VALUES (?, ?)", (order_id, total))
        conn.execute("INSERT INTO outbox (topic, payload) VALUES (?, ?)",
                     ("order.placed", json.dumps({"id": order_id, "total": total})))

def relay(conn, publish, batch: int = 10) -> int:
    """A separate process: read unpublished rows, publish, mark them done."""
    rows = conn.execute(
        "SELECT id, topic, payload FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL "
        "ORDER BY id LIMIT ?", (batch,)).fetchall()
    sent = 0
    for row_id, topic, payload in rows:
        publish(topic, json.loads(payload))      # may fail — the row stays unpublished
        conn.execute("UPDATE outbox SET published_at = datetime('now') WHERE id = ?",
                     (row_id,))
        conn.commit()
        sent += 1
    return sent

published: list[tuple] = []
place_order(db, "ord_1", 4250)
place_order(db, "ord_2", 9900)
print("orders committed:", db.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM orders").fetchone()[0])
print("pending in outbox:",
      db.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL").fetchone()[0])

# A failed order rolls back BOTH the row and the event — no orphan either way.
try:
    with db:
        db.execute("INSERT INTO orders VALUES ('ord_3', 100)")
        db.execute("INSERT INTO outbox (topic, payload) VALUES ('order.placed', '{}')")
        raise RuntimeError("payment declined after the insert")
except RuntimeError as exc:
    print("rolled back:", exc)
print("orders now:", db.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM orders").fetchone()[0],
      "outbox now:", db.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM outbox").fetchone()[0])

print("relay published:", relay(db, lambda topic, body: published.append((topic, body))))
print("delivered:", published)

orders committed: 2
pending in outbox: 2
rolled back: payment declined after the insert
orders now: 2 outbox now: 2
relay published: 2
delivered: [('order.placed', {'id': 'ord_1', 'total': 4250}), ('order.placed', {'id': 'ord_2', 'total': 9900})]

The rollback line is the point: the failed order left neither a row nor an event. There is no window in which the two systems can disagree, because there is only one system at write time.

Four things the outbox gives you, and one it does not:

  • Ordering, if you publish in id order and process one batch at a time.
  • Durability — an event survives a crash of the relay, because it is a committed row.
  • Replay — the table is a log; re-publishing is an UPDATE away.
  • Simplicity — no coordinator, no new infrastructure.
  • Not exactly-once. The relay may publish and crash before marking the row, so the event goes out twice. Which is §12.5's subject, and is why consumers must be idempotent.

Two operational notes: the relay must take a lock (or use SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED) so two instances do not publish the same row, and published rows need a retention policy or the table grows forever. The alternative to a polling relay is change data capture — reading the database's replication log directly, as Debezium does — which removes the polling latency at the cost of new infrastructure.

12.4 Long-Running Operations

Some operations cannot finish inside a request: generating a report, transcoding a video, running an import, provisioning a resource. Holding the connection open is wrong — it ties up a worker, it will hit some proxy's timeout, and a dropped connection loses the result.

The pattern is a long-running operation: create a resource representing the work, return 202 Accepted immediately, and let the client follow it.

POST /reports HTTP/1.1
Content-Type: application/json

{"quarter": "2026-Q1", "format": "csv"}

HTTP/1.1 202 Accepted
Location: /reports/f81c
Retry-After: 5

{"id": "f81c", "status": "pending", "createdAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"}

GET /reports/f81c HTTP/1.1

HTTP/1.1 200 OK

{"id": "f81c", "status": "succeeded", "progress": 100,
 "resultUrl": "https://files.example.com/reports/f81c.csv?...",
 "completedAt": "2026-03-04T10:04:12Z"}

The design decisions that make this pleasant to use:

  • The operation is a real resource, so it can be listed, cancelled (DELETE /reports/f81c), retried and audited. §3.7's "find the noun" applied to an asynchronous verb.
  • Retry-After on the 202 tells the client how often to poll. Without it, clients poll every 100 ms.
  • Report progress when you can. It is the difference between "working" and "hung" for the person watching.
  • A terminal failure is a 200 with "status": "failed" and a problem document inside, not a 500. The request to check the status succeeded; the work did not.
  • The result is a separate URL — often a presigned one (§4.7) — so the status resource stays small and the payload can be re-downloaded without regenerating.
  • Make creation idempotent (§11.2), or a client that retries a lost 202 starts a second report.

Polling is the simplest option and is right most of the time. The alternatives — a webhook (§12.7) or an SSE stream (§13.3) — remove the polling latency and add delivery machinery; offer them in addition, not instead, because a client that missed the callback still needs a way to ask.

12.5 Queues, Workers and At-Least-Once Delivery

A message queue decouples the request from the work: the handler enqueues and returns, a worker picks the message up and does it. It buys you throughput smoothing, retry with backoff, and isolation between the request path and slow work. A message queue is also a durability boundary — once the message is accepted, the work survives the crash of whatever produced it — and that is usually the property that justifies introducing one.

The property to understand before anything else is the delivery guarantee.

Guarantee Reality
At-most-once Fire and forget. Messages are lost on failure. Rarely what you want
At-least-once Delivered until acknowledged. Duplicates are guaranteed to happen
Exactly-once Not achievable end-to-end across systems. Vendors that claim it mean at-least-once delivery plus deduplication somewhere

Essentially every real queue is at-least-once, and the reason is §11.1 again: a consumer that processes a message and crashes before acknowledging it will be given the message again, and the broker cannot know the work was done.

So the rule is unavoidable: every consumer must be idempotent. Three ways, in decreasing order of preference:

  • Make the operation naturally idempotent. UPDATE orders SET status='shipped' WHERE id=? is safe to run twice. Design for this first; it needs no bookkeeping.
  • Deduplicate on a message ID. Record processed IDs in a table with a unique constraint, in the same transaction as the effect. This is §11.2 applied to messages.
  • Use a conditional write. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING, or a version check, so the second attempt is a no-op.

Six more rules that separate a working queue consumer from one that will page you:

  • Acknowledge after the work, never before. Acknowledging first converts at-least-once into at-most-once and loses messages silently.
  • Keep messages small and put a reference in them, not the payload. A message is not a database; a 5 MB message is a queue outage waiting to happen.
  • Version your message schema and make consumers tolerant of unknown fields — producer and consumer deploy at different times, always (§14.4).
  • Set a visibility timeout longer than the work takes, or the broker redelivers a message that is still being processed and you get genuine concurrent duplicates.
  • Bound concurrency per consumer. A worker pool with no limit will exhaust the database connection pool the moment the queue backs up.
  • Monitor queue age, not just depth (§11.7). Depth tells you how much work there is; age tells you whether you are keeping up.

12.6 Dead Letters and Poison Messages

Some messages can never succeed: a malformed payload, a reference to a deleted row, a bug that throws on one specific input. Retried forever, such a poison message blocks the queue (if ordered) or burns capacity indefinitely (if not).

A dead letter queue is where they go: after N failed attempts, the broker moves the message aside, out of the main flow, and keeps it for inspection.

The policy worth adopting:

  • Retry with backoff first — 3 to 5 attempts, exponentially spaced (§11.3). Most failures are transient.
  • Then dead-letter, with the failure context attached: the exception, the attempt count, the timestamps, the original message.
  • Alert on the dead-letter queue being non-empty. A dead-letter queue nobody watches is a silent data-loss mechanism, and this is the single most common failure of the pattern.
  • Make replay a one-command operation. After the bug is fixed, you need to reprocess. If that requires a bespoke script written under pressure, it will be done badly.
  • Distinguish permanent from transient failures at the consumer. A validation error should dead-letter on the first attempt — retrying it four times is pure waste and delays the alert.

One subtlety that catches people: with an ordered queue, dead-lettering a message means the messages behind it are now processed out of order relative to it. If order matters for that key — three updates to the same order — you must either halt the partition or accept the reordering. Deciding which, in advance, is much easier than deciding it during an incident.

12.7 Webhooks

A webhook inverts the direction: instead of clients polling you, you POST to a URL they registered. It is the standard way to tell someone else's system that something happened, and it is a distributed-systems problem wearing a simple costume — you are now a client calling an endpoint you do not control, with all of §11 applying to you.

Signing comes first, because an unsigned webhook is an unauthenticated POST from the internet, and receivers have no way to know it came from you.

import hashlib
import hmac
import json

SECRET = b"whsec_2f6c1a9b4d8e0f3a5c7b9d1e3f5a7c9b"

def sign(payload: dict, timestamp: int) -> str:
    body = json.dumps(payload, separators=(",", ":"), sort_keys=True)
    signed = f"{timestamp}.{body}".encode()          # timestamp INSIDE the signature
    return hmac.new(SECRET, signed, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()

def verify(payload: dict, timestamp: int, signature: str, now: int,
           tolerance: int = 300) -> tuple[bool, str]:
    if abs(now - timestamp) > tolerance:
        return False, "timestamp outside tolerance — replay"
    expected = sign(payload, timestamp)
    if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, signature):
        return False, "signature mismatch"
    return True, "accepted"

event = {"id": "evt_9f4a", "type": "order.placed", "data": {"orderId": "ord_1"}}
sent_at = 1_772_000_000
signature = sign(event, sent_at)

print("genuine    :", verify(event, sent_at, signature, now=sent_at + 3))
print("replayed   :", verify(event, sent_at, signature, now=sent_at + 4000))
tampered = {**event, "data": {"orderId": "ord_999"}}
print("tampered   :", verify(tampered, sent_at, signature, now=sent_at + 3))
print("header form:", f"t={sent_at},v1={signature[:16]}…")

genuine    : (True, 'accepted')
replayed   : (False, 'timestamp outside tolerance — replay')
tampered   : (False, 'signature mismatch')
header form: t=1772000000,v1=809a22f9452497d8…

The timestamp is inside the signed material, not merely alongside it — otherwise an attacker replays an old, validly-signed event with a fresh timestamp. That is the detail most home-grown webhook signing gets wrong.

The rest of the contract you owe a receiver:

  • A stable event ID, so the receiver can deduplicate. Webhook delivery is at-least-once for the same reason everything else is, and the receiver's documentation should say so plainly.
  • Retry with backoff on any non-2xx, over hours rather than seconds — receivers have outages too — and stop after a bounded number of attempts.
  • Treat any 2xx as success and nothing else as success. A receiver that returns 302 should be treated as a failure, not followed.
  • A short timeout on your side, 5–10 seconds, and documentation telling receivers to acknowledge immediately and process asynchronously. A receiver that does its work inside your request will time out and get duplicates.
  • A delivery log the customer can see, with attempts, response codes and a manual replay button. This removes most webhook support tickets.
  • Rotatable secrets, with two valid at once during rotation — the §3.5 lesson again.
  • A stable source IP range or mTLS, so receivers can allowlist you.

And the honest caveat: webhooks are the least reliable integration you offer, because delivery depends on someone else's uptime. Always provide a polling alternative — a GET /events?since=... change feed (§7.7) — so a receiver that missed a day can catch up without asking you to replay.

Exercises

Exercise 12.1 — Fix the dual write. This handler loses events. Show the two crash windows, then fix it two different ways and say which you would ship.

async def cancel_order(order_id: str) -> dict:
    order = await db.fetch_one("SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = $1", order_id)
    await db.execute("UPDATE orders SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = $1", order_id)
    await queue.publish("order.cancelled", {"id": order_id})
    await email.send(order["email"], "Your order was cancelled")
    return {"status": "cancelled"}
Solution

Crash window 1 — between the UPDATE and the publish. The order is cancelled and nothing downstream knows: the warehouse still ships it, the analytics never sees it, and no compensating action ever runs.

Crash window 2 — between the publish and the email. The event went out but the customer was never told. Worse, if the handler is retried the event is published again, so downstream consumers see two cancellations for one order.

There is also a third defect that is not a crash: email.send is a network call in the request path, so a slow mail provider makes cancellation slow, and a failing one makes it fail — after the order is already cancelled in the database.

Fix A — transactional outbox (§12.3):

async def cancel_order(order_id: str) -> dict:
    async with db.transaction():
        updated = await db.execute(
            "UPDATE orders SET status='cancelled' WHERE id=$1 AND status='pending'",
            order_id)
        if updated == 0:
            raise Conflict("order is not pending")
        await db.execute(
            "INSERT INTO outbox (topic, payload) VALUES ($1, $2)",
            "order.cancelled", json.dumps({"id": order_id}))
    return {"status": "cancelled"}

The email is no longer here at all: it becomes a consumer of order.cancelled, which is where it belonged. The relay publishes the event, a notification worker sends the mail, and a mail outage delays a message instead of failing a cancellation.

Fix B — make the state the source of truth and derive the work. Drop the outbox; have a poller select orders in cancelled state with notified_at IS NULL, do the work, and stamp the column. Simpler, and it works when the event is genuinely a function of the row.

Which to ship: A. Fix B needs a bespoke poller and column per event type, and it only works for events that correspond to a durable state change — it cannot express "the customer clicked resend". The outbox is one table and one relay for every event in the system. Fix B is a reasonable local answer when you have exactly one such event and no queue infrastructure yet.

Note what both fixes share: exactly one write inside the transaction, and everything else derived from it afterwards. That is the whole technique.

Exercise 12.2 — Design the long-running operation. A CSV import of up to 2 GB takes 5–40 minutes. Design the complete API: endpoints, status model, progress, cancellation, failure, and what happens if the client disappears.

Solution

Endpoints.

POST   /imports                      -> 201 + presigned upload URL   (§4.7)
POST   /imports/{id}/completion      -> 202 + Location: /imports/{id}
GET    /imports/{id}                 -> the operation resource
GET    /imports/{id}/errors          -> paginated row-level failures  (§7)
DELETE /imports/{id}                 -> request cancellation
GET    /imports?status=running       -> list, so a client can recover lost IDs

Status model, an explicit state machine so every transition is legal or refused:

awaiting_upload → validating → running → succeeded
                            ↘ failed
                  running   → cancelling → cancelled

The resource.

{
  "id": "imp_f81c",
  "status": "running",
  "progress": {"rowsTotal": 1840221, "rowsProcessed": 903118, "rowsFailed": 47},
  "createdAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z",
  "startedAt": "2026-03-04T10:02:40Z",
  "estimatedCompletion": "2026-03-04T10:19:00Z",
  "errorsUrl": "/imports/imp_f81c/errors"
}

Progress is a count rather than a percentage, because the total is known after validation and a count degrades honestly when it is not. estimatedCompletion is worth including and worth labelling as an estimate.

Cancellation is a request, not a command: DELETE moves the operation to cancelling and returns 202. The worker checks a cancellation flag between batches and stops at a batch boundary, leaving the already-imported rows in place — and the resource says how many that was, because a half-import the client cannot see the extent of is worse than no import.

Failure is GET /imports/{id} returning 200 with "status": "failed" and an embedded problem document (§5.2). The status check succeeded; the work did not. Row-level failures live in /errors because there may be 40,000 of them, which is a paginated collection, not a field.

If the client disappears — which is the normal case for a 40-minute job — nothing changes. The work is owned by the worker and the operation resource, not by the connection. The client (or a different one) can GET the resource any time, and GET /imports?status=running lets it recover an ID it lost. Retention: keep the resource for 30 days after completion so it stays auditable, and expire the uploaded object sooner.

Two things that make it robust. The POST /imports accepts an Idempotency-Key (§11.2), so a retried creation does not start a second import. And the worker checkpoints its progress — the row offset — so a worker crash resumes rather than restarts a 40-minute job.

Exercise 12.3 — Make the consumer idempotent. This worker sends a duplicate email whenever the broker redelivers. Fix it, and explain why moving the acknowledgement earlier is not a fix.

async def on_order_placed(message):
    order = await orders.get(message["orderId"])
    await email.send(order.email, f"Order {order.id} confirmed")
    await message.ack()
Solution

Why the early-ack "fix" is wrong. Acknowledging before sending converts at-least-once into at-most-once: a crash after the ack and before the send loses the email permanently, with no record that it was ever due. You have traded a duplicate email — mildly annoying — for a missing one, which the customer notices and support cannot explain. Always acknowledge after the work.

The fix: record the delivery in the same transaction as the effect.

async def on_order_placed(message):
    event_id = message["id"]                       # a stable, producer-assigned id
    async with db.transaction():
        inserted = await db.execute(
            "INSERT INTO processed_events (event_id, consumer) VALUES ($1, 'email') "
            "ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING",
            event_id)
        if inserted == 0:
            await message.ack()                    # already handled; ack and move on
            return
        order = await orders.get(message["orderId"])
        await outbox_email(order.email, f"Order {order.id} confirmed")
    await message.ack()

Two things make this work. The unique constraint on (event_id, consumer) is what makes the check atomic — two concurrent deliveries of the same message cannot both pass it, which an if exists check before an insert cannot promise.
