And the email is queued through an outbox rather than sent inline, so the send and the dedup record commit together; sending inline re-introduces the dual write of §12.3 one level down.

Three details that matter in production. consumer is in the key because a second consumer of the same event must not be deduplicated by the first one's row. processed_events needs a retention policy — delete rows older than the broker's maximum redelivery window, or the table grows forever. And the producer must assign the event ID: a broker-generated delivery ID differs per delivery and deduplicates nothing.

The better fix, where it is available: make the operation naturally idempotent so no bookkeeping is needed. If the email were instead "ensure a confirmation exists for this order", it could be an upsert keyed on the order, and duplicates would collapse on their own.

Exercise 12.4 — Review the webhook implementation. Find five defects.

async def deliver(subscription, event):
    body = json.dumps(event)
    signature = hashlib.sha256((SECRET + body).encode()).hexdigest()
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(follow_redirects=True) as client:
        r = await client.post(subscription.url, content=body,
                              headers={"X-Signature": signature})
    if r.status_code != 200:
        await asyncio.sleep(1)
        await deliver(subscription, event)
Solution

1. The signature is a hash, not an HMAC. sha256(secret + body) is vulnerable to a length-extension attack against SHA-256's Merkle–Damgård construction: an attacker who has one valid signature can compute a valid signature for an extended body without knowing the secret. Use hmac.new(SECRET, body, sha256), which is built for exactly this.

2. No timestamp, so every delivery is replayable forever. A captured request can be re-sent at any time and will verify. Sign f"{timestamp}.{body}" and have receivers reject anything outside a tolerance (§12.7).

3. Unbounded recursive retry with no backoff. One failing subscriber produces an infinite loop of one-second retries — and recursion means it also grows the stack until the process dies. It needs an attempt cap, exponential backoff with jitter (§11.3), and an iterative loop or a queue.

4. follow_redirects=True turns your signed, authenticated POST into an SSRF primitive: a subscriber registers a URL that redirects to 169.254.169.254 or to an internal service, and your server makes the request from inside the perimeter (§6.5). Disable redirects, and vet the registered URL by resolved address.

5. != 200 treats every other 2xx as a failure. A receiver answering 202 Accepted — which is the correct response for "queued, will process asynchronously" — gets retried indefinitely and receives duplicates. Accept any 2xx.

Also missing, and worth adding: no timeout on the request, so one slow subscriber occupies a worker forever; no stable event ID in the payload, so receivers cannot deduplicate; no Content-Type; no dead-lettering after the retries are exhausted (§12.6); and the signature header should carry a version prefix (t=...,v1=...) so the scheme can be changed later without breaking every receiver at once.

SECTION 13

Streaming Responses

Everything so far has assumed a request produces one response, assembled and then sent. Three situations break that assumption: the response is too large to hold in memory, the response is not finished yet, and the interaction is genuinely bidirectional. HTTP has an answer for each, and choosing correctly between chunked responses, server-sent events and WebSockets saves a great deal of infrastructure you would otherwise have to build.

13.1 When One Object Is the Wrong Shape

Three signals that a normal response is not going to work:

Size. A 900 MB export cannot be built in memory. Serialising it into a list first is an out-of-memory error waiting for a big enough customer.
Time. The first row is available in 20 ms and the last in 40 seconds. Waiting for all of it means the client stares at nothing for 40 seconds; sending as you go means it renders immediately.
Open-endedness. There is no last item — a live feed, a log tail, progress on a running job. The request is a subscription, not a question.

Note what is not on that list. "The client wants updates" is often better served by polling a normal endpoint (§12.4), and "the response is a bit slow" is better served by fixing the query. Streaming adds real complexity — buffering intermediaries, error handling after the status line, connection limits — so reach for it when one of the three above genuinely applies.

13.2 Streaming Responses

Streaming in the plain HTTP sense means sending the body incrementally, using chunked transfer encoding in HTTP/1.1 or DATA frames in HTTP/2. The client can start processing before the server has finished producing.

The format has to be one a reader can consume incrementally, which rules out a single JSON array — a parser cannot yield the first element until it has seen the closing bracket. Two formats that work:

NDJSON  (application/x-ndjson)     one JSON object per line
{"id":"ord_1","total":4250}
{"id":"ord_2","total":9900}

CSV     (text/csv)                 one record per line, header first
id,total
ord_1,4250

Three consequences that catch people out:

You cannot change your mind about the status code. The status line goes out with the first byte. If the query fails after 200,000 rows, you have already sent 200 OK, and there is no way to retract it. The convention is to end the stream with an error record and document that consumers must check for it — an NDJSON stream whose last line is {"error": {...}}. A truncated stream with no such line is a failure the client must treat as one.

Content-Length is unknown, so no progress bar and no Range support. If the client needs those, generate the file to object storage and hand back a URL (§4.7, §12.4).

Intermediaries buffer. Nginx's proxy_buffering is on by default and will hold your carefully-incremental output until it has a full buffer, destroying the latency benefit. X-Accel-Buffering: no disables it for Nginx; other proxies have their own switch, and a CDN in between may buffer regardless.

And one rule that is easy to violate accidentally: do not hold a database transaction open for the length of the stream. Streaming a 40-second export from inside a transaction holds locks for 40 seconds. Use a server-side cursor outside a write transaction, or page the query.

13.3 Server-Sent Events

Server-sent events are a small, well-specified protocol for server-to-client push over an ordinary HTTP response: Content-Type: text/event-stream, one event per blank-line-delimited block. The browser API is EventSource, and it handles reconnection for you.

import asyncio
import httpx
from fastapi import FastAPI
from fastapi.responses import StreamingResponse

app = FastAPI()

async def events():
    """Each event is `field: value` lines, terminated by a blank line."""
    for n in range(1, 4):
        yield f'id: {n}\nevent: progress\ndata: {{"done": {n * 33}}}\n\n'
        await asyncio.sleep(0)
    yield 'id: 4\nevent: complete\ndata: {"done": 100}\n\n'

@app.get("/imports/imp_1/events")
async def stream() -> StreamingResponse:
    return StreamingResponse(
        events(),
        media_type="text/event-stream",
        headers={"Cache-Control": "no-store",       # never cache a live stream
                 "X-Accel-Buffering": "no"})        # tell nginx not to buffer

async def main() -> None:
    transport = httpx.ASGITransport(app=app)
    async with httpx.AsyncClient(transport=transport, base_url="http://x") as client:
        async with client.stream("GET", "/imports/imp_1/events") as response:
            print(response.status_code, response.headers["content-type"])
            async for line in response.aiter_lines():
                if line:
                    print("  ", line)

asyncio.run(main())

200 text/event-stream; charset=utf-8
   id: 1
   event: progress
   data: {"done": 33}
   id: 2
   event: progress
   data: {"done": 66}
   id: 3
   event: progress
   data: {"done": 99}
   id: 4
   event: complete
   data: {"done": 100}

Four fields are defined, and each earns its place:

Field Purpose
data: the payload. Repeat the field for a multi-line payload
event: a named type, so the client can dispatch on it
id: the last-seen position, which the browser sends back as Last-Event-ID on reconnect
retry: how many milliseconds to wait before reconnecting

id plus Last-Event-ID is the feature that makes SSE genuinely useful. When the connection drops — and it will — the browser reconnects automatically and tells you where it got to, so you can resume rather than restart. Implementing that means keeping a short replay buffer of recent events, and it is what turns "push updates" into "push updates without losing any".

Four operational realities:

  • Send a heartbeat. A comment line (: ping\n\n) every 15–30 seconds keeps proxies and load balancers from closing an idle connection, and lets the client detect a dead one.
  • Browsers allow only about six concurrent HTTP/1.1 connections per origin — it is a browser convention rather than a protocol rule, but a universal one — and an open SSE stream occupies one for its whole life. Two tabs with two streams each is most of the budget. HTTP/2 multiplexes and removes the problem, which is a real reason to be on HTTP/2 before relying on SSE.
  • Each open stream is a held connection on your side too. Ten thousand subscribers is ten thousand connections; this is a capacity-planning number, and it is why async frameworks matter here.
  • EventSource cannot set headers, so it cannot send Authorization. Authenticate with a cookie, or a short-lived token in the query string — accepting the §8.1 caveat about URLs in logs, and mitigating it by making the token single-use and brief.

13.4 WebSockets

A WebSocket starts as an HTTP request with Upgrade: websocket, gets 101 Switching Protocols, and from then on the connection is a full-duplex, message-oriented channel that is no longer HTTP at all.

GET /ws HTTP/1.1
Upgrade: websocket
Connection: Upgrade
Sec-WebSocket-Key: dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==
Sec-WebSocket-Version: 13

That last sentence is the important one: once upgraded, you have left HTTP behind. Everything HTTP gave you must be re-implemented:

  • No status codes, so you need your own error model.
  • No caching, no conditional requests, no content negotiation.
  • No per-message authorization from a gateway — your application must authorize every message.
  • No standard idempotency or retry semantics.
  • Load balancers and gateways need explicit WebSocket support, and sticky routing.
  • The connection is stateful, which is at odds with §1.5 — reconnection after a deploy must restore state.
  • SameSite cookies do not protect the handshake, so you must validate the Origin header yourself or you have built cross-site WebSocket hijacking.

Use them when the interaction is genuinely bidirectional and low-latency: collaborative editing, multiplayer, live chat, a trading UI. Do not use them because "the client needs updates" — that is SSE's job, at a fraction of the cost.

If you do use them, three rules. Authenticate at the handshake and re-check on every message, because authorization can be revoked mid-connection. Define your own message envelope with a type, an ID and a version, since you no longer have headers. And build reconnection with backoff into the client, because a deploy disconnects every client at once and an un-jittered reconnect storm is §11.3's thundering herd with a different name.

13.5 Choosing

Chunked / NDJSON SSE WebSocket Polling
Direction server → client, once server → client, ongoing both ways client → server
Still HTTP? yes yes no, after upgrade yes
Auto-reconnect no yes, with resume build it yourself n/a
Works through proxies mostly mostly needs support always
Text or binary either text only either either
Complexity low low high lowest

The decision procedure, in order:

  1. Can polling do it? For updates slower than a few seconds, GET with ETag and 304 (§10.5) is cheap, cacheable, stateless and needs no new infrastructure. Most "we need real-time" requirements are satisfied by polling every two seconds, and it is worth checking before building anything.
  2. Is it one big or slow response? Chunked NDJSON or CSV.
  3. Is it server-to-client updates? SSE. It is HTTP, it reconnects and resumes on its own, and it is text-only — which matters only if you need binary frames.
  4. Is it genuinely bidirectional and latency-sensitive? WebSocket, and accept the operational cost.

One practical note that decides more cases than the table: SSE and streaming responses go through your existing HTTP stack — the same gateway, the same authentication, the same logging, the same load balancer. WebSockets need every one of those to be reconsidered. That difference is usually larger than the protocol differences.

Exercises

Exercise 13.1 — Pick the mechanism. For each, choose from polling, chunked NDJSON, SSE and WebSocket, and justify it in one sentence.

  1. A dashboard tile showing today's order count, acceptable staleness 30 seconds.
  2. Exporting 4 million rows to CSV.
  3. Progress on the 40-minute import from §12.4.
  4. A collaborative document editor with cursors.
  5. A log viewer tailing a running deployment.
  6. A chat feature between a customer and a support agent.
Solution
  1. Polling. GET every 30 seconds with ETag; unchanged responses cost a 304 and a few hundred bytes. A persistent connection per dashboard tile is enormously more expensive than the requirement justifies.
  2. Chunked NDJSON or CSV — or, better, 202 plus a generated file in object storage (§12.4). Four million rows is minutes of work, so the operation deserves a resource; stream only if the client genuinely wants to start processing immediately.
  3. Polling first, SSE as an upgrade. GET /imports/{id} with Retry-After: 5 needs nothing new and works everywhere. SSE is a nice improvement if you already have the plumbing; a WebSocket for a progress bar is not defensible.
  4. WebSocket. Genuinely bidirectional, latency-sensitive, and cursor updates flow constantly in both directions. This is the case the protocol is for.
  5. SSE. Server-to-client, ongoing, text, and — crucially — Last-Event-ID resumption means a dropped connection does not lose log lines. Almost the textbook case.
  6. WebSocket, or SSE plus POST. Both work: the message rate is low enough that sending with a normal POST and receiving over SSE is entirely adequate and much simpler operationally. Choose WebSocket if you also want typing indicators and read receipts, which push the rate up.

The pattern across all six: bidirectional and fast → WebSocket; one-way updates → SSE; one large response → chunked; everything else → polling. And check polling first, every time.

Exercise 13.2 — The stream that fails halfway. Your NDJSON export sends 200,000 rows, then the database connection drops. You have already sent 200 OK. Describe what the client sees, and design a protocol that lets it tell success from truncation.

Solution

What the client sees: a 200 OK, 200,000 valid lines, and then the connection closes. If the transfer was chunked, a well-behaved client detects the missing terminal chunk and raises a transport error — but many HTTP libraries do not surface that distinctly, and any client reading line by line will simply stop receiving lines. The most likely outcome is that the client concludes the export finished and has 200,000 rows out of 900,000, silently.

The protocol: make completion explicit.

{"type":"meta","exportId":"exp_9f4a","startedAt":"2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"}
{"type":"row","data":{"id":"ord_1","total":4250}}
{"type":"row","data":{"id":"ord_2","total":9900}}
{"type":"end","count":2,"checksum":"sha256:1f3a…","status":"complete"}

The rule for consumers, which must be in your documentation: a stream without a terminal end record is a failure, regardless of the status code. On error, send {"type":"end","status":"failed","error":{...},"count":200000} instead, so the client knows both that it failed and how far it got.

Three refinements.

A checksum and a count let the client verify it received everything, not merely that the sender thought it was done.

A resumable cursor. Include the last row's sort key in the end record — and accept it as a parameter — so a failed export can resume from row 200,000 instead of restarting. This is §7.2's keyset pagination applied to a stream, and it turns a 900,000-row failure from a 40-minute retry into a 30-second one.

Trailers, if you can use them. HTTP trailers (Trailer header plus trailing fields) let you send metadata after the body, which is the protocol-native way to say "and it completed successfully". Support in clients and intermediaries is patchy enough that the in-band end record is the reliable choice.

The general principle: once you have sent the status line you have spent your only out-of-band signal, so every streaming format needs an in-band completion marker. Design it before you ship the endpoint, because adding one later is a breaking change for every consumer that was guessing.

Exercise 13.3 — Secure the WebSocket. A team ships /ws with authentication at the handshake only. Name three vulnerabilities, and give the design that fixes all three.

Solution

1. No Origin validation — cross-site WebSocket hijacking. The handshake is an ordinary HTTP request, so the browser attaches cookies, and SameSite does not apply to WebSocket handshakes the way it does to fetch. Any page on the internet can open a WebSocket to your endpoint as the logged-in user and read everything it sends. Fix: validate Origin against an allowlist at the handshake and reject anything else — the equivalent of §2.7's rule, and there is no preflight to help you here.

2. Authorization is checked once and never re-checked. A session revoked, a token expired, a user removed from a project — none of it reaches an open connection, which may live for hours. Fix: carry the principal on the connection, re-validate it on a timer (and on any message that changes what may be accessed), and close the connection with a defined close code when it fails. Treat the token's exp as a hard deadline for the connection.

3. No per-message authorization. The handshake proves who; it says nothing about what. A client that can send {"type":"subscribe","channel":"tenant-42"} will send tenant-99 next. Fix: authorize every message against the object it names, exactly as §9.2 requires for HTTP — the absence of a gateway does not remove the requirement, it removes the safety net.

The design.

handshake:  validate Origin → authenticate → attach principal + expiry to the connection
every msg:  parse into a typed envelope {id, type, version, payload}
            → check the principal is still valid
            → authorize(principal, action, object)
            → handle, and reply with {id, ok|error}
timer:      re-validate the principal every 60s; close with 4401 when it fails
limits:     max message size, messages per second per connection, subscriptions
            per connection, and a total connection cap per principal

Two more things worth adding. Rate-limit inside the connection (§11.6) — a WebSocket bypasses whatever limiting your gateway does per request, so one connection can send a million messages. And log with the same correlation ID scheme as HTTP (§15.2), because a WebSocket that is invisible to your tracing is a debugging problem the day it misbehaves.

SECTION 14

Contracts, Versioning and Evolution

An API you cannot change is a liability, and an API you change carelessly is worse. This section is about the machinery that makes change safe: writing the contract down in a form machines can check, knowing precisely which changes break clients and which do not, choosing a versioning strategy you can live with, retiring an endpoint without an outage, and — the part everyone skips — knowing what you have actually shipped and who is using it.

14.1 Contract-First

Two ways to arrive at an API specification. Code-first: write handlers, generate the document from them. Contract-first (or design-first): write the document, agree it, then implement against it.

Contract-first wins on three counts, all of which are about time:

  • Design review happens before implementation. Changing a YAML file after a review comment costs minutes; changing a shipped endpoint costs a deprecation cycle (§14.6).
  • Clients start immediately. Given the document, a frontend team can generate a typed client and a mock server on day one, in parallel with the backend rather than after it.
  • The document is the source of truth, so it cannot drift into being an artefact of whatever the code happens to do — including the parts that were accidents.

The honest objection is that a hand-written document drifts from the implementation unless something enforces the correspondence. That objection is correct, and the answer is not to abandon contract-first but to test against the contract (§15.6): validate real responses against the schema in CI, and the two cannot diverge silently.

In practice most teams land on a hybrid, and it works well: design the contract first for a new API or a significant addition, generate the document from code thereafter, and diff the generated document against the reviewed one in CI. You get the design review where it matters and the accuracy guarantee everywhere.

14.2 OpenAPI and JSON Schema

OpenAPI is the standard description format for HTTP APIs. Version 3.1 — the one to use — aligned its schema language with JSON Schema Draft 2020-12, which means the schemas in your document are real JSON Schema and can be used by any validator, not only by OpenAPI tooling. That alignment is the main reason to be on 3.1 rather than 3.0.

The object model, in the order you will meet it:

Object Holds
OpenAPI the root: version, info, servers, paths, components, security
Info title, version, description, contact, licence
Paths / Path Item one entry per URL template, one per method inside it
Operation operationId, parameters, requestBody, responses, security, tags
Parameter one per path, query, header or cookie parameter
Request Body / Response keyed by media type, each with a Schema
Schema JSON Schema Draft 2020-12
Components reusable schemas, responses, parameters, security schemes
Security Scheme http/bearer, apiKey, oauth2, openIdConnect

What a good document contains beyond the obvious: an operationId on every operation (it becomes the method name in every generated client, so a bad one is inflicted on every user), every error response and not just the happy path, examples on request and response bodies, deprecated: true where it applies, and security on every operation — which is what makes the §8.8 audit possible.

Generated from code, the document looks like this:

import json
from fastapi import FastAPI
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field

app = FastAPI(title="Orders API", version="1.4.0")

class OrderOut(BaseModel):
    id: str
    status: str = Field(description="one of: pending, paid, shipped")

@app.get("/orders/{oid}", response_model=OrderOut, operation_id="getOrder",
         summary="Fetch one order")
def get_order(oid: str) -> OrderOut:
    return OrderOut(id=oid, status="pending")

spec = app.openapi()
print("openapi version:", spec["openapi"])
print("paths          :", list(spec["paths"]))
print("operationId    :", spec["paths"]["/orders/{oid}"]["get"]["operationId"])
print("schema         :", json.dumps(spec["components"]["schemas"]["OrderOut"]))

openapi version: 3.1.0
paths          : ['/orders/{oid}']
operationId    : getOrder
schema         : {"properties": {"id": {"type": "string", "title": "Id"}, "status": {"type": "string", "title": "Status", "description": "one of: pending, paid, shipped"}}, "type": "object", "required": ["id", "status"], "title": "OrderOut"}

Both fields are required because neither has a default — which is the correspondence worth noticing: the schema is derived from the types, so it cannot describe something the code does not do. Note also that status is typed as a bare string with the values only in a description. A real contract would use an enum, so that a generated client gets a typed union rather than a string and a comment.

Four things the document unlocks once it exists:

  • Generated clients in every language, so nobody hand-writes an HTTP call.
  • Mock servers, so a frontend can build against an API that does not exist yet.
  • Request and response validation in tests and at the gateway.
  • Documentation that is never out of date, because it is the same artefact.

14.3 Generated or Written?

If you generate the document from code, three rules keep it honest:

Commit the generated document and diff it in CI. A pull request that changes the API then shows the API change in the diff, and a reviewer can see it. Without this, an accidental breaking change is invisible in review.

Fail the build on a breaking diff. Tools like oasdiff classify changes; wire one in so that removing a field or narrowing a type requires an explicit override rather than a merge.

Do not let the generator invent your contract. A response model of dict produces a schema of "object, unspecified", which is a document that describes nothing. Every endpoint needs a real response model — which §6.2 wanted anyway.

If you write it by hand, the corresponding rule is to validate responses against it in tests, so the document is checked rather than aspirational.

14.4 What Is a Breaking Change

Backwards compatibility has a precise operational definition: a change is backwards compatible if every client that worked before still works after. Not "should still work" — actually does, including clients written by people who read your documentation optimistically.

Safe. Adding an optional request field · adding a response field · adding an endpoint · adding an optional query parameter · relaxing a validation rule · adding a new value to a request enum you accept
Breaking. Removing or renaming a field · changing a type · making an optional field required · adding a required request field · removing an endpoint · changing a status code · tightening validation · adding a value to a response enum · changing default ordering or page size · changing an error code

Four entries on that list surprise people, and each has caused a real outage:

Adding a value to a response enum is breaking. A client with switch (status) { case "pending": … case "shipped": … } and no default hits an unhandled case. Adding a value to a request enum — one you accept — is safe, because no existing client sends it. The asymmetry is the whole point, and it is why AIP-180 treats new enum values as a compatibility hazard.

Tightening validation is breaking. If you accepted a 500-character name and now reject anything over 100, clients that were sending 200 characters now get 422. Loosening is safe; tightening is not.

Changing default ordering or page size is breaking even though no field changed. A client paginating with limit defaulted to 100 that suddenly gets 20 will silently process a fifth of the data.

Changing an error code or a problem type is breaking, because clients branch on it (§5.2). The message is prose and can change freely; the code cannot.

Three practices that make compatibility manageable:

  • Clients must ignore unknown fields. Say so in your documentation, and generate clients that do. A client that rejects unknown fields makes every addition breaking, and there is nothing you can do about it afterwards.
  • Never reuse a name for a different meaning. Renaming total from pence to pounds while keeping the name is worse than a rename — it fails silently. Add totalPounds, deprecate total.
  • Additive migration: add the new field, populate both, migrate clients, then remove the old one in a later version. Three releases, no outage.

14.5 Versioning

Four places to put a version, and the choice is a trade-off rather than a matter of correctness.

Strategy Example For Against
URI path /v1/orders Obvious, greppable, trivially routable, easy to curl §1.2 objection: it mints a new identity for the same resource, and every URL in every client changes
Query parameter /orders?version=1 Easy to default Pollutes the cache key; easy to omit by accident
Custom header Api-Version: 2026-03-01 URIs stay stable Invisible in a browser, easy to forget, needs Vary
Media type Accept: application/vnd.acme.order+json;version=2 Purest — the version is a property of the representation (§2.5) Awkward to use by hand, and poorly supported by tooling

URI versioning is the pragmatic default. It is what most large public APIs use, for the unglamorous reason that it is impossible to get wrong: a developer can see the version, a proxy can route on it, and a support conversation can name it.

Two decisions matter more than which of the four you pick:

Version the API, not each endpoint. Per-endpoint versions produce a matrix nobody can reason about, and clients end up mixing v1 and v3 in one flow.

Use dates rather than integers if you version often. 2026-03-01 conveys age, sorts naturally, and does not imply a hierarchy. Stripe's date-based versions, pinned per account and defaulted at signup, are the most widely-copied design in the industry — and its important property is not the format but the pinning: an existing client never changes version until it asks to.

Two anti-patterns worth naming:

  • Versioning everything at once for one endpoint's change. If only /orders changed, forcing every client onto v2 for all endpoints is gratuitous churn. Date-based pinning avoids this; integer versions rarely do.
  • Never versioning, and "just being careful". It works until the first change that cannot be made additively, and then there is no mechanism at all.

Whatever you choose, have exactly one supported version plus a deprecation window. Three live versions is three test matrices and three sets of bugs, and it is the state most APIs drift into by not deciding.

14.6 Deprecation and Sunset

Removing something is a process, not a release note.

The two standard headers make it machine-visible:

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Deprecation: @1772409600
Sunset: Sat, 30 May 2026 23:59:59 GMT
Link: <https://developers.example.com/deprecations/orders-v1>; rel="deprecation"
Warning: 299 - "GET /v1/orders is deprecated; migrate to /v2/orders by 2026-05-30"

Deprecation (RFC 9745) states when the deprecation took or takes effect; Sunset (RFC 8594) states when the resource will stop working. A client can log or alert on either without a human reading a changelog.

The process that actually works:

1. Ship the replacement and let both run. Nothing is deprecated until there is somewhere to go.
2. Announce: headers on every response, deprecated: true in the OpenAPI document, a changelog entry, and email to the accounts actually using it — which requires §14.7.
3. Measure. Track usage per client. You cannot remove what you cannot see, and the tail is always longer than expected.
4. Contact the remaining users individually. The last 5% of traffic is usually three customers, and an email to each is faster than any announcement.
5. Brown-out before sunset. Return errors for a few minutes at announced times, so a client that ignored everything discovers it in a controlled window rather than at removal.
6. Remove, answering 410 Gone, not 404 — the difference tells a confused developer that the endpoint existed and was retired.

Windows should be proportionate: 6–12 months for a public API with paying integrators, 1–3 months for internal consumers you can talk to, and as long as it takes for a partner with a contractual commitment. Whatever you announce, honour it — a sunset date that slips twice teaches every client to ignore the next one.

14.7 Knowing What You Have Shipped

OWASP API9:2023 is Improper Inventory Management, and it is on the list because the endpoints that get exploited are disproportionately the ones nobody remembered existed: a v1 left running after v2 shipped, a staging host with production data, an internal admin API exposed through an ingress rule that outlived its purpose.

An API inventory answers five questions, and if you cannot answer all five you do not have one — a diagram in a wiki is not an API inventory, because nothing keeps it true:

  • What endpoints exist, in every environment, including the ones not in the current OpenAPI document.
  • Which are public, and which are meant to be internal but are reachable.
  • What version each is, and which are deprecated.
  • Who is using each one — per client, per endpoint, over time.
  • What data each touches, so a breach can be scoped in minutes rather than weeks.

How to keep it true rather than aspirational:

  • Generate it from code, not from a wiki. The route table is the truth; publish it on every deploy.
  • Log per-endpoint, per-client usage (§15.1). This is what makes deprecation possible at all, and it is the single highest-value item on the list.
  • Scan externally. Ask what is actually reachable from the internet, from outside your network, on a schedule. Ingress rules and cloud load balancers accumulate.
  • Fail the build on an undocumented route. The same test as §8.8's and §9.3's: enumerate the route table and assert every entry is in the specification with a declared security requirement.
  • Give non-production environments non-production data. The staging copy of a production database is one misconfigured ingress away from being the breach.

Exercises

Exercise 14.1 — Classify twelve changes. Breaking or not? Where breaking, give the compatible alternative.

  1. Add optional nickname to the request body.
  2. Add riskScore to the response.
  3. Rename response field total to totalAmount.
  4. Change quantity from string to integer in the response.
  5. Add "partially_refunded" to the status response enum.
  6. Accept "EUR" in addition to "GBP" for a request currency.
  7. Make an optional request field required.
  8. Change the default limit from 100 to 20.
  9. Change 404 to 403 for an unauthorised read.
  10. Add a required Idempotency-Key header to POST /payments.
  11. Reduce name maximum length from 500 to 100.
  12. Change an error's type URI.
Solution
# Verdict Note / alternative
1 Safe No existing client sends it
2 Safe, if clients ignore unknown fields Which your documentation must require (§14.4)
3 Breaking Add totalAmount, populate both, deprecate total, remove next version
4 Breaking Typed clients fail to deserialise. Add quantityInt, or bump the version
5 Breaking Clients without a default case break. Reserve an "unknown" value from day one and document that clients must tolerate new values
6 Safe Widening what you accept never breaks a caller
7 Breaking Keep it optional with a documented default; require it only in a new version
8 Breaking, and silently so The worst kind: a client paginating gets a fifth of the data with no error. Keep the old default for existing versions
9 Breaking Clients branch on the code. It is also a disclosure change — see §9.2 before doing it in either direction
10 Breaking Accept it optionally first, measure adoption, then require it in a new version (§10.3's exercise is the same shape)
11 Breaking Tightening validation rejects previously-valid requests. Enforce for new records only, or in a new version
12 Breaking The type URI is the stable identifier clients branch on. Add a new type; keep emitting the old one where the meaning is unchanged

The two general rules that generate every row: widening what you accept is safe; narrowing it is not, and adding to what you send is safe only if clients tolerate the unknown — and never for enum values.

Exercise 14.2 — Choose a versioning strategy. A B2B API with 200 integrators, a mobile app you control, and a public developer portal. Decide, and specify the policy completely.

Solution

Decision: date-based versions, pinned per account, sent in a header, with a URI-path major version as a coarse fallback.

Base path:      https://api.example.com/v1/...
Version header: Api-Version: 2026-03-01
Vary:           Api-Version

The policy.

  • A new account is pinned at signup to the then-current date version and never moves unless it asks. This is the property that matters: an integrator who deploys once and never touches the code again keeps working indefinitely.
  • The header is optional; omitting it uses the account's pinned version. So a curl from a developer's laptop works with no ceremony.
  • Sending an explicit Api-Version overrides the pin per request, which is how an integrator tests an upgrade before committing to it.
  • A new date version is published whenever a breaking change ships, with a changelog listing exactly what changed and a migration guide.
  • The /v1 path segment moves only for a wholesale redesign — a decade-scale event. It exists so that routing, gateways and log filters have something coarse to key on.
  • Support window: 24 months from a version's supersession, with Deprecation and Sunset headers from the moment it is superseded.
  • Vary: Api-Version on every cacheable response, or caches serve one version's bytes to another (§10.3).

Why not the alternatives. Pure URI versioning (/v2/orders) forces all 200 integrators to change every URL for a change that affects one endpoint, so in practice you never do it and the API ossifies. Media-type versioning is the most correct and the least usable — an integrator debugging with curl should not have to construct an Accept header. A bare header with no pinning means an integrator who forgets it gets silently upgraded, which is the outage you were trying to prevent.

One more requirement this policy creates: you must be able to run several versions at once. In practice that means a transformation layer — the handler implements the newest version, and a chain of small transformers converts requests forward and responses backward for older ones. Stripe's published account of this is worth reading; the key property is that the handler only ever knows one version, so complexity does not accumulate in the business logic.

Exercise 14.3 — Retire an endpoint. GET /v1/reports is used by 40 of your 200 integrators and must be removed. Write the twelve-month plan, and say what you would do if, one week before sunset, one large customer is still using it.

Solution

Month 0. Ship GET /v2/reports. Publish a migration guide with a request/response diff and a worked example. Do not deprecate anything yet — there must be somewhere to go first.

Month 1. Announce. Deprecation and Sunset headers on every /v1/reports response, deprecated: true in the OpenAPI document, a portal banner, a changelog entry, and an email to the 40 accounts actually using it — which you can identify only because you have per-endpoint, per-client metrics (§14.7).

Months 2–8. Measure monthly and report the number publicly. Contact any account whose usage has not fallen. Offer help: for a large integrator, an hour of a solutions engineer's time is cheaper than a slipped sunset.

Month 9. Personal outreach to everyone remaining. At this point it is typically fewer than ten accounts, and the blocker is usually one team's roadmap rather than a technical problem — which a conversation can move and an email cannot.

Month 10. Brown-outs. Return 410 Gone for 30 minutes at announced times — one window, then a longer one two weeks later, at times chosen to be low-impact. This is the step that converts "I did not know" into a controlled discovery, and it is the single most effective item in the plan.

Month 11. Final notice, 30 days out, to every account still calling it, plus their account manager.

Month 12. Remove. 410 Gone with a problem document naming the replacement and linking the migration guide. Keep answering 410 indefinitely — it costs nothing and it answers a question a confused developer will otherwise open a ticket for.

One week before sunset, one large customer is still calling it. Do not slip the date silently, and do not remove it and hope. Three options, in order:

  1. Escalate commercially. Their account manager and yours, to their engineering lead, with the exact traffic numbers and the date. This resolves it most of the time, because the request has usually not reached anyone who can prioritise it.
  2. Offer a scoped, time-boxed extension for that account only — the endpoint keeps working for their API key for 60 more days, with a written commitment and a hard stop. The rest of the world sunsets on schedule, so the announced date is honoured for everyone else and your future sunsets remain credible.
  3. Sunset as planned if the endpoint is a security or compliance problem, or if the customer has been unresponsive after documented attempts. Have support briefed and a rollback ready.

What not to do: move the date for everyone. It teaches all 200 integrators that your dates are negotiable, which makes the next sunset twice as hard — and there will be a next one.

SECTION 15

Observability and Testing

An API you cannot observe is an API you cannot operate: every incident begins with someone asking "which request, which customer, which downstream call", and the answer has to already be recorded. An API you cannot test at the contract level is one whose clients discover your changes in production. This section covers the four things a service must emit to be diagnosable from the outside, and the testing that keeps the contract in §14 true.

15.1 Structured Logging

Structured logging means emitting logs as machine-parseable records — JSON objects with named fields — rather than as sentences. The difference is not aesthetic. A line like

2026-03-04 10:02:11 ERROR Failed to charge order 91 for customer 4183: timeout

can be read by a human and searched by substring, and that is all. You cannot ask "the p99 latency of charge attempts for customers on the enterprise plan, by region", because none of those are fields. The same event, structured:

import json
import logging
import sys

class JsonFormatter(logging.Formatter):
    """One JSON object per line; everything passed via `extra` becomes a field."""
    RESERVED = set(logging.LogRecord("", 0, "", 0, "", (), None).__dict__) | {
        "message", "asctime", "taskName"}

    def format(self, record: logging.LogRecord) -> str:
        payload = {
            "ts": "2026-03-04T10:02:11Z",              # time.time() in production
            "level": record.levelname,
            "msg": record.getMessage(),
            "logger": record.name,
        }
        payload |= {k: v for k, v in record.__dict__.items() if k not in self.RESERVED}
        if record.exc_info:
            payload["errorType"] = record.exc_info[0].__name__
        return json.dumps(payload, sort_keys=True)

logger = logging.getLogger("orders")
handler = logging.StreamHandler(sys.stdout)
handler.setFormatter(JsonFormatter())
logger.addHandler(handler)
logger.setLevel(logging.INFO)
logger.propagate = False

logger.error("charge failed", extra={
    "event": "payment.charge.failed",
    "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K", "traceId": "4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736",
    "orderId": "ord_91", "customerId": "cus_4183", "tenantId": "acme",
    "amountMinor": 4250, "currency": "GBP", "provider": "stripe",
    "durationMs": 8021, "outcome": "timeout", "attempt": 2,
})

{"amountMinor": 4250, "attempt": 2, "currency": "GBP", "customerId": "cus_4183", "durationMs": 8021, "event": "payment.charge.failed", "level": "ERROR", "logger": "orders", "msg": "charge failed", "orderId": "ord_91", "outcome": "timeout", "provider": "stripe", "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K", "tenantId": "acme", "traceId": "4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736", "ts": "2026-03-04T10:02:11Z"}

Every one of those keys is now a dimension you can filter, group and alert on.

The fields worth putting on every request log line, without exception:

Field Why
requestId ties the log to the response the customer is holding (§15.2)
traceId, spanId ties it to the distributed trace (§15.3)
method, route, status the route template (/orders/{id}), not the concrete path — otherwise every ID is a distinct value and nothing aggregates
durationMs the basis of every latency metric
principalId, tenantId who, and whose data
clientId, apiVersion what §14.7 needs to deprecate anything
event a stable dotted name, so a query does not depend on message wording

And the fields that must never appear: passwords, tokens, API keys, Authorization headers, card numbers, full request bodies containing personal data, and anything else from §5.6. Logs are replicated, retained for months and readable by far more people than your database — so treat a log pipeline as a place data escapes to. Redact at the point of logging, not in the aggregator, because the aggregator is already the wrong side of the boundary.

Two more habits worth adopting. Log at the boundaries — one line per inbound request and one per outbound call — rather than sprinkling logger.info through the logic; the boundaries are where the interesting facts are. And sample high-volume success logs while keeping every error: 1% of 200s and 100% of 5xxs gives you the shape of normal traffic and every anomaly, at a fraction of the cost.

15.2 Correlation IDs

A correlation ID — also called a request ID — is a unique value assigned to each inbound request and carried through everything that request touches: your own log lines, your calls to other services, the messages you enqueue, and the response you return.

The rules:

  • Accept one if the client sent it. An X-Request-Id from a caller lets them correlate their side with yours. Validate it — bounded length, safe characters — because it will end up in logs and in queries.
  • Generate one if not. A ULID or UUID. ULIDs sort by creation time, which is convenient in a log store.
  • Return it in the response, always, on success and on error, and include it in every problem document (§5.1). This is what lets a user quote an ID and a support engineer find the request in seconds.
  • Propagate it downstream on every outbound call and into every queue message, so a chain of five services shares one ID.
  • Put it in a context variable, not a parameter threaded through forty functions.
import contextvars
import uuid

request_id: contextvars.ContextVar[str] = contextvars.ContextVar("request_id",
                                                                 default="-")

def middleware(headers: dict[str, str]) -> dict[str, str]:
    """Accept the client's id when it is sane; otherwise mint one."""
    incoming = headers.get("x-request-id", "")
    valid = 0 < len(incoming) <= 64 and incoming.replace("-", "").isalnum()
    rid = incoming if valid else uuid.uuid4().hex[:16]
    request_id.set(rid)
    return {"X-Request-Id": rid}

def deep_in_the_call_stack() -> str:
    return f"charging… (request {request_id.get()})"     # no parameter threaded here

print(middleware({"x-request-id": "client-abc-123"}), deep_in_the_call_stack())
print(middleware({"x-request-id": "\n injected \n"})["X-Request-Id"] != "\n injected \n")
minted = middleware({})["X-Request-Id"]
print("minted length:", len(minted), "| alnum:", minted.isalnum())

{'X-Request-Id': 'client-abc-123'} charging… (request client-abc-123)
True
minted length: 16 | alnum: True

The second line is the reason for the validation: an unvalidated header goes straight into your log records, and a newline in it splits one log line into two — a log injection, which lets an attacker forge log entries and can break the aggregator's parser.

contextvars is the right mechanism in Python because it is asyncio-aware: each task gets its own value, so concurrent requests do not see each other's IDs. A thread-local would work for a threaded server and silently fail under async.

15.3 Trace Context and Distributed Tracing

A correlation ID tells you which request. Distributed tracing tells you where the time went: a request becomes a tree of spans — one per service, per database query, per outbound call — each with a start, a duration and attributes, so you can see that 8 of 9 seconds were spent in one downstream call.

The standard for propagation is W3C Trace Context, and it defines two headers:

00
version
4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736
trace-id — 16 bytes, one per request tree
00f067aa0ba902b7
parent-id — the caller's span
01
flags — low bit is sampled

The header is those four fields joined with hyphens, and a second header carries vendor state:

traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01
tracestate: acme=t61rcWkgMzE,congo=t61rcWkgMzE

traceparent carries the identity of the trace and the caller's span; tracestate carries vendor-specific data. The flags byte's low bit is sampled.

import re
import secrets

TRACEPARENT = re.compile(
    r"^(?P<version>[0-9a-f]{2})-(?P<trace_id>[0-9a-f]{32})"
    r"-(?P<parent_id>[0-9a-f]{16})-(?P<flags>[0-9a-f]{2})$")

def parse(header: str) -> dict | None:
    """W3C Trace Context §3.2. An unparseable header means 'start a new trace',
    never 'fail the request' — tracing must not be able to break traffic."""
    match = TRACEPARENT.match(header or "")
    if not match:
        return None
    fields = match.groupdict()
    if fields["version"] == "ff":                        # reserved, invalid
        return None
    if fields["trace_id"] == "0" * 32 or fields["parent_id"] == "0" * 16:
        return None                                      # all-zero ids are invalid
    fields["sampled"] = bool(int(fields["flags"], 16) & 0x01)
    return fields

def child_of(header: str) -> str:
    """The header to send downstream: same trace, this service's span as parent."""
    parent = parse(header)
    trace_id = parent["trace_id"] if parent else secrets.token_hex(16)
    flags = parent["flags"] if parent else "01"
    return f"00-{trace_id}-{secrets.token_hex(8)}-{flags}"

incoming = "00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01"
parsed = parse(incoming)
print("trace id :", parsed["trace_id"])
print("sampled  :", parsed["sampled"])
print("rejects all-zero trace:", parse("00-" + "0" * 32 + "-00f067aa0ba902b7-01"))
print("rejects garbage       :", parse("not-a-traceparent"))
outbound = child_of(incoming)
print("downstream shares trace:", outbound.split("-")[1] == parsed["trace_id"])
print("downstream has new span:", outbound.split("-")[2] != parsed["parent_id"])

trace id : 4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736
sampled  : True
rejects all-zero trace: None
rejects garbage       : None
downstream shares trace: True
downstream has new span: True

The comment in parse is the operational rule that matters most: a malformed traceparent must start a new trace, not fail the request. Telemetry is never allowed to be a availability dependency, and a strict parser that raises is one line away from a tracing library taking down your API.

Four practical notes:

  • Use OpenTelemetry rather than writing this. The code above exists to make the format legible; the SDK handles propagation, batching, sampling and export, and its auto-instrumentation covers your HTTP client and database driver for free.
  • Put the traceId in your logs and your problem documents. One identifier that reaches logs, traces and the customer is worth more than three that do not meet.
  • Sample deliberately. Head-based sampling at 1–10% is normal; tail-based sampling — decide after the trace completes, and keep everything slow or failed — is much more useful and needs a collector that buffers.
  • Propagate through queues. Put traceparent in the message headers, or the trace stops at the enqueue and the asynchronous half of every operation is invisible.

15.4 Health and Readiness

Two endpoints, answering two different questions, and conflating them causes outages.

A health check (liveness) answers should this process be restarted? A liveness health check must not depend on anything external. If it checks the database and the database has a hiccup, your orchestrator restarts every instance simultaneously — turning a brief dependency blip into a full outage, at the moment when you least want a cold start.

Readiness answers should this instance receive traffic right now? This one does check dependencies, because an instance that cannot reach its database should be taken out of the load balancer rotation rather than serving errors.

GET /healthz    →  200 {"status":"ok"}                    process is alive; no I/O
GET /readyz     →  200 {"status":"ready","checks":{"db":"ok","cache":"ok"}}
                   503 {"status":"not_ready","checks":{"db":"unreachable"}}
GET /startupz   →  200 once warm-up (migrations, cache priming) is done

Four rules:

  • Liveness checks nothing external. A process that is running and can answer HTTP is alive.
  • Readiness checks only hard dependencies. If the service can still serve 90% of its traffic without the cache, the cache being down must not make it unready — mark it degraded and keep serving.
  • Cache the readiness result for a second or two. A probe every second per instance times three dependencies is a surprising amount of load on those dependencies.
  • Fail readiness before shutdown. On SIGTERM, start returning 503 from /readyz, wait for the load balancer to notice (typically a few seconds), then stop accepting connections and drain the in-flight ones. Skipping the wait is why deploys drop requests — the instance stops accepting before anything has been told to stop sending.

Do not expose detailed dependency status publicly: it is reconnaissance. Keep /readyz internal, or return a bare status externally and the detail only to authenticated callers.

15.5 Testing an API at the Right Level

The testing course covers technique; this is about what is specific to an HTTP API. Four levels, and the mistake is almost always doing too much at the wrong one.

Level What it covers What it should not do
Unit domain rules, pure functions touch HTTP or a database
Handler / service one endpoint in-process, real routing and validation, fake infrastructure start a network listener
Integration the service plus real database and queue, in containers call third-party APIs
End-to-end the deployed system be numerous, or gate every merge

The handler level is the sweet spot for an API, and it is underused. An in-process client (httpx.ASGITransport, as used throughout this course) exercises routing, parsing, validation, serialisation, status codes and headers — most of what §2 through §6 is about — in milliseconds and with no network.

What is worth testing that people skip:

  • Every error path (§5.4's exercise). Status, media type, exact member set, and the headers the problem type promises.
  • Authorization, with two accounts. The §9.1 cross-account test finds real bugs on first run in most codebases.
  • Headers, not just bodies. Location on 201, ETag on GET, Allow on 405, Retry-After on 429, Cache-Control on everything.
  • Pagination to exhaustion. Walk a collection with a page size of 1 and assert you saw every item exactly once (§7.2).
  • Idempotency. Send the same request twice with the same key and assert one effect and two identical responses (§11.2).
  • Concurrency. Two conflicting writes with the same If-Match, asserting exactly one 200 and one 412 (§10.6).

15.6 Contract Testing

A contract test verifies that a provider and a consumer agree, without running both together. It solves the specific failure where every service's own tests pass and the integration is still broken — because each side tested against its own idea of the other.

Two flavours:

Schema-based. Validate real responses against the OpenAPI document (§14.2) in the provider's test suite. Cheap, catches drift between code and contract, and catches nothing about whether the contract is what consumers need.

Consumer-driven. Each consumer publishes the interactions it depends on — "given an order exists, when I GET /orders/91, I expect 200 with id and status". The provider runs all published contracts in CI and fails if any is violated. This is what Pact implements, and its value is that it tells the provider which consumer breaks and what they rely on, so an unused field can be removed with confidence.

The schema-based half is a handful of lines and belongs in every API's test suite:

def test_response_matches_the_published_schema(client, openapi_document):
    response = client.get("/orders/ord_91")
    assert response.status_code == 200
    schema = (openapi_document["paths"]["/orders/{oid}"]["get"]["responses"]
              ["200"]["content"]["application/json"]["schema"])
    validate(instance=response.json(), schema=resolve_refs(schema, openapi_document))

Three rules that make contract testing pay for itself:

  • Test the contract, not the data. Assert that status is one of the enum values, not that it is "pending" — otherwise the test breaks on every fixture change and teaches people to ignore it.
  • Run consumer contracts in the provider's CI, not the consumer's. The provider is who breaks them, and a failure has to block the provider's merge to be useful.
  • Version the contracts alongside the API version (§14.5), so a provider supporting two versions runs both sets and knows which consumers are on which.

Contract tests do not replace integration tests: they prove the shape of the conversation, not that the system works. What they replace is the sprawling, slow, flaky end-to-end suite that exists only to catch interface drift — and that is a very good trade.

Exercises

Exercise 15.1 — Design the request log. Specify the JSON record emitted once per request: every field, its source, and the redaction rules. Then name two queries it makes possible that an unstructured log cannot answer.

Solution 
{
  "ts": "2026-03-04T10:02:11.482Z",
  "level": "INFO",
  "event": "http.request",
  "requestId": "01JQ8F3P2K",
  "traceId": "4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736",
  "spanId": "00f067aa0ba902b7",
  "method": "PATCH",
  "route": "/orders/{id}",
  "path": "/orders/ord_91",
  "status": 200,
  "durationMs": 47,
  "requestBytes": 218,
  "responseBytes": 1104,
  "principalId": "usr_4183",
  "tenantId": "acme",
  "clientId": "cli_mobile_ios",
  "apiVersion": "2026-03-01",
  "userAgent": "AcmeApp/4.2 (iOS 19.1)",
  "ip": "203.0.113.7",
  "cacheStatus": "miss",
  "rateLimitRemaining": 447
}

Sources. requestId from the validated X-Request-Id or minted (§15.2); traceId/spanId from the trace context (§15.3); route from the matched template, path from the concrete URL; principalId, tenantId, clientId from the credential, never from the request (§9.5); apiVersion from the pinned or explicit version (§14.5).

Redaction rules. No Authorization header, no cookies, no body — ever, by default. Query strings are logged with an allowlist of parameter names, because tokens and emails end up there. ip is a personal identifier under GDPR: log it, retain it for a short window (30 days), and truncate or hash it in longer-retained aggregates. Any field explicitly marked sensitive in a schema is dropped by the formatter, so redaction is structural rather than a habit.

Two queries an unstructured log cannot answer.

"p99 latency of PATCH /orders/{id} for the iOS client, by tenant, over the last hour." This needs route as a template (or ord_91 becomes its own group), durationMs as a number, and clientId and tenantId as dimensions. Grep gives you none of the four.

"Which clients and API versions still call /v1/reports, and how much?" This is exactly the §14.7 inventory question that makes deprecation possible, and it needs route, clientId and apiVersion on every line. Without it, a sunset date is a guess.

A third worth having: "show me every log line for the request whose ID the customer quoted" — which needs requestId on every line, including the ones emitted deep in the call stack.

Exercise 15.2 — Fix the health check. A deploy causes a five-minute outage. The liveness probe is GET /healthz, which runs SELECT 1 against the database and returns 503 on failure. The database had a 20-second failover. Explain, and give the correct design.

Solution

What happened. During the 20-second failover every instance's /healthz returned 503. The orchestrator treats a failed liveness probe as "this process is broken" and restarts it — so it killed every instance at once. The database came back after 20 seconds; the fleet then took five minutes because every instance was cold-starting simultaneously: reconnecting pools, warming caches, JIT-ing, and hammering the freshly-failed-over database while doing it. The outage was caused entirely by the response to the fault, not by the fault (§11.4's death spiral, in a different costume).

The correct design.

GET /healthz   — liveness. Returns 200 if the process can serve HTTP. No I/O at all.
GET /readyz    — readiness. Checks hard dependencies. 503 removes this instance
                 from the load balancer WITHOUT restarting it.
GET /startupz  — startup. 200 once migrations and cache priming are done; the
                 liveness probe does not begin until this passes.

The distinction is the whole fix: a database outage should make instances not ready (out of rotation, still running, ready to serve the instant the database returns) rather than not alive (killed and restarted). Nothing about a database failover is repaired by restarting an application process.

Probe settings that matter as much as the endpoints. Liveness failureThreshold high (5+) and periodSeconds generous, so a transient hiccup never restarts anything. Readiness the opposite — quick to react, quick to recover. A startup probe with a long threshold, so slow-starting instances are not killed mid-warm-up.

Two more changes worth making at the same time. Cache the readiness result for 2 seconds, or the probes themselves become load on the failing dependency. And on SIGTERM, fail readiness first, wait for the load balancer to deregister, then drain — otherwise every deploy drops the requests already in flight.

Exercise 15.3 — Trace a slow request. A customer reports that checkout takes 9 seconds. You have the request ID from the error page. Describe exactly what you look at and in what order, and say which of §15.1–§15.3 each step depends on.

Solution

1. Find the request. Query the log store for requestId: "01JQ8F3P2K". This gives the route, status, durationMs, tenant, principal, client and API version — enough to know whether it is one customer or a class of request. Depends on §15.2: the ID being in the response and on every log line.

2. Pull the trace. Take traceId from that log line and open the trace. The waterfall shows every span: your handler, each database query, each outbound call, each queue publish, with durations. In a 9-second checkout the answer is usually immediate — one span is 8 seconds long. Depends on §15.3, and on the traceId being in the logs, which is what joins the two systems.

3. Read the slow span's attributes. If it is an HTTP client span: which host, which status, was it retried (§11.3), did it time out (§11.4). If it is a database span: the statement, the row count, whether it waited on the pool. Depends on instrumentation attaching those attributes — the OpenTelemetry auto-instrumentation gives you most of them.

4. Ask whether it is systemic. Query for the same route over the last hour, grouped by tenantId and clientId, at p50/p95/p99. One tenant slow means data skew — a customer with 400,000 orders hitting a query that assumes a few hundred. Everyone slow means a dependency or a deploy. Depends on §15.1: route as a template and durationMs as a number.

5. Correlate with deploys and dependency health. Overlay the latency series with deploy markers and the downstream's own metrics. This is where "it started at 14:02" becomes "it started with the 14:01 release".

6. Check retries. A 9-second request is often three 3-second attempts. The trace shows sibling spans to the same host; the logs show attempt: 2. This distinction matters because the fix is different — a slow dependency versus an over-eager retry policy (§11.3).

The dependency chain is the point. Step 1 needs the ID on the response; step 2 needs the trace ID in the log; step 3 needs instrumented clients; step 4 needs route templates. Miss any one of them and the investigation stops there — which is why §15.1's field list is not a style preference.

Exercise 15.4 — Introduce contract testing. Six teams consume your API. Twice this quarter a change broke a consumer despite green CI. Design the scheme, and say what it does not solve.

Solution

Layer 1 — schema validation in your own CI (week 1). Every response in your handler-level test suite is validated against the committed OpenAPI document. This catches code/contract drift immediately and costs almost nothing. Combine it with an oasdiff check that fails the build on a breaking change to the document (§14.3) unless a reviewer applies an explicit override label.

Layer 2 — consumer-driven contracts (weeks 2–6). Each of the six teams publishes a Pact file describing the interactions they actually depend on: given a state, when this request, expect this response shape. They run against a mock generated from their own contract, so their tests get faster too — which is the incentive that makes them participate.

Layer 3 — provider verification in your CI. Your pipeline fetches every published contract from the broker and replays it against your service with the required states seeded. A failure blocks your merge and names the consumer and the interaction. This is the layer that would have caught both incidents.

Layer 4 — deployment gating. The broker records which contract versions are verified against which provider versions ("can-i-deploy"). You cannot deploy a provider version that has not been verified against every consumer version currently in production.

What this does not solve.

  • Semantic changes. A field that keeps its name and type but changes meaning — total moving from pence to pounds — passes every contract test and breaks every consumer. Only naming discipline prevents it (§14.4).
  • Behaviour under load. Contracts say nothing about latency, rate limits or timeouts.
  • Interactions nobody declared. A consumer relying on a field it never wrote a contract for is unprotected — which is a feature, since it lets you remove genuinely unused fields, and a hazard when a consumer's contracts are incomplete.
  • The state-setup burden. Provider states ("given order 91 exists and is pending") have to be implemented and maintained on your side. This is the real ongoing cost, and it is the reason schemes like this get abandoned; budget for it explicitly.
  • Non-HTTP consumers. Anyone reading your events or your database directly is outside the scheme entirely.

Sequencing note: ship Layer 1 first and alone. It is a day's work, it catches a real class of bug, and it gives you the credibility to ask six teams for the several weeks that Layers 2–4 require.

SECTION 16

Capstone — Building `ledger`

This section builds one small API that uses almost every decision in this course, and then runs it. ledger is a multi-tenant expenses service: create an expense, read it, patch it, delete it, list it. That is five endpoints — deliberately few, because the interest is not in how many things it does but in how carefully it does them. Every line below was executed; the transcript in §16.8 is real output, not an illustration.

16.1 The Requirements, and the Decisions They Force

Tenants record expenses. Each expense has a description, an amount and a currency, moves through draft → submitted → approved, and belongs to exactly one tenant. Clients are mobile apps on unreliable networks. One tenant must never see another's data. The service publishes an event whenever an expense is created.

Read literally, that paragraph makes eight decisions for us:

Requirement Decision Section
"expenses" — a thing with a lifecycle /expenses collection, /expenses/{id} member §3.3
a status that moves through states PATCH with a Literal type; illegal transitions are 409 §2.3, §5.5
mobile clients on unreliable networks Idempotency-Key on create, retry-safe by construction §11.2
concurrent edits from several devices ETag + mandatory If-Match, 412/428 §10.6
"never see another tenant's data" tenant from the token, in the primary key and every query; 404 not 403 §9.2, §9.5
"publishes an event" transactional outbox, so the row and the event commit together §12.3
an API, therefore errors one taxonomy, application/problem+json §5.2
a list that will grow keyset pagination with a capped limit §7.2

Nothing here is speculative. Each is the smallest mechanism that satisfies a stated requirement.

16.2 Storage and the Domain Errors

Three tables. Note the composite primary key on expenses: (tenant_id, id) means a query missing the tenant cannot find a row at all — the §9.5 mechanism that turns a forgotten predicate into a 404 rather than a leak.

import base64
import hashlib
import json
import math
import sqlite3
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Annotated, Any, Literal

from fastapi import Depends, FastAPI, Header, HTTPException, Query, Request, Response
from fastapi.responses import JSONResponse
from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field

PROBLEM = "application/problem+json"
BASE = "https://api.ledger.example/problems/"

SCHEMA = """
CREATE TABLE expenses (
  tenant_id   TEXT NOT NULL,
  id          TEXT NOT NULL,
  description TEXT NOT NULL,
  amount_minor INTEGER NOT NULL,          -- integer minor units, never a float (§4.3)
  currency    TEXT NOT NULL,
  status      TEXT NOT NULL,
  version     INTEGER NOT NULL,           -- the concurrency validator (§10.4)
  created_at  TEXT NOT NULL,
  deleted_at  TEXT,                       -- soft delete (§7.6)
  PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, id)             -- the tenant is part of identity (§9.5)
);
CREATE TABLE outbox (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
  topic TEXT NOT NULL, payload TEXT NOT NULL, published_at TEXT
);
CREATE TABLE idempotency (
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL, key TEXT NOT NULL, fingerprint TEXT NOT NULL,
  state TEXT NOT NULL, status INTEGER, body TEXT,
  PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, key)            -- keys are scoped per tenant (§11.2)
);
"""

def connect() -> sqlite3.Connection:
    db = sqlite3.connect(":memory:", check_same_thread=False)
    db.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    db.executescript(SCHEMA)
    return db

The error taxonomy is §5.3's design: domain errors that know nothing about HTTP, and one table mapping them to status codes and problem types.

class DomainError(Exception): ...

@dataclass
class NotFound(DomainError):
    kind: str
    id: str

@dataclass
class Conflict(DomainError):
    reason: str

@dataclass
class PreconditionRequired(DomainError):
    header: str

@dataclass
class PreconditionFailed(DomainError):
    reason: str

@dataclass
class RateLimited(DomainError):
    retry_after: int

@dataclass
class IdempotencyConflict(DomainError):
    state: str

MAPPING: dict[type[DomainError], tuple[int, str, str]] = {
    NotFound:             (404, "not-found", "Resource not found"),
    Conflict:             (409, "conflict", "Conflicting state"),
    PreconditionRequired: (428, "precondition-required", "Precondition required"),
    PreconditionFailed:   (412, "precondition-failed", "Precondition failed"),
    RateLimited:          (429, "rate-limited", "Rate limit exceeded"),
    IdempotencyConflict:  (409, "idempotency-conflict", "Request already in progress"),
}

16.3 Three Models, Three Boundaries

ExpenseIn is what a client may set. ExpensePatch is what a client may change. ExpenseOut is what a client may see. The database row is a fourth thing entirely, and none of them is the others (§6.2).

class ExpenseIn(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")           # a typo is a 422, not a no-op
    description: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=140)
    amountMinor: int = Field(gt=0, le=100_000_00)       # a bound, not just a type (§6.4)
    currency: str = Field(pattern=r"^[A-Z]{3}$")

class ExpensePatch(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")
    description: str | None = Field(default=None, min_length=1, max_length=140)
    status: Literal["draft", "submitted", "approved"] | None = None

class ExpenseOut(BaseModel):                            # the allowlist on the way out
    id: str
    description: str
    amountMinor: int
    currency: str
    status: str
    createdAt: str

class Page(BaseModel):
    items: list[ExpenseOut]                             # never a bare array (§3.3)
    nextPageToken: str | None = None

def b64(raw: bytes) -> str:
    return base64.urlsafe_b64encode(raw).decode().rstrip("=")

def unb64(token: str) -> bytes:
    return base64.urlsafe_b64decode(token + "=" * (-len(token) % 4))

def etag_of(row: sqlite3.Row) -> str:
    return f'W/"{row["version"]}-{row["id"]}"'          # weak: derived from the version

def to_out(row: sqlite3.Row) -> ExpenseOut:
    return ExpenseOut(id=row["id"], description=row["description"],
                      amountMinor=row["amount_minor"], currency=row["currency"],
                      status=row["status"], createdAt=row["created_at"])

class TokenBucket:
    def __init__(self, capacity: int, rate: float) -> None:
        self.capacity, self.rate = capacity, rate
        self.tokens, self.updated = float(capacity), 0.0
    def take(self, now: float, cost: int = 1) -> int:
        """Returns 0 when allowed, or the seconds to wait (§11.6)."""
        self.tokens = min(self.capacity, self.tokens + (now - self.updated) * self.rate)
        self.updated = now
        if self.tokens >= cost:
            self.tokens -= cost
            return 0
        return math.ceil((cost - self.tokens) / self.rate)

Two details worth pausing on. amountMinor is an integer of minor units with an upper bound, so neither floating-point rounding nor a £4 billion expense is representable (§4.3, §6.4). And etag_of derives the validator from the row's version column, which is exactly the "version column for If-Match" recommendation in §10.4.

16.4 Authentication, Scopes and Rate Limiting

Three things happen before any handler runs, and the order matters: authenticate, rate-limit, then authorize.

The FastAPI(dependencies=[Depends(principal)]) line is §8.8's inversion — authentication is applied to every route by default, so a route added in a hurry inherits it and a forgotten decorator cannot open an endpoint.

@dataclass
class Principal:
    id: str
    tenant: str
    scopes: frozenset[str]

TOKENS = {                                   # a real system verifies a JWT here (§8.6)
    "tok_ada":   Principal("usr_ada", "acme",
                           frozenset({"expenses:read", "expenses:write"})),
    "tok_grace": Principal("usr_grace", "globex",
                           frozenset({"expenses:read", "expenses:write"})),
    "tok_ro":    Principal("usr_ro", "acme", frozenset({"expenses:read"})),
}

def build_app(db: sqlite3.Connection, clock: list[float]) -> FastAPI:
    """`clock` is injected so the rate limiter is testable without sleeping."""
    buckets: dict[str, TokenBucket] = {}

    def principal(authorization: Annotated[str | None, Header()] = None) -> Principal:
        token = (authorization or "").removeprefix("Bearer ")
        found = TOKENS.get(token)
        if found is None:
            raise HTTPException(401, "invalid token",             # 401, and it says how
                                headers={"WWW-Authenticate": 'Bearer realm="ledger"'})
        bucket = buckets.setdefault(found.id, TokenBucket(5, 1.0))
        wait = bucket.take(clock[0])
        if wait:
            raise RateLimited(retry_after=wait)
        return found

    def require(scope: str):
        def dependency(who: Annotated[Principal, Depends(principal)]) -> Principal:
            if scope not in who.scopes:
                raise HTTPException(403, f"missing scope {scope}")  # 403, not 401 (§2.3)
            return who
        return dependency

    app = FastAPI(title="Ledger API", version="1.0.0",
                  dependencies=[Depends(principal)])   # authentication is opt-OUT (§8.8)

    @app.exception_handler(DomainError)
    async def domain_handler(request: Request, exc: DomainError) -> JSONResponse:
        status, slug, title = MAPPING[type(exc)]
        body: dict[str, Any] = {"type": BASE + slug, "title": title, "status": status,
                                "instance": request.url.path,
                                "requestId": request.headers.get("x-request-id", "-")}
        headers: dict[str, str] = {}
        match exc:
            case NotFound(kind=kind, id=rid):
                body["detail"] = f"No {kind} with id {rid}."
            case Conflict(reason=reason) | PreconditionFailed(reason=reason):
                body["detail"] = reason
            case PreconditionRequired(header=header):
                body["detail"] = f"{header} is required on this endpoint."
            case RateLimited(retry_after=seconds):
                body["detail"] = f"Try again in {seconds} seconds."
                headers["Retry-After"] = str(seconds)              # the header, not prose
            case IdempotencyConflict(state=state):
                body["detail"] = f"A request with this key is {state}."
        return JSONResponse(body, status_code=status, media_type=PROBLEM,
                            headers=headers)

    def load(tenant: str, expense_id: str) -> sqlite3.Row:
        """The tenant predicate is part of the lookup, so it cannot be forgotten."""
        row = db.execute(
            "SELECT * FROM expenses WHERE tenant_id=? AND id=? AND deleted_at IS NULL",
            (tenant, expense_id)).fetchone()
        if row is None:
            raise NotFound("expense", expense_id)      # 404, never 403 (§9.2)
        return row

    @app.post("/expenses", status_code=201, response_model=ExpenseOut)
    def create(body: ExpenseIn, response: Response,
               who: Annotated[Principal, Depends(require("expenses:write"))],
               idempotency_key: Annotated[str | None, Header()] = None) -> ExpenseOut:
        fingerprint = hashlib.sha256(body.model_dump_json().encode()).hexdigest()[:16]
        if idempotency_key:
            prior = db.execute("SELECT * FROM idempotency WHERE tenant_id=? AND key=?",
                               (who.tenant, idempotency_key)).fetchone()
            if prior is not None:
                if prior["fingerprint"] != fingerprint:            # same key, other body
                    raise HTTPException(422, "Idempotency-Key reused with another body")
                if prior["state"] == "in_progress":
                    raise IdempotencyConflict("in_progress")
                response.status_code = prior["status"]             # replay the ORIGINAL
                return ExpenseOut.model_validate_json(prior["body"])
            db.execute("INSERT INTO idempotency VALUES (?,?,?,?,?,?)",
                       (who.tenant, idempotency_key, fingerprint,
                        "in_progress", None, None))
        expense_id = "exp_" + hashlib.sha256(
            f"{who.tenant}{fingerprint}"
            f"{len(db.execute('SELECT 1 FROM expenses').fetchall())}"
            .encode()).hexdigest()[:8]
        created = "2026-03-04T10:02:00Z"     # a real clock in production
        with db:                             # ONE transaction: row + event + key (§12.3)
            db.execute("INSERT INTO expenses VALUES (?,?,?,?,?,?,?,?,?)",
                       (who.tenant, expense_id, body.description, body.amountMinor,
                        body.currency, "draft", 1, created, None))
